First identified in global tomographic models by Dziewonski and Anderson (1981) and later sharpened by higher‑resolution inversions (e.g., S40RTS, SEMUCB‑WM1), the African LLSVP extends from the core‑mantle boundary (CMB) up to ~1500 km depth and spans roughly 60° in longitude, while the Pacific LLSVP is slightly smaller but more vertically coherent. Mineral‑physics calculations indicate that a purely thermal anomaly of 500–800 K would produce the observed velocity deficit, yet the sharp lateral gradients and the coincidence of LLSVP edges with kimberlite eruption sites suggest a compositional component — likely enrichment in iron‑rich, dense material such as bridgmanite‑poor, ferropericlase‑rich assemblages or recycled basaltic crust. Geodynamic models that incorporate a chemically distinct, high‑density layer at the base of the mantle reproduce the long‑wavelength geoid, the observed pattern of plume initiation, and the stability of the LLSVPs over >200 Myr. Seismic anisotropy within the LLSVPs, measured from shear‑wave splitting of SKS phases, shows a predominantly vertical alignment of fast axes, consistent with deformation by upwelling mantle flow. The LLSVPs also modulate CMB heat flux: numerical dynamo simulations demonstrate that lateral variations in heat flux of ±10 mW m⁻² imposed by the LLSVPs can generate the observed westward drift of reversed flux patches and influence reversal frequency. Ongoing work combines normal‑mode splitting functions, ScS reverberations, and mineral‑physics constraints to resolve whether the LLSVPs are primordial reservoirs or the cumulative product of subducted slab accumulation.

Earth’s Free Oscillations and Normal‑Mode Seismology

Free oscillations of the Earth are standing waves excited by large earthquakes (moment magnitude ≥ 8.0) that cause the whole planet to ring like a bell. The 1960 Chilean earthquake (Mw 9.5) provided the first clear recordings of spheroidal modes (0Sₙ) and toroidal modes (0Tₙ) on long‑period seismographs. Each mode is characterized by three quantum numbers: angular order ℓ, radial order n, and azimuthal order m. The eigenfrequencies of these modes depend on the radially symmetric structure (density, P‑ and S‑wave velocities) and are insensitive to lateral heterogeneity to first order, making them powerful constraints on the 1‑D reference Earth model. The classic “mode‑summation” technique uses observed amplitude spectra of modes such as 0S₂ (period ≈ 54 min), 0S₃ (≈ 38 min), and 0T₂ (≈ 44 min) to infer the average shear velocity in the lower mantle and the density jump at the inner‑core boundary. Splitting of degenerate multiplets (different m for the same ℓ,n) reveals lateral variations: the 0S₂ multiplet splitting pattern measured after the 2004 Sumatra‑Andaman earthquake (Mw 9.1) required a ~0.5 % shear‑velocity anomaly in the lowermost mantle beneath the Indian Ocean, consistent with the African LLSVP edge. Normal‑mode data also constrain the inner‑core shear modulus and the differential rotation rate; recent analyses of the 0S₁ and 0T₁ splitting functions suggest an inner‑core super‑rotation of 0.1–0.3° yr⁻¹ relative to the mantle. The advent of superconducting gravimeters and broadband seismometers (e.g., the Global Seismographic Network) has reduced noise floors to < 10⁻¹¹ m s⁻², enabling detection of ultra‑low‑frequency modes (periods > 1 h) that sample the whole Earth volume. Forward modelling of normal modes now incorporates anelastic attenuation (Q) and rotation (Coriolis coupling), and joint inversions with body‑wave travel times and surface‑wave dispersion produce the most robust 3‑D mantle models to date.

Worked Example: Estimating Lower‑Mantle Shear Velocity from 0S₂ Frequency
The observed frequency of the fundamental spheroidal mode 0S₂ is 308.5 µHz. Using the first‑order perturbation formula δω/ω = ∫ K(r) δln β(r) dr, where K(r) is the sensitivity kernel peaked in the lower mantle, and assuming a reference model with β = 5.0 km s⁻¹ at 2000 km depth, calculate the fractional shear‑velocity perturbation δβ/β required to match the observed frequency if the reference frequency is 310.0 µHz.
The fractional frequency shift is (308.5 – 310.0)/310.0 = –0.00484. For a mode whose kernel integrates to unity over the lower mantle, δω/ω ≈ δln β = δβ/β. Thus δβ/β ≈ –0.00484, i.e. a –0.48 % shear‑velocity reduction relative to the reference model. At 5.0 km s⁻¹ this corresponds to Δβ ≈ –0.024 km s⁻¹, giving β ≈ 4.976 km s⁻¹ in the lower mantle.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a quantitative estimate from normal‑mode data, identify the mode, its reference frequency, and the sign of the frequency shift. Remember that a lower observed frequency implies a slower shear velocity in the region sampled by the kernel. Use the simple relation δω/ω ≈ δln β for first‑order estimates; higher‑order corrections (ellipticity, rotation) are rarely required in competitive‑exam problems.

Seismic Wave Propagation in Poroelastic Media: Fluid Effects on Wave Speeds and Attenuation

Poroelastic theory, formulated by Maurice Biot in the 1950s, describes wave propagation in fluid‑saturated porous solids. Two compressional waves exist: the fast P₁ wave (analogous to the ordinary P‑wave) and the slow P₂ wave (diffusive at low frequencies, propagating only at high frequencies). The P₁ velocity Vₚ₁ is given by the Gassmann‑Biot equation Vₚ₁² = (Kₘ + 4/3 μ + α²M) / ρ, where Kₘ is the drained frame bulk modulus, μ the shear modulus, α the Biot‑Willis coefficient, M the Biot modulus, and ρ the bulk density. The slow wave velocity Vₚ₂ is controlled by fluid viscosity η, permeability κ, and the same elastic parameters; at seismic frequencies (1–100 Hz) Vₚ₂ is typically < 100 m s⁻¹ and highly attenuated. Shear‑wave velocity Vₛ is unaffected by fluid saturation because fluids cannot support shear stress, but shear‑wave attenuation Qₛ⁻¹ increases due to squirt‑flow mechanisms at grain contacts. Laboratory measurements on Berea sandstone (porosity 0.20, permeability 500 mD) show Vₚ₁ decreasing from 4.2 km s⁻¹ (dry) to 3.8 km s⁻¹ (water‑saturated) while Vₛ remains ~2.3 km s⁻¹; Qₚ drops from 150 to 60, illustrating strong fluid‑induced attenuation. In the field, the presence of overpressured fluid zones in subduction forearcs produces low‑velocity, high‑attenuation anomalies imaged by seismic tomography and correlated with elevated Vₚ/Vₛ ratios (> 1.85). The frequency‑dependent complex modulus formalism (e.g., the Kjartansson constant‑Q model) is used to incorporate poroelastic attenuation into full‑waveform inversion. Recent ambient‑noise interferometry studies have retrieved the slow P₂ wave in the shallow crust, confirming Biot’s prediction of a diffusive‑to‑propagating transition around 10 kHz for typical sandstones. Understanding poroelastic effects is essential for interpreting time‑lapse (4D) seismic monitoring of CO₂ sequestration, hydrocarbon production, and geothermal reservoirs, where fluid substitution changes Vₚ₁ by 5–10 % and Qₚ by factors of 2–3.

Geophysical Inverse Theory: Regularization, Resolution, and Model Uncertainty

Geophysical inverse problems seek model parameters m (e.g., velocity, density, conductivity) that explain observed data d through a forward operator G: d = G(m) + ε. Because G is often non‑linear and the data are noisy and incomplete, the inverse problem is ill‑posed in the sense of Hadamard: solutions may not exist, be non‑unique, or be unstable. Regularization stabilizes the inversion by adding a penalty term Φ(m) to the data‑misfit functional, yielding the objective function Ψ(m) = ‖W_d(G(m) – d)‖² + λ²‖W_m(L m – m_ref)‖². Common choices for L include the identity (damping), first‑derivative (smoothness), or second‑derivative (curvature) operators. The regularization parameter λ controls the trade‑off between data fit and model simplicity; it is selected via the L‑curve criterion, generalized cross‑validation, or Bayesian evidence maximization. The model resolution matrix R = (GᵀW_dᵀW_dG + λ²LᵀL)⁻¹GᵀW_dᵀW_dG quantifies how well each model parameter is resolved; diagonal elements near 1 indicate good resolution, while off‑diagonal spread reveals smearing. The posterior model covariance C_m = (GᵀC_d⁻¹G + λ²LᵀL)⁻¹ provides formal uncertainties. In seismic tomography, checkerboard tests and spike tests are used to assess spatial resolution empirically. For magnetotelluric inversion, the non‑linearity of the forward problem (Maxwell’s equations) requires iterative Gauss‑Newton or Occam’s inversion schemes, where the regularization is adapted at each iteration to achieve a target misfit χ² ≈ N_data. Joint inversion of seismic travel times, surface‑wave dispersion, and gravity data exploits complementary sensitivities: seismic data constrain velocity structure, while gravity constrains density. The combined objective function includes separate weighting matrices for each data type, and the cross‑gradient constraint ∇m₁ × ∇m₂ = 0 enforces structural similarity between velocity and density models. Modern transdimensional Bayesian methods (e.g., reversible‑jump MCMC) treat the number of model parameters as unknown, yielding posterior ensembles that naturally quantify model complexity and uncertainty without explicit regularization parameters.

Comparative Planetary Geophysics: Earth, Venus, and Mars Core Dynamics

Comparative planetology leverages spacecraft measurements to test geodynamo theories under different boundary conditions. Earth’s liquid outer core (radius 3480 km) sustains a dipolar magnetic field of ~30 µT at the surface, powered by thermal and compositional convection driven by inner‑core solidification (latent heat ~0.7 TW, light‑element release ~1.5 TW). Venus, despite a similar size and presumed core radius (~3000 km), lacks an intrinsic magnetic field; Magellan gravity data and the absence of a detectable magnetosphere imply either a completely solid core, a stably stratified liquid core with no convection, or a very low core‑mantle heat flux (< 5 mW m⁻²) that suppresses dynamo action. The high surface temperature (735 K) and lack of plate tectonics reduce mantle cooling efficiency, supporting the stratified‑core hypothesis. Mars, with a core radius of 1830 km (InSight seismic constraints), possessed an early dynamo that ceased ~3.7 Ga, recorded as crustal magnetization in the southern highlands. The InSight mission measured a core density deficit of ~5–7 % relative to pure Fe‑Ni, implying a light‑element (S, C, O) content of 15–20 wt %. Thermal evolution models show that Mars’ small size led to rapid core cooling; once the heat flux dropped below the adiabatic threshold (~10 mW m⁻²), convection stopped and the dynamo died. Numerical dynamo simulations for Mars reproduce a strong early dipole when the core heat flux is > 15 mW m⁻², but the field collapses when the flux falls below ~8 mW m⁻². The contrast among the three planets highlights the controlling role of core‑mantle heat flux, inner‑core growth, and mantle convection style on magnetic field generation. Future missions (e.g., VERITAS to Venus, Mars Sample Return) will refine core size, composition, and thermal state, providing tighter constraints on the parameter space of planetary dynamos.

  • Confusing the slow P₂ Biot wave with a conventional shear wave — the slow wave is compressional, highly attenuated, and only propagates at high frequencies.
  • Assuming normal‑mode splitting directly yields 3‑D structure without accounting for the limited sensitivity of low‑ℓ modes to small‑scale heterogeneity.
  • Treating regularization parameter λ as a fixed constant rather than a trade‑off parameter that must be calibrated (e.g., via L‑curve or cross‑validation).
  • Equating the absence of a magnetic field on Venus with a solid core — a stably stratified liquid core can also suppress dynamo action.
  • Neglecting the frequency dependence of poroelastic attenuation when comparing laboratory ultrasonic data (MHz) with field seismic data (Hz).
  • Overinterpreting checkerboard resolution tests as proof of true model resolution; they only indicate the linearized resolving power for the specific test pattern.
  • Assuming Mars’ early dynamo required an inner core — compositional convection from light‑element partitioning can drive a dynamo without inner‑core solidification.

These advanced topics integrate seismology, mineral physics, inverse theory, and planetary science to give a quantitative framework for interpreting Earth’s deep interior and its planetary analogues.

Seismic Coda Wave Interferometry and Scattering Tomography

Coda waves — the late‑arriving, multiply scattered energy that follows the direct S‑wave — have become a powerful probe of small‑scale heterogeneity and temporal changes in the crust and upper mantle. The technique exploits the fact that the coda envelope is a superposition of waves that have sampled a broad volume around the source‑receiver pair. By cross‑correlating coda windows from repeated earthquakes or from ambient noise, one can retrieve the Green’s function between two stations and measure minute velocity perturbations (Δv/v) at the 10⁻⁴ level. The method was first demonstrated on laboratory ultrasonic data (Snieder & Page, 1998) and transferred to field seismology by Snieder et al. (2002) using aftershocks of the 1999 Mw 7.1 Hector Mine earthquake. Since then, coda‑wave interferometry (CWI) has been applied to monitor volcanic unrest at Piton de la Fournaise (2007), to detect pre‑seismic velocity drops before the 2011 Mw 9.0 Tohoku‑Oki event (Brenguier et al., 2014), and to image damage zones around the 2008 Mw 7.9 Wenchuan fault (Zhang et al., 2019).

Scattering tomography extends CWI by inverting the spatial distribution of scattering strength (the inverse of the transport mean free path, ℓ*). The radiative‑transfer equation, solved in the diffusion approximation for late coda, links the energy density E(r,t) to the scattering coefficient g₀ = 1/ℓ*. By fitting the observed coda envelope decay rates across a network, one obtains a 3‑D map of g₀. Early work by Hoshiba (1991) on Japanese data revealed a high‑scattering zone beneath the volcanic front, later confirmed by dense array deployments. Modern implementations combine CWI with full‑waveform inversion of the early coda (e.g., Obermann et al., 2016) to separate intrinsic attenuation (Qᵢ⁻¹) from scattering attenuation (Qₛ⁻¹). The resulting Qₛ models correlate with fracture density, fluid saturation, and temperature, providing independent constraints on geothermal reservoirs and fault‑zone architecture.

Key practical considerations: (1) Frequency band selection — 1–10 Hz for crustal studies, 0.1–1 Hz for upper‑mantle scattering; (2) Station spacing — dense arrays (≤ 5 km) improve spatial resolution to a few kilometres; (3) Temporal stability — seasonal groundwater variations can produce apparent Δv/v of 10⁻³, requiring careful detrending; (4) Source distribution — repeating earthquakes or controlled sources (vibroseis) yield the most stable cross‑correlations. The technique complements traditional travel‑time tomography by sensing heterogeneity at scales below the Fresnel zone of direct waves, and it offers a unique window on time‑dependent processes such as fluid migration, stress‑induced microcracking, and volcanic magma movement.

Geomagnetic Jerks, Rapid Core Flow Changes, and Core‑Surface Dynamics

Geomagnetic jerks are abrupt changes in the secular variation (SV) of Earth’s magnetic field, appearing as step‑like inflections in the first time derivative of the field (dB/dt) at the Earth’s surface. The first well‑documented jerk occurred in 1969 (Courtillot et al., 1978), followed by global events in 1978, 1991, 2003, 2007, 2011, and 2016. Each jerk is detected simultaneously at observatories worldwide, implying a common origin in the fluid outer core. The amplitude of a jerk in the radial field component at the core‑mantle boundary (CMB) is typically 5–15 nT yr⁻¹, corresponding to a sudden acceleration of core flow of order 10⁻⁴ m s⁻¹ over a few months.

Core‑flow inversions that assimilate satellite (Ørsted, CHAMP, Swarm) and ground observatory data reveal that jerks coincide with localized, rapid changes in the tangential flow pattern just below the CMB. The 2003 jerk, for example, is associated with a strong azimuthal flow surge beneath the Atlantic sector, while the 2016 jerk correlates with a meridional flow reversal under the Pacific. Numerical geodynamo models (e.g., Aubert et al., 2010) reproduce jerk‑like events when the Lorentz force temporarily overwhelms the Coriolis force in regions of strong magnetic shear, leading to a rapid re‑organisation of the flow. The timescale of a jerk (months to a year) is far shorter than the magnetic diffusion time (~10⁴ yr), indicating that the underlying dynamics are essentially magnetostrophic and that the observed SV step reflects an impulsive torque on the core fluid.

Recent high‑resolution Swarm data have enabled the detection of “sub‑jerk” features — smaller, regional SV accelerations that may precede major jerks by 1–2 years. These precursors are interpreted as the signature of growing magnetic instabilities (e.g., magnetic Rossby waves) that eventually trigger a global jerk. Understanding jerks is crucial for improving geomagnetic field forecasts used in navigation, satellite attitude control, and space‑weather modelling. Moreover, the jerk recurrence interval (≈ 6–10 yr) provides a constraint on the core’s effective viscosity and the strength of the magnetic field at the CMB, linking observable SV to deep‑Earth physical parameters.

Worked Example: Estimating Core‑Flow Acceleration from a Geomagnetic Jerk
A geomagnetic jerk in 2003 produced a step increase of 12 nT yr⁻¹ in the radial SV at the Earth’s surface over a region of angular radius 30°. Assuming the field at the CMB is amplified by a factor of 10 relative to the surface and that the jerk reflects a uniform acceleration of a zonal flow band of width 1000 km at the top of the outer core, estimate the order‑of‑magnitude flow acceleration (m s⁻²).
1. Convert surface SV step to CMB: 12 nT yr⁻¹ × 10 = 120 nT yr⁻¹. 2. 1 yr = 3.15 × 10⁷ s, so Δ(dB/dt) ≈ 120 × 10⁻⁹ T / 3.15 × 10⁷ s ≈ 3.8 × 10⁻¹⁵ T s⁻¹. 3. Induction equation for a zonal flow Uφ: ∂Bᵣ/∂t ≈ (Bᵣ / L) Uφ, where L ≈ 1000 km = 10⁶ m is the flow length scale and Bᵣ ≈ 0.5 mT at CMB. 4. Rearranged: Uφ ≈ (∂Bᵣ/∂t) L / Bᵣ ≈ (3.8 × 10⁻¹⁵ × 10⁶) / 5 × 10⁻⁴ ≈ 7.6 × 10⁻⁶ m s⁻¹. 5. The jerk occurs over ~6 months (1.6 × 10⁷ s), so acceleration a ≈ ΔU / Δt ≈ 7.6 × 10⁻⁶ / 1.6 × 10⁷ ≈ 5 × 10⁻¹³ m s⁻². This matches published estimates of 10⁻¹³–10⁻¹² m s⁻² for jerk‑driven core‑flow accelerations.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the physical mechanism behind a geomagnetic jerk, focus on the magnetostrophic balance and the role of Lorentz‑force instabilities rather than thermal convection alone. Cite the 1969, 2003, and 2016 jerks as canonical examples, and be ready to convert a surface SV step to a core‑flow acceleration using the induction equation (∂B/∂t ≈ ∇×(U×B)). Remember that the amplification factor from surface to CMB is ~10 for the radial component, and that typical jerk timescales are months, not years.

Seismic Imaging of the Lithosphere‑Asthenosphere Boundary Using Receiver Functions and Surface Waves

The lithosphere‑asthenosphere boundary (LAB) marks the transition from rigid, plate‑like behaviour to ductile, convecting mantle. Its depth varies from ~50 km beneath young oceanic plates to >200 km beneath cratons. Two complementary seismic techniques — S‑receiver functions (SRF) and surface‑wave dispersion — have converged on a consistent picture of a sharp, often anisotropic, velocity drop of 3–6 % in shear‑wave speed (Vs) across the LAB. SRF analysis exploits the conversion of incident S‑waves to P‑waves (Sp) at a negative velocity gradient. The classic study by Rychert & Shearer (2009) using global broadband data identified a ubiquitous Sp phase at 60–90 km beneath oceans, with a polarity indicating a decrease in Vs with depth. Beneath continents, the Sp arrival is often split into two phases, suggesting a layered LAB: a sharp “mid‑lithospheric discontinuity” (MLD) at 80–120 km and a deeper, more gradual transition at 150–250 km (Abt et al., 2010).

Surface‑wave tomography, particularly fundamental‑mode Rayleigh‑wave phase velocities at periods 20–150 s, provides independent depth sensitivity. Joint inversion of Rayleigh‑wave dispersion curves and SRF move‑out times (e.g., Fischer et al., 2010) reduces trade‑offs between Vs magnitude and discontinuity depth. The resulting models show that the LAB beneath the Pacific plate at 70 Ma is ~65 km deep with a Vs drop of 4.5 %, while beneath the Kaapvaal craton the LAB lies at ~200 km with a 3 % drop. Azimuthal anisotropy measured from Love‑Rayleigh wave phase‑velocity differences reveals a fast‑axis alignment parallel to absolute plate motion in the asthenosphere, supporting the hypothesis of shear‑driven lattice‑preferred orientation of olivine.

Recent advances include the use of P‑to‑S receiver functions (Ps) from teleseismic P‑waves to image the LAB beneath oceans where S‑wave sources are scarce (Kawakatsu et al., 2009). The Ps phase from the LAB arrives ~10 s after the direct P, and its amplitude is sensitive to the impedance contrast. Combined Ps‑SRF stacks from the Cascadia Initiative amphibious array have resolved a double LAB structure: a sharp 5 % Vs drop at 45 km (interpreted as the base of the thermal lithosphere) and a broader 3 % drop at 80 km (the mechanical LAB). These observations constrain the rheological definition of the lithosphere and inform geodynamic models of plate coupling and mantle upwelling.

Deep Carbon Cycle: Geophysical Constraints from Seismic Velocity, Electrical Conductivity, and Diamond Inclusions

Carbon is a key volatile element that influences mantle melting, rheology, and the oxidation state of the deep Earth. Geophysical observations provide independent constraints on the distribution and form of carbon in the mantle. Seismic tomography reveals large low‑shear‑velocity provinces (LLSVPs) beneath Africa and the Pacific, where Vs is reduced by 1–2 % relative to the surrounding mantle. While temperature and composition both affect Vs, the co‑location of high electrical conductivity (σ ≈ 0.1–1 S m⁻¹) from magnetotelluric (MT) studies (e.g., Kelbert et al., 2009) suggests the presence of carbon‑bearing phases such as carbonate melt or metallic iron‑carbide. Laboratory measurements show that 0.1 wt % carbonate melt can increase σ by two orders of magnitude at transition‑zone pressures (15–20 GPa), while having a modest effect on Vs.

Diamond inclusions provide direct petrological evidence. Super‑deep diamonds from the Juina region (Brazil) and the Kankan region (Guinea) contain inclusions of majoritic garnet, Ca‑perovskite, and ferropericlase, indicating formation depths of 300–700 km (Walter et al., 2011). Carbon isotopic compositions (δ¹³C) of these diamonds range from –5 ‰ to –25 ‰, consistent with a subducted organic carbon component. Moreover, some diamonds host fluid inclusions of CO₂‑rich saline fluids, demonstrating that carbon is mobile in the transition zone and lower mantle. The coexistence of reduced (metallic Fe) and oxidized (carbonate) carbon species implies a heterogeneous redox state, which influences the speciation of carbon (diamond vs. carbonate vs. carbide) and the electrical conductivity of the host rock.

Integrated geophysical‑geochemical models (e.g., Thomson et al., 2016) combine seismic Vs anomalies, MT conductivity profiles, and diamond inclusion depths to estimate the global deep‑carbon budget. Current best estimates place 0.5–1.5 wt % carbon in the transition zone and up to 0.2 wt % in the lower mantle, corresponding to a total mantle carbon inventory of 10²²–10²³ g. This reservoir is comparable to the surface carbon budget and underscores the importance of deep‑carbon cycling for long‑term climate regulation. Future constraints will come from high‑pressure electrical conductivity measurements on Fe‑carbide and from multi‑observable inversions that jointly fit seismic, MT, and gravity data.

Induced Seismicity: Poroelastic Stress Transfer, Injection‑Induced Fault Reactivation, and Monitoring Strategies

Fluid injection for hydrocarbon extraction, geothermal energy, carbon sequestration, and wastewater disposal has been linked to a dramatic increase in seismicity rates in intraplate regions. The physical mechanism is poroelastic stress transfer: injection raises pore pressure (Δp) in a permeable formation, reducing the effective normal stress (σₙ' = σₙ – αΔp, where α ≈ 0.6–1.0 is Biot’s coefficient) on pre‑existing faults. When the Coulomb failure stress (ΔCFS = Δτ + μΔσₙ') exceeds a critical threshold, slip occurs. The diffusion equation ∂p/∂t = D∇²p (with hydraulic diffusivity D ≈ 0.1–10 m² s⁻¹) predicts a pressure front that expands as √(Dt), explaining the observed migration of seismicity away from injection wells at rates of kilometres per month.

Case studies illustrate the range of behaviours. The 2006 Basel geothermal project (Switzerland) injected 11 500 m³ of water at 5 km depth, triggering a Mw 3.4 event that caused surface damage; the seismicity cloud expanded laterally ~2 km over six months, consistent with D ≈ 1 m² s⁻¹. In Oklahoma, USA, wastewater disposal into the Arbuckle formation (depth 3–5 km) increased the annual M≥3 rate from <2 to >900 by 2015; the spatial correlation between injection volumes and seismicity clusters is strong (Ellsworth, 2013). The 2011 Mw 5.7 Prague, Oklahoma earthquake sequence was preceded by a decade of increasing injection rates, and finite‑fault modelling shows that the mainshock ruptured a fault segment that had been brought closer to failure by cumulative pore‑pressure diffusion.

Monitoring strategies combine real‑time seismic networks, continuous GPS, and InSAR to detect surface deformation (mm‑scale uplift/subsidence) associated with pressure changes. The “traffic‑light” protocol (green: continue, amber: reduce rate, red: suspend) uses magnitude thresholds (e.g., Mw 2.5 for amber, Mw 3.5 for red) and spatial proximity to critical infrastructure. Physics‑based forecasting models (e.g., the Rate‑and‑State friction framework coupled with pore‑pressure diffusion) are being tested in the Basel and Oklahoma settings to provide probabilistic hazard estimates. A key remaining challenge is the characterisation of fault permeability and orientation at depth, which controls the efficiency of pressure transmission and the likelihood of reactivation.

  • Confusing coda‑wave interferometry with ambient‑noise interferometry — CWI uses earthquake coda, not noise cross‑correlations, and measures relative velocity changes, not absolute structure.
  • Assuming every geomagnetic jerk is globally synchronous — some jerks (e.g., 2007) show regional amplitude variations and may be composites of multiple core‑flow events.
  • Equating the LAB depth from S‑receiver functions with the thermal lithosphere thickness — the seismic LAB often marks a rheological or melt‑related boundary that can be shallower than the 1300 °C isotherm.
  • Attributing all low‑velocity zones to temperature alone — compositional heterogeneity (carbonate melt, water, iron enrichment) can produce comparable Vs reductions without a thermal anomaly.
  • Treating induced seismicity as purely pressure‑driven — poroelastic stress changes (Δσ) can be comparable to pore‑pressure effects, especially for injections into low‑permeability formations where pressure diffusion is slow.
  • Neglecting the frequency dependence of electrical conductivity when linking MT anomalies to carbon — laboratory data are typically at 10⁻³–10⁻¹ Hz, while MT periods span 10⁻⁴–10⁴ s; extrapolation requires a conduction mechanism model.
  • Overinterpreting a single diamond inclusion depth as representative of the whole mantle carbon budget — diamond formation is episodic and spatially biased toward subduction zones and plume‑related metasomatism.

Seismic Source Scaling Relations and Earthquake Magnitude–Frequency Statistics

The quantitative description of earthquake sources rests on a set of scaling relations that link seismic moment (M₀), corner frequency (f_c), stress drop (Δσ), rupture area (A), and average slip (D). In the Brune ω² model the far‑field displacement spectrum is flat at low frequency and decays as ω⁻² above f_c, where f_c ≈ 4.9 β (Δσ/M₀)¹ᐟ³ and β is the shear‑wave speed. This yields the classic scaling M₀ ∝ Δσ A³ᐟ² and, assuming a constant stress drop, M₀ ∝ L³ where L is the characteristic rupture length. Empirical compilations show that stress drop is roughly constant (≈ 3 MPa) for crustal earthquakes but increases for deep events, implying a magnitude‑dependent scaling of rupture dimensions. The Gutenberg‑Richter frequency–magnitude relation log₁₀N = a − b M describes the cumulative number N of events with magnitude ≥ M; the b‑value is typically 0.9–1.1 for tectonic regimes but can be lower in volcanic or induced‑seismicity settings, reflecting a relatively larger proportion of large events. The moment‑magnitude scale M_w = (2/3) log₁₀M₀ − 6.07 (M₀ in N·m) provides a uniform measure that does not saturate, unlike local magnitude M_L. Combining the ω² source model with the Gutenberg‑Richter law permits estimation of the seismic moment release rate and the fraction of tectonic strain accommodated by earthquakes versus aseismic creep. In practice, the corner frequency is measured from P‑wave spectra after correcting for geometrical spreading, attenuation (Q), and site effects; the resulting stress‑drop estimates are then used to test whether a given fault segment follows a characteristic‑earthquake model or a scale‑invariant self‑similar rupture process.

Geophysical Constraints on Inner Core Growth, Age, and Solidification Rate

The solid inner core grows by the freezing of liquid iron alloy at the inner‑core boundary (ICB), releasing latent heat and light elements (S, O, Si, C) that drive compositional convection in the outer core. Seismic body‑wave phases PKIKP (traversing the inner core) and PKiKP (reflected from the ICB) provide differential travel‑time residuals that are sensitive to the ICB radius and to the velocity structure within the inner core. Global datasets give an ICB radius of 1221 ± 0.5 km, corresponding to an inner‑core volume increase of ≈ 0.5 km³ yr⁻¹. Thermal history models that couple core cooling, mantle heat flux, and the partitioning of light elements between solid and liquid predict an inner‑core age of 0.5–1.5 Ga, with a preferred value near 1 Ga. The solidification rate follows from the latent‑heat release L ≈ 750 kJ kg⁻¹ and the outer‑core heat flux across the core‑mantle boundary (CMB) of 5–15 TW; a CMB heat flux of 10 TW yields a growth rate of ≈ 1 mm yr⁻¹, consistent with the observed ICB radius change. Anisotropy of PKIKP travel times (≈ 3 % faster along the rotation axis) implies a textured inner core, likely produced by crystal alignment during solidification. The light‑element budget is constrained by the density deficit of the outer core (≈ 5–10 % relative to pure Fe) and by the seismic velocities of the inner core (V_P ≈ 11.0 km s⁻¹, V_S ≈ 3.5 km s⁻¹). Joint inversion of normal‑mode frequencies, body‑wave travel times, and the Earth’s moment of inertia narrows the permissible light‑element combinations to Fe‑S‑O or Fe‑Si‑O systems, each implying a different solidification trajectory and thus a different inner‑core age.

Worked Example: Estimating Inner‑Core Growth Rate from Seismic Data
A global seismic study measures a PKIKP‑PKiKP differential travel‑time residual of +0.12 s relative to a reference Earth model. Assuming the residual is caused solely by a 0.5 km increase in ICB radius over the past 30 years, compute the average inner‑core growth rate in mm yr⁻¹ and comment on its consistency with thermal‑history predictions.
The ICB radius change ΔR = 0.5 km = 500 m over Δt = 30 yr gives a linear growth rate v = ΔR/Δt = 500 m / 30 yr ≈ 16.7 m yr⁻¹ = 16 700 mm yr⁻¹. This value is orders of magnitude larger than the ≈ 1 mm yr⁻¹ predicted by standard thermal models, indicating that the observed travel‑time residual cannot be explained by a simple radial growth of the inner core alone. In practice, the residual reflects a combination of inner‑core anisotropy, lateral heterogeneity, and mantle structure; isolating the true growth signal requires joint inversion of multiple phases and normal modes. Therefore, the naive conversion of a single differential residual into a growth rate is a common pitfall.
Exam‑Day Strategy for Geophysics
When a question asks for a quantitative estimate, first identify the governing physical relation (e.g., Brune scaling, Gutenberg‑Richter law, inner‑core growth from latent heat). Write the equation in plain text, substitute the given numbers with units, and track the unit conversion explicitly. Examiners reward clear dimensional analysis more than a memorised final number. For conceptual parts, structure the answer as: (1) definition, (2) controlling parameters, (3) observational constraints, (4) current uncertainties. This four‑step template works for source scaling, mantle rheology, anisotropy, and inverse theory alike.

Mantle Rheology: Dislocation Creep, Diffusion Creep, and the Viscosity Profile

The long‑term deformation of the mantle is governed by thermally activated creep mechanisms that operate on geological time scales. Dislocation creep follows a power‑law flow law ε̇ = A σⁿ exp(−(E + PV)/RT) with stress exponent n ≈ 3–3.5, activation energy E ≈ 300–540 kJ mol⁻¹, and activation volume V ≈ 10–20 cm³ mol⁻¹. It dominates at high stresses (≳ 10 MPa) and relatively low temperatures, typical of the upper mantle and the lithospheric mantle. Diffusion creep (Nabarro‑Herring and Coble) has n = 1, lower activation energy (E ≈ 200–300 kJ mol⁻¹), and a strong grain‑size dependence (ε̇ ∝ d⁻ᵐ with m = 2–3). It becomes important in the hotter, lower‑stress environment of the deep upper mantle and transition zone, especially where water weakens the lattice and reduces grain size. The effective viscosity η_eff = σ/(2 ε̇) therefore varies by several orders of magnitude with depth: ≈ 10²⁰ Pa·s in the asthenosphere, rising to 10²²–10²³ Pa·s in the lower mantle where diffusion creep of bridgmanite and ferropericlase prevails. Water content, even at tens of ppm, can lower the viscosity by a factor of 10–100 by enhancing dislocation mobility and promoting grain‑boundary sliding. The viscosity profile inferred from post‑glacial rebound, geoid anomalies, and mantle convection models typically shows a low‑viscosity channel (≈ 10¹⁹ Pa·s) near 200 km depth, a modest increase through the transition zone, and a high‑viscosity lower mantle (≈ 10²² Pa·s). This layering controls the wavelength of dynamic topography, the speed of slab sinking, and the pattern of plate motions. Laboratory deformation experiments on olivine, wadsleyite, and ringwoodite at relevant pressures and temperatures provide the flow‑law parameters that are extrapolated to mantle conditions using the Arrhenius relation; uncertainties in the extrapolation dominate the error budget of global viscosity models.

Seismic Wave Propagation in Anisotropic Media: Shear‑Wave Splitting and Azimuthal Anisotropy

Seismic anisotropy arises when elastic properties vary with direction, most commonly due to lattice‑preferred orientation (LPO) of olivine in the upper mantle or aligned cracks and melt pockets in the crust. In a transversely isotropic medium with a vertical symmetry axis (VTI), the shear wave splits into a fast quasi‑SV and a slow quasi‑SH component; in a horizontally transverse isotropic medium (HTI) the fast polarization aligns with the symmetry axis. The observable is shear‑wave splitting: a single incident S‑wave arrives as two polarized phases separated by a delay time δt. For teleseismic SKS phases that traverse the anisotropic mantle, the fast polarization direction φ and delay time δt are measured by rotating the horizontal components to maximize energy on the transverse component. Typical δt values are 0.5–2.0 s for continental paths, corresponding to an integrated anisotropy of 1–3 % over a 200‑km thick anisotropic layer. Azimuthal anisotropy, where φ varies with back‑azimuth, reveals depth‑dependent fabric: a constant φ with depth indicates a single coherent fabric (e.g., fossil lithospheric anisotropy), while systematic rotation of φ with depth signals mantle flow reorientation (e.g., asthenospheric shear beneath moving plates). Surface‑wave phase‑velocity maps (Love vs. Rayleigh) provide complementary constraints on radial anisotropy (V_SH ≠ V_SV) in the upper mantle and transition zone. Joint inversion of SKS splitting, surface‑wave dispersion, and body‑wave travel times yields depth‑resolved anisotropy models that can be compared with geodynamic simulations of LPO development. A key diagnostic is the correlation between fast‑axis orientation and absolute plate motion: a strong correlation supports the hypothesis that asthenospheric shear aligns olivine a‑axes with the flow direction, whereas a lack of correlation points to frozen‑in lithospheric fabric or complex three‑dimensional flow.

Bayesian Geophysical Inverse Theory: Prior Information, Posterior Uncertainty, and Model Selection

Geophysical inverse problems seek model parameters m (e.g., velocity structure, density, anisotropy) that explain observed data d (travel times, waveforms, gravity, magnetic fields). In the Bayesian framework the posterior probability density p(m|d) ∝ p(d|m) p(m) combines the likelihood p(d|m) (often Gaussian with data covariance C_d) and the prior p(m) encoding geological knowledge, smoothness, or parameter bounds. The posterior mean provides the optimal estimate, while the posterior covariance quantifies uncertainty and parameter trade‑offs. For linear problems with Gaussian priors the posterior is analytic: m_post = m_prior + Gᵀ(C_d + G C_m Gᵀ)⁻¹(d − G m_prior), where G is the sensitivity matrix and C_m the prior covariance. Non‑linear problems (e.g., full‑waveform inversion) require sampling methods such as Markov‑chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) or Hamiltonian Monte Carlo to explore the high‑dimensional posterior. Model selection uses the Bayesian evidence Z = ∫ p(d|m) p(m) dm; the Bayes factor between two models quantifies the relative support, automatically penalising excessive complexity (Occam’s razor). Practical implementations adopt hierarchical priors (e.g., unknown noise variance) and trans‑dimensional reversible‑jump MCMC to let the data determine the number of model parameters (e.g., layer interfaces). Resolution analysis in the Bayesian context is expressed through the posterior covariance or the model resolution matrix R = ∂m_post/∂d G; diagonal elements near unity indicate well‑resolved parameters. A common pitfall is over‑reliance on the prior when data coverage is sparse; sensitivity tests that vary the prior covariance length scale reveal whether features are data‑driven or prior‑driven. Modern applications combine multiple data types (seismic, gravity, magnetotelluric) in a joint likelihood, with cross‑gradient constraints to enforce structural similarity, yielding more robust Earth models.

  • Confusing the stress exponent n in dislocation creep with the b‑value in Gutenberg‑Richter statistics — they describe fundamentally different physical processes.
  • Assuming a single SKS splitting measurement uniquely determines the depth of anisotropy — without depth‑sensitive phases the result is non‑unique.
  • Treating the Bayesian posterior mean as the “true” model while ignoring the full posterior covariance — uncertainties and trade‑offs are essential for interpretation.
  • Equating the inner‑core growth rate derived from a single differential travel‑time residual with the long‑term thermal‑history rate — residuals contain contributions from anisotropy and mantle structure.
  • Using a constant stress‑drop scaling for all magnitudes — stress drop may increase with depth and differ between tectonic and induced events.
  • Neglecting the grain‑size dependence of diffusion creep when extrapolating laboratory flow laws to mantle conditions — grain size can vary by orders of magnitude.
  • Overinterpreting a Bayes factor without checking the sensitivity to prior choices — the evidence can be prior‑dominated for poorly constrained problems.

Adjoint Methods for Full‑Waveform Inversion and Gradient‑Based Model Updating

Full‑waveform inversion (FWI) seeks a subsurface model that reproduces observed seismograms by minimizing a misfit functional, typically the L2 norm of waveform residuals. The gradient of this functional with respect to model parameters is obtained efficiently through the adjoint‑state method: a forward simulation generates the synthetic wavefield, the residuals are injected as sources in a time‑reversed adjoint simulation, and the interaction of forward and adjoint wavefields yields the Fréchet kernels. In practice the forward problem is solved with a high‑order spectral‑element or finite‑difference scheme on a mesh that honors topography and bathymetry, while the adjoint problem uses the same discretization to guarantee discrete adjoint consistency. Preconditioning the gradient with an approximation of the Hessian diagonal (e.g., the source‑receiver illumination matrix) mitigates the strong parameter trade‑offs between velocity and density. Multi‑scale strategies — starting from low‑frequency data (≈0.01 Hz) and progressively adding higher frequencies — reduce cycle‑skipping, a major non‑linearity where the misfit surface contains many local minima. Regularization is introduced through Tikhonov smoothing, total‑variation penalties, or model‑covariance operators that encode geological prior information such as layering or known fault zones. Recent advances include the use of envelope‑based misfit functions and optimal‑transport distances to enlarge the basin of attraction, as well as stochastic gradient descent where subsets of sources are used per iteration to lower computational cost. The adjoint framework also accommodates elastic, anisotropic, and attenuative media by augmenting the forward/adjoint equations with the appropriate rheological parameters, enabling joint inversion for Vp, Vs, density, and Q. Validation relies on resolution analysis (e.g., point‑spread functions) and checkerboard tests, but the ultimate benchmark is the ability to predict independent data such as body‑wave travel times or surface‑wave dispersion curves not used in the inversion.

Geodetic Constraints on Interseismic Coupling, Slow Slip Events, and Transient Deformation

Space‑based geodesy (GNSS, InSAR, and satellite laser ranging) provides millimetre‑level measurements of surface velocity fields that are inverted for fault slip‑rate distributions. The interseismic coupling coefficient, defined as the ratio of observed slip deficit rate to the long‑term plate convergence rate, quantifies the fraction of the fault that is locked versus creeping. Inverting GNSS time series with a back‑slip model yields a spatial map of coupling; high coupling (>0.8) correlates with the rupture patches of historic megathrust earthquakes, while low coupling zones often host slow slip events (SSEs). SSEs are transient aseismic slip episodes lasting days to years, detected as coherent velocity reversals in GNSS and as line‑of‑sight displacement gradients in InSAR. Their moment magnitude typically ranges from Mw 6.5 to 7.5, releasing strain without generating high‑frequency seismic radiation. The physics of SSEs is linked to rate‑and‑state friction parameters (a‑b) that become velocity‑strengthening at the temperatures and pressures of the deep seismogenic zone, allowing stable sliding. Coupled geodetic‑seismic inversions now resolve the spatiotemporal evolution of SSEs, revealing along‑strike migration speeds of 5–15 km day⁻¹ and a tendency to nucleate near the downdip edge of the locked zone. Transient deformation also includes post‑seismic afterslip, viscoelastic relaxation in the asthenosphere, and poroelastic rebound; separating these contributions requires joint inversion of GNSS, InSAR, and seismic data with rheologically layered Earth models. The resulting coupling and SSE catalogs feed directly into probabilistic seismic hazard assessments by updating the time‑dependent probability of large earthquakes on partially locked segments.

Worked Example: Estimating Coupling from GNSS Velocities
A subduction zone segment converges at 80 mm yr⁻¹. GNSS stations on the overriding plate show a trench‑parallel velocity of 12 mm yr⁻¹ relative to the stable interior. Assuming a simple back‑slip model with a single locked patch, compute the interseismic coupling coefficient.
The slip deficit rate equals the difference between the plate convergence rate and the observed surface velocity projected onto the convergence direction. Here the deficit = 80 mm yr⁻¹ − 12 mm yr⁻¹ = 68 mm yr⁻¹. Coupling coefficient = deficit / convergence = 68 / 80 = 0.85. Thus the patch is 85 % locked, consistent with a high‑coupling zone capable of generating a megathrust event.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the coupling coefficient, first identify the long‑term convergence rate (often given or derivable from plate models) and the geodetically measured surface velocity. Subtract the observed velocity from the convergence rate to obtain the slip deficit, then divide by the convergence rate. Remember that coupling is dimensionless and bounded between 0 (fully creeping) and 1 (fully locked).

High‑Pressure Electrical Conductivity of Mantle Minerals and Implications for Water Content

Electrical conductivity in the mantle is dominated by charge transport through point defects in nominally anhydrous minerals (olivine, wadsleyite, ringwoodite, bridgmanite) and by interconnected melt or fluid phases. Laboratory measurements at pressures up to 30 GPa and temperatures to 2000 K use multi‑anvil apparatus with impedance spectroscopy, revealing that conductivity follows an Arrhenius law σ = σ₀ exp(−Eₐ/RT) where the activation energy Eₐ decreases with increasing water fugacity because hydrogen incorporates as protonic defects (OH⁻) that enhance charge mobility. For olivine, dry conductivity at 14 GPa and 1600 K is ≈10⁻³ S m⁻¹, while 0.1 wt % H₂O raises it by two orders of magnitude. Magnetotelluric (MT) surveys image bulk conductivity structures; high‑conductivity anomalies (0.1–1 S m⁻¹) in the mantle wedge above subducting slabs are interpreted as hydrated olivine or low‑degree melt. Joint inversion of MT and seismic velocity models constrains the water partitioning between minerals and melt, because seismic velocities are less sensitive to small water concentrations than conductivity. In the transition zone, the high conductivity of wadsleyite and ringwoodite (up to 10⁻¹ S m⁻¹ at 15 GPa) suggests that a water‑rich transition zone could store several oceans of water, influencing mantle rheology and the deep carbon cycle. However, conductivity is also affected by iron content, oxygen fugacity, and the presence of minor phases such as garnet or ferropericlase; therefore, quantitative water estimates require self‑consistent thermodynamic modeling of defect chemistry combined with geophysical data.

Inner Core Hemispherical Asymmetry, Seismic Velocity Structure, and Geodynamic Implications

Seismic observations reveal a pronounced hemispherical dichotomy in the inner core: the eastern hemisphere (beneath the Pacific) exhibits higher P‑wave velocity (≈11.3 km s⁻¹) and stronger anisotropy (≈3 % fast axis aligned with the rotation axis) than the western hemisphere (beneath the Atlantic) where velocities are ≈0.5 % lower and anisotropy weaker. Differential travel‑time measurements of PKIKP and PKiKP phases, as well as normal‑mode splitting functions, constrain the asymmetry to the upper ≈300 km of the inner core. Two end‑member geodynamic models explain the pattern: (1) translational convection, where solidification on one side and melting on the other drives a lateral flow that aligns iron crystals, and (2) heterogeneous nucleation of the inner core with a permanent east‑west thermal gradient imposed by mantle convection. The translational model predicts a westward drift of the inner core relative to the mantle at ≈0.1–0.3° yr⁻¹, consistent with observed differential rotation rates from PKIKP coda correlation studies. However, recent high‑resolution PKPbc‑df differential times suggest the asymmetry may be confined to a shallow “hemispherical layer” rather than the entire inner core, implying a more complex solidification history. The asymmetry also influences the geodynamo by modulating the heat flux across the inner‑core boundary; a laterally varying heat flux can generate non‑axisymmetric flow in the outer core, potentially linking inner‑core structure to the observed secular variation and the South Atlantic Anomaly. Future constraints will come from array‑based waveform modeling of PKIKP precursors and from mineral‑physics measurements of iron alloy elasticity at inner‑core conditions.

Phase Transition Kinetics, Seismic Scattering, and the 410/660 km Discontinuities

The 410 km and 660 km seismic discontinuities correspond to the olivine‑wadsleyite and ringwoodite‑bridgmanite + ferropericlase phase transitions, respectively. Their sharpness and topography are controlled by the kinetics of the transformations, which depend on temperature, composition, and the presence of water. In cold subducting slabs the transitions are depressed (410 km deeper, 660 km shallower) and broadened because the Clapeyron slopes are negative for the 410 km and positive for the 660 km transition, causing a “double‑crossing” of the phase boundaries within the slab. Seismic scattering from the rough topography of these boundaries generates coda waves that are observed in short‑period P‑wave records; the scattering amplitude scales with the RMS height of the undulations (typically 5–15 km) and the correlation length (≈50 km). Receiver‑function stacks and SS‑precursor analyses map the global topography, revealing that the 660 km discontinuity is globally smoother than the 410 km, consistent with a higher activation energy for the ringwoodite breakdown. Water lowers the transition pressures by up to 2 GPa, shifting the discontinuities upward and reducing their impedance contrast, which in turn diminishes reflected amplitudes. Joint inversion of travel‑time residuals, reflected‑wave amplitudes, and scattering coda provides constraints on the water content of the transition zone, with current estimates suggesting 0.1–0.5 wt % H₂O globally, but locally up to 1 wt % in hydrated slabs. These results link the mineral‑physics kinetics to large‑scale mantle dynamics, because the buoyancy forces associated with the phase changes influence slab stagnation, plume penetration, and the overall convection pattern.

  • Confusing the adjoint‑state gradient with the finite‑difference gradient — the adjoint method yields the exact gradient at the cost of two wave simulations, whereas finite differences require N + 1 simulations for N parameters.
  • Assuming a single GNSS velocity vector directly gives the coupling coefficient without correcting for rigid‑block rotation and reference‑frame effects.
  • Treating MT conductivity anomalies as uniquely indicating melt — hydrated minerals can produce comparable conductivities at mantle temperatures.
  • Equating inner‑core hemispherical asymmetry with whole‑inner‑core rotation — the asymmetry may be confined to a shallow layer and does not require bulk differential rotation.
  • Neglecting the effect of water on the Clapeyron slopes when interpreting 410/660 km topography — even modest water contents shift transition depths by several kilometres.
  • Over‑interpreting a single scattering coda measurement as a direct estimate of discontinuity roughness — scattering amplitude also depends on incident wave frequency and correlation length.
  • Using a constant activation energy for all mantle minerals in conductivity models — each phase has distinct defect chemistry and pressure dependence.

The advanced topics above integrate seismology, geodesy, mineral physics, and geomagnetism to provide a coherent framework for interpreting deep‑Earth processes in competitive examinations.

Seismic Imaging of Mantle Plume Conduits Using Finite‑Frequency Tomography

Finite‑frequency tomography replaces the ray‑theoretical assumption of infinitely thin rays with volumetric sensitivity kernels that account for wavefront healing and diffraction. The kernels, often called banana‑doughnut kernels, have zero sensitivity exactly along the geometric ray path and maximum sensitivity in a surrounding tube whose radius scales with the Fresnel zone width (≈√(λ L) where λ is wavelength and L is propagation distance). This property allows plume conduits, which are typically 100–300 km wide, to be resolved even when the ray coverage is sparse. In practice, travel‑time measurements of teleseismic P and S phases are inverted for three‑dimensional perturbations in Vp and Vs using a linearized Born approximation. The resulting models beneath Hawaii, Iceland, and the Afar hotspot show low‑velocity columns that extend from the core‑mantle boundary to the upper mantle, with Vs reductions of 2–4 % relative to the surrounding mantle. The amplitude of the anomaly correlates with the buoyancy flux estimated from surface heat flow and geoid data, providing an independent check on the thermal versus compositional origin of the plume. A key limitation is the trade‑off between vertical resolution and kernel width: at periods of 20–50 s the Fresnel zone radius at 1500 km depth is ≈250 km, so narrow plume tails (<100 km) remain below the resolution limit. Recent advances combine finite‑frequency kernels with adjoint‑state gradient calculations, enabling full‑waveform inversion that exploits both travel‑time and amplitude information, thereby sharpening the plume image and reducing smearing artifacts.

Geophysical Constraints on the Post‑Perovskite Phase Transition at the Core‑Mantle Boundary

The discovery of a high‑pressure polymorph of MgSiO₃ — post‑perovskite — above ≈125 GPa and 2500 K has reshaped interpretations of the D″ region. Laboratory measurements using laser‑heated diamond‑anvil cells and synchrotron X‑ray diffraction show that the transition from perovskite to post‑perovskite produces a 1–2 % increase in density and a 2–3 % increase in Vs, while Vp changes are smaller. Because the Clapeyron slope of the transition is positive (≈+6 MPa K⁻¹), colder regions of the lowermost mantle (e.g., beneath subducted slabs) should host a deeper post‑perovskite boundary, whereas hotter regions (e.g., beneath large low‑shear‑velocity provinces) should have a shallower boundary. Seismic observations of a sharp Vs increase of ≈2 % at depths of 2600–2700 km, often accompanied by strong azimuthal anisotropy, are consistent with a post‑perovskite lens. The anisotropy arises from the preferred orientation of post‑perovskite crystals, which have a highly anisotropic elastic tensor; lattice‑preferred orientation developed by mantle flow aligns the fast shear‑wave polarization parallel to the flow direction. Global stacks of ScS and S‑diff phases map the topography of the D″ discontinuity, revealing undulations of ±100 km that correlate with tomographic images of slab graveyards and plume roots. However, the exact pressure‑temperature conditions of the transition remain uncertain because the Fe content of the lower mantle shifts the transition pressure by up to 10 GPa per 10 % Fe. Joint inversion of seismic velocities, density (from normal‑mode splitting), and geodynamic models of mantle flow is currently the most robust way to constrain the Fe partitioning and the depth extent of the post‑perovskite layer.

Worked Example: Estimating Post‑Perovskite Depth from ScS Travel‑Time Residuals
A set of ScS phases recorded at a broadband station shows a systematic early arrival of 0.8 s relative to a 1‑D reference model for paths sampling the D″ region beneath the Pacific. Assuming the early arrival is caused solely by a 2 % Vs increase across a sharp post‑perovskite boundary, estimate the depth perturbation of the boundary relative to the reference depth of 2700 km. Use a simple ray‑theoretical approximation where the travel‑time change Δt ≈ –(ΔVs/Vs) · (Δh / V̄s) with V̄s ≈ 5.5 km s⁻¹ in D″.
The fractional Vs increase is ΔVs/Vs = 0.02. Rearranging the approximation gives Δh ≈ –Δt · V̄s / (ΔVs/Vs) = –(–0.8 s) · 5.5 km s⁻¹ / 0.02 = 0.8 · 5.5 / 0.02 = 220 km. The positive sign indicates the boundary is shallower by ≈220 km, i.e., at ≈2480 km depth. This large uplift is consistent with a hot, buoyant plume root beneath the Pacific large low‑shear‑velocity province.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a quantitative estimate from travel‑time anomalies, first identify the assumed velocity contrast and the reference velocity. Write the linearized relation explicitly, substitute numbers with units, and keep track of signs. A quick sanity check: a 1 % Vs change over 100 km produces ≈0.2 s travel‑time shift for ScS; scale accordingly.

Joint Inversion of Seismic and Gravity Data for Lithospheric Structure

Seismic tomography provides high‑resolution images of Vp and Vs but is intrinsically insensitive to absolute density. Gravity measurements, by contrast, integrate density contrasts over large volumes but lack vertical resolution. Joint inversion exploits the petrological link between seismic velocities and density (e.g., the Nafe‑Drake or Birch‑Murnaghan relationships) to simultaneously fit travel‑time residuals and free‑air or Bouguer gravity anomalies. The objective function typically combines a seismic misfit term (weighted by data covariance) and a gravity misfit term, with a regularization term that enforces smoothness or geological plausibility. Because the gravity kernel is broad, the inversion is often parameterized on a coarser grid for density than for velocity, or a cross‑gradient constraint is applied to force structural similarity between the two models without imposing a fixed velocity‑density scaling. Applications to cratonic regions such as the Kaapvaal and Slave cratons reveal a high‑velocity, high‑density lithospheric keel extending to 250–300 km depth, whereas the surrounding Proterozoic mobile belts show lower velocities and densities consistent with metasomatic enrichment. In rift settings (e.g., the East African Rift), joint models resolve a low‑velocity, low‑density asthenospheric upwelling that is not apparent in seismic data alone because of limited ray coverage. A persistent challenge is the non‑uniqueness introduced by the trade‑off between temperature and composition: a 100 K temperature increase reduces Vs by ≈1 % and density by ≈0.3 %, while a 5 % basaltic enrichment produces a similar Vs reduction but a density increase. Incorporating additional constraints — such as surface heat flow, xenolith petrology, or magnetotelluric conductivity — helps to break this trade‑off and yields a more geologically realistic lithospheric model.

Seismic Wave Propagation in Partially Molten Media: Melt‑Induced Velocity Reductions and Attenuation

Partial melt dramatically alters elastic wave speeds and attenuation because the melt phase is orders of magnitude more compliant than the solid matrix. Effective‑medium theories (e.g., the Hashin‑Shtrikman bounds, the differential effective medium model, and the self‑consistent approximation) predict that for melt fractions φ up to ≈0.1 the shear modulus μ drops roughly as μ ≈ μ₀(1 – αφ) with α ≈ 5–10 depending on melt geometry (films, tubes, or spheroids), while the bulk modulus K is less affected. Consequently, Vs decreases much more strongly than Vp, leading to a characteristic increase in the Vp/Vs ratio that is widely used as a melt indicator in seismic tomography. Attenuation (inverse quality factor Q⁻¹) peaks when the seismic period matches the melt‑squirt relaxation time, which for typical mantle melt viscosities (10–100 Pa s) and grain sizes (1–10 mm) falls in the 0.1–10 Hz band. This frequency‑dependent attenuation produces measurable dispersion: phase velocities increase with frequency across the relaxation peak. Observations from mid‑ocean ridge seismic experiments (e.g., the MELT experiment on the East Pacific Rise) show Vs reductions of 5–10 % and Qs values as low as 30–50 within the melt lens, consistent with φ ≈ 0.02–0.05. In continental rift zones, receiver‑function analyses detect a sharp Vs drop at the base of the crust that correlates with high Vp/Vs and low Q, interpreted as a melt‑rich sill complex. A key caveat is that similar seismic signatures can be produced by high‑temperature, water‑rich solid mantle (hydrous weakening) without any melt; therefore, joint interpretation with electrical conductivity (which is far more sensitive to interconnected melt) and petrology is essential for a robust melt diagnosis.

Monte Carlo Uncertainty Quantification in Geophysical Inverse Problems

Deterministic inverse methods (e.g., damped least squares) provide a single best‑fit model but often underestimate uncertainties because they linearize the forward problem around the solution and ignore multi‑modal posterior distributions. Monte Carlo approaches, particularly Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC), sample the full posterior probability density p(m|d) ∝ p(d|m) p(m) where p(d|m) is the likelihood (usually Gaussian with data covariance) and p(m) encodes prior information such as smoothness, bounds, or geological facies. The Metropolis‑Hastings algorithm and its adaptive variants (e.g., DREAM, Hamiltonian Monte Carlo) are widely used; they require only the ability to evaluate the forward model and its misfit, not its gradient. For high‑dimensional problems (10⁴–10⁶ model parameters) the computational cost of thousands of forward simulations is prohibitive, so surrogate models (Gaussian process emulators, polynomial chaos expansions) or dimension‑reduction techniques (principal component analysis of the model space) are employed. In seismic tomography, MCMC has been applied to regional body‑wave travel‑time datasets to quantify the uncertainty of low‑velocity anomalies beneath volcanoes, revealing that the amplitude of a 3 % Vs anomaly may have a 95 % credible interval of ±1.5 % — far larger than the formal error from a linearized covariance estimate. In gravity inversion, transdimensional MCMC (reversible‑jump) allows the number of density interfaces to vary, providing a natural Occam’s razor. The main pitfalls are poor chain convergence (diagnosed with Gelman‑Rubin statistics), strong parameter correlations that slow mixing, and the choice of prior which can dominate the posterior when data are sparse. Best practice includes running multiple independent chains, thinning to reduce autocorrelation, and reporting marginal posterior summaries (median, credible intervals) rather than a single point estimate.

  • Assuming finite‑frequency kernels are identical to ray paths — they have zero sensitivity on the geometric ray and peak off‑axis.
  • Treating the post‑perovskite transition depth as globally uniform — it varies by >200 km due to temperature and composition.
  • Using a single velocity‑density scaling law in joint inversion — the scaling depends on temperature, composition, and melt fraction.
  • Equating high Vp/Vs solely with melt — hydrous, hot solid mantle can produce similar ratios.
  • Reporting only the maximum‑a‑posteriori model from MCMC without credible intervals — the posterior is often non‑Gaussian.
  • Neglecting the frequency dependence of attenuation when interpreting Q values — Q measured at 1 Hz differs from Q at 0.01 Hz.
  • Assuming a constant prior variance for all model parameters in Bayesian inversion — geological units require distinct prior variances.

Seismic Wave Propagation in Viscoelastic Anisotropic Media: Laboratory Measurements and Seismic Observations

Viscoelastic anisotropy combines two fundamental departures from the ideal elastic isotropic model: frequency‑dependent attenuation and direction‑dependent wave speeds. In the laboratory, polycrystalline aggregates of olivine, pyroxene, and garnet are deformed under controlled stress, temperature, and water‑content conditions to produce lattice‑preferred orientation (LPO) and shape‑preferred orientation (SPO). Ultrasonic pulse‑transmission experiments at 0.5–2 MHz measure phase velocities and quality factors Q for compressional (P) and shear (S) waves as a function of propagation angle relative to the symmetry axes. The resulting stiffness tensor Cijkl(ω) is complex; its real part governs velocity anisotropy while the imaginary part encodes attenuation anisotropy. Typical upper‑mantle olivine aggregates exhibit 5–10 % P‑wave azimuthal anisotropy and 10–15 % S‑wave splitting, with QS varying by a factor of 2–3 between fast and slow shear polarizations. The frequency dependence follows a power‑law Q⁻¹ ∝ ω−α with α ≈ 0.2–0.3, consistent with dislocation‑creep‑controlled anelasticity. In the field, shear‑wave splitting measurements from SKS, SKKS, and local S phases map the fast polarization direction and delay time δt. When combined with surface‑wave azimuthal anisotropy (Rayleigh and Love wave phase‑velocity maps), the data constrain the depth distribution of anisotropic layers. A key diagnostic is the ratio of splitting intensity to surface‑wave anisotropy: a high ratio points to a thin, strongly anisotropic layer (e.g., the lithosphere‑asthenosphere boundary), whereas a low ratio suggests distributed anisotropy through the upper mantle. Viscoelastic effects also manifest as frequency‑dependent phase velocities: Rayleigh‑wave phase velocity increases by 0.5–1 % between 20 s and 100 s periods in regions of high attenuation (Qμ ≈ 80). Joint inversion of splitting, surface‑wave dispersion, and attenuation tomography yields a self‑consistent model of the complex stiffness tensor, revealing that the asthenosphere beneath mid‑ocean ridges possesses a vertically aligned symmetry axis (VTI) with a 3–5 % radial anisotropy (VSH > VSV) and Qμ ≈ 70, while cratonic lithosphere shows a horizontally aligned axis (HTI) with weaker radial anisotropy but stronger azimuthal anisotropy. These observations confirm that both LPO and melt‑induced SPO contribute to the observed viscoelastic anisotropic signature.

Geophysical Constraints on Earth's Core Composition from Seismic Velocities, Density, and Cosmochemical Models

The outer core is known to be a liquid iron alloy with a density deficit of ≈ 5–10 % relative to pure iron at core pressures (135–330 GPa) and temperatures (4000–6000 K). Seismic body‑wave travel times (PKP, PKiKP) and normal‑mode frequencies provide precise constraints on the compressional velocity VP and density ρ profiles (PREM, AK135). The observed VP is ≈ 4–5 % lower than that of pure Fe at the same P‑T conditions, while the density is ≈ 8 % lower. Light elements — principally S, O, Si, C, and H — are invoked to explain the deficits. Cosmochemical arguments from chondritic meteorites and mantle siderophile element depletions suggest that the core contains ≈ 1.5–2.5 wt % S, 0.5–1.5 wt % O, and up to 1 wt % Si, with C and H as minor contributors. High‑pressure experiments using diamond‑anvil cells and laser heating measure the sound velocities and densities of Fe‑light‑element alloys up to 200 GPa. For Fe‑8 wt % S, VP matches the outer‑core profile within 0.5 % but the density remains ≈ 2 % high; adding 2 wt % O brings both VP and ρ into agreement. Fe‑Si alloys require ≈ 6 wt % Si to match VP, but then the density deficit is over‑corrected. Ab initio molecular‑dynamics simulations compute the equation of state and elastic moduli of candidate alloys at core temperatures, confirming that a ternary Fe‑S‑O system with ≈ 1.8 wt % S and 1.2 wt % O satisfies seismic and cosmochemical constraints simultaneously. The inner core, solid Fe‑alloy, shows a 2–3 % VP anisotropy (fast axis parallel to rotation) and a density jump of ≈ 4.5 % at the inner‑core boundary. Partitioning experiments indicate that S and O preferentially remain in the liquid, while Si partitions more evenly, implying the inner core is slightly depleted in S and O relative to the outer core. This compositional gradient drives compositional convection that powers the geodynamo. Uncertainties remain in the exact temperature profile (adiabatic vs. superadiabatic) and the possible presence of a stratified layer at the top of the outer core (the “F‑layer”), which would modify the light‑element budget inferred from seismic velocities alone.

Worked Example: Estimating Light‑Element Content from Seismic Velocity Deficit
Given that pure Fe at outer‑core conditions has VP = 11.0 km s⁻¹ and ρ = 13.0 g cm⁻³, while PREM gives VP = 10.3 km s⁻¹ and ρ = 12.0 g cm⁻³, estimate the combined weight percent of S and O required if each 1 wt % S reduces VP by 0.15 km s⁻¹ and ρ by 0.12 g cm⁻³, and each 1 wt % O reduces VP by 0.10 km s⁻¹ and ρ by 0.08 g cm⁻³. Assume only S and O are present.
Let x = wt % S, y = wt % O. Velocity deficit: 11.0 − 10.3 = 0.7 km s⁻¹ = 0.15x + 0.10y. Density deficit: 13.0 − 12.0 = 1.0 g cm⁻³ = 0.12x + 0.08y. Solve the linear system: multiply the first equation by 0.08 → 0.012x + 0.008y = 0.056; multiply the second by 0.10 → 0.012x + 0.008y = 0.10. The two equations are inconsistent, indicating that S and O alone cannot simultaneously satisfy both deficits with the assumed linear coefficients. In practice, a third light element (Si or C) or non‑linear mixing is required. This illustrates why joint seismic‑cosmochemical inversion uses ternary or quaternary alloy models.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the light‑element budget of the core, first write the seismic deficits (ΔVP, Δρ) relative to pure Fe. Then list the candidate light elements and their experimentally calibrated effects on VP and ρ. Set up a linear (or non‑linear) system and comment on the need for at least three elements to satisfy both constraints. Mention the role of cosmochemical siderophile depletions as an independent check.

Seismic Imaging of Subducted Slabs Using Seismic Reflection and Refraction Profiling

Active‑source seismic reflection and wide‑angle refraction surveys across convergent margins provide the highest‑resolution images of subducted slab geometry, internal structure, and the overlying mantle wedge. In a typical marine experiment, a tuned air‑gun array (≈ 6000 in³) fires every 20–30 s while a 6–10 km streamer records reflected energy to 15–20 s two‑way travel time. Wide‑angle data are captured by ocean‑bottom seismometers (OBS) spaced 10–15 km apart, extending offsets to 150–200 km. Reflection processing (pre‑stack depth migration with velocity models from refraction tomography) images the slab top as a high‑amplitude, continuous reflector from the trench to 150–200 km depth, marking the plate interface. The slab’s internal reflectivity reveals a double‑layered structure: an upper crustal layer (≈ 5–7 km thick) with strong, laterally continuous reflections from basaltic layer 2/3 boundaries, and a lower mantle‑peridotite layer that is largely transparent but shows sporadic reflections from dehydration‑induced metamorphic fronts (e.g., lawsonite‑eclogite transition at ≈ 70–90 km). Refraction tomography inverts first‑arrival travel times (Pg, PmP, Pn) for a 2‑D VP model. The slab exhibits VP ≈ 7.8–8.2 km s⁻¹ in the crust and 8.0–8.4 km s⁻¹ in the mantle portion, compared with 7.0–7.5 km s⁻¹ in the surrounding mantle wedge. A low‑velocity zone (LVZ) of 6.5–6.8 km s⁻¹ is often imaged directly above the slab, interpreted as serpentinized mantle wedge or fluid‑rich melange. The slab dip derived from reflection images (typically 20–30° in the upper 100 km, steepening to 45–60° below) is cross‑checked against the refraction‑derived velocity gradient. In the Cascadia and Nankai margins, combined reflection‑refraction profiles have resolved the megathrust fault zone as a ≤ 1 km thick, high‑reflectivity band with VP/VS > 1.9, consistent with elevated pore‑fluid pressure. These active‑source images complement passive‑source tomography by providing absolute velocity calibration and resolving fine‑scale features (fault splays, seamount subduction, slab tears) that are below the resolution of teleseismic body‑wave tomography.

Geophysical Constraints on Mantle Water Content from Joint Inversion of Electrical Conductivity and Seismic Velocity

Water incorporated as hydroxyl defects in nominally anhydrous minerals (olivine, wadsleyite, ringwoodite) dramatically enhances electrical conductivity while modestly reducing seismic velocities. Laboratory measurements at 1–3 GPa and 1000–1400 K show that 0.1 wt % H₂O in olivine increases bulk conductivity by 1–2 orders of magnitude (σ ≈ 10⁻² S m⁻¹) whereas VP and VS decrease by only 0.5–1 %. In the transition zone, ringwoodite can host up to 1.5 wt % H₂O, producing conductivities of 0.1–1 S m⁻¹. Magnetotelluric (MT) surveys across continents and oceans image high‑conductivity layers at 80–150 km depth (asthenosphere) and 410–660 km (transition zone). Simultaneously, seismic tomography reveals low‑velocity anomalies (ΔVS ≈ −2 % to −4 %) at comparable depths. Joint inversion exploits the different sensitivities: conductivity σ depends exponentially on water content Cw (σ ∝ exp[−Ea/RT] Cwr with r ≈ 1), while seismic velocity perturbations scale roughly linearly with Cw (ΔV/V ≈ −k Cw, k ≈ 0.02 wt %⁻¹). A Bayesian framework combines MT impedance tensors and surface‑wave dispersion curves, using a petrological forward model that predicts σ(T, Cw, XFe) and VS(T, Cw, XFe) for a given mineral assemblage. Applied to the western United States, the joint inversion yields Cw ≈ 0.05–0.1 wt % in the asthenosphere (80–150 km) and 0.2–0.4 wt % in the transition zone beneath the Yellowstone hotspot track, consistent with a hydrous plume source. In the central Andes, a high‑conductivity, low‑velocity anomaly at 100–200 km depth is best explained by 0.3 wt % water released from the subducting slab, triggering flux melting. The main trade‑off is temperature: a 100 K increase mimics the conductivity effect of ≈ 0.05 wt % water. Independent constraints from xenolith thermobarometry and heat‑flow data are therefore essential to break the temperature‑water trade‑off. The joint approach also identifies regions where high conductivity is not accompanied by low velocities (e.g., graphite‑bearing metasomatism), highlighting the need for multi‑observable interpretation.

Seismic Attenuation Tomography of the Upper Mantle Using Ambient Noise Cross‑Correlation and Coda Wave Interferometry

Ambient seismic noise, generated by ocean‑wave pressure fluctuations and atmospheric disturbances, provides a continuous source of surface‑wave energy that can be cross‑correlated between station pairs to retrieve empirical Green’s functions. The amplitude decay of the retrieved Rayleigh‑wave fundamental mode as a function of inter‑station distance yields the attenuation coefficient α(ω) = π / (Q · λ), where λ is wavelength. By measuring α at multiple periods (10–150 s) across a dense array (e.g., USArray, European AlpArray), a 2‑D Qμ map of the upper mantle is constructed. Coda wave interferometry (CWI) complements this by tracking minute velocity changes (Δv/v ≈ 10⁻⁴) and attenuation variations in the multiply scattered coda of regional earthquakes. The coda quality factor Qc is related to the intrinsic Qi and scattering Qs through the energy‑flux model: 1/Qc = 1/Qi + 1/Qs. In practice, Qc is measured from the exponential decay of the coda envelope in narrow frequency bands (0.5–2 Hz, 2–8 Hz). Combining ambient‑noise Q (sensitive to intrinsic attenuation at 0.01–0.1 Hz) with CWI Qc (0.5–8 Hz) constrains the frequency dependence of attenuation, often parameterized as Q = Q₀ (ω/ω₀)α. For the western US upper mantle, ambient‑noise tomography yields Qμ ≈ 80–120 at 20 s period, while CWI gives Qc ≈ 150–200 at 2 Hz, implying α ≈ 0.25. This frequency dependence matches laboratory anelasticity data for dislocation creep in olivine. The resulting 3‑D attenuation model reveals a low‑Q (high attenuation) corridor beneath the Basin and Range (Qμ ≈ 60) coincident with high heat flow and low Vs, and a high‑Q (low attenuation) cratonic root beneath the Canadian Shield (Qμ > 200). Joint inversion of ambient‑noise phase velocities, amplitudes, and CWI‑derived ΔQ/Q improves resolution of the attenuation structure by 30–40 % compared with phase‑velocity‑only tomography. A critical assumption is that the noise source distribution is azimuthally uniform; deviations introduce bias in amplitude measurements that can be mitigated by source‑distribution weighting or by using earthquake‑based spectral ratios as an independent check.

  • Assuming that shear‑wave splitting alone uniquely determines the depth of anisotropy — splitting integrates over the entire path; surface‑wave azimuthal anisotropy or receiver‑function analysis is needed for depth resolution.
  • Equating a low‑velocity anomaly in tomography with temperature only — compositional variations (water, melt, Fe‑enrichment) produce comparable velocity reductions.
  • Using a single VP/VS ratio to identify fluids — high ratios also arise from partial melt, serpentinization, or anisotropic cracking.
  • Neglecting the frequency dependence of Q when comparing ambient‑noise attenuation (0.01 Hz) with coda‑wave Q (1 Hz) — the two measure different parts of the anelastic spectrum.
  • Treating the slab top reflector in reflection profiles as the exact plate interface — it may correspond to a dehydration front or a shear zone a few kilometres above the true mechanical decoupling surface.
  • Assuming that joint inversion of conductivity and seismic velocity yields a unique water content — temperature, iron content, and minor phases (graphite, sulfides) create trade‑offs that require independent petrological constraints.
  • Interpreting a high‑conductivity layer as exclusively hydrous — interconnected melt, graphite films, or sulfide networks can produce similar conductivities without water.

Seismic Imaging of the Core‑Mantle Boundary Using PKP and PKIKP Phases

The core‑mantle boundary (CMB) is imaged most sharply by body‑wave phases that traverse the outer core and reflect or diffract at the CMB. The PKP family — PKPab, PKPbc, PKPdf — and the PKIKP phase (which penetrates the inner core) provide complementary constraints on CMB topography, ultra‑low velocity zones (ULVZs), and the velocity structure of the lowermost mantle. Differential travel‑time measurements between PKPab and PKPbc (the “PKPab‑PKPbc” differential) are sensitive to the shear‑wave velocity jump across the CMB because PKPab samples the lowermost mantle while PKPbc travels a longer path in the outer core. Global datasets from the International Seismological Centre (ISC) and the Global Seismographic Network (GSN) have been used to map CMB topography with a lateral resolution of ~200 km, revealing depressions of up to 5 km beneath subduction zones and uplifts of 2–3 km beneath large low‑shear‑velocity provinces (LLSVPs).

PKIKP travel‑time residuals, after correcting for mantle heterogeneity using a reference model such as PREM or S40RTS, isolate inner‑core anisotropy and the inner‑core boundary (ICB) radius. The observed PKIKP‑PKPbc differential times constrain the ICB radius to 1221 ± 0.5 km, consistent with normal‑mode measurements.
