Moreover, the polarity of PKIKP‑PKPbc differentials varies with azimuth, confirming a cylindrical anisotropy in the inner core with a fast axis aligned near the rotation axis. ULVZs are detected as localized precursors to PKPab (PKP‑precursor) with travel‑time advances of 0.5–2 s and amplitude enhancements of 10–30 %. Array stacking of broadband records from the USArray and the Japanese Hi‑net has imaged ULVZ patches 10–50 km thick and 50–200 km wide, often coincident with the edges of LLSVPs, suggesting a compositional origin (e.g., iron‑enriched post‑perovskite or partial melt).

Joint inversion of PKP differential times, PKIKP residuals, and normal‑mode splitting functions yields a self‑consistent model of CMB topography, ULVZ distribution, and inner‑core anisotropy. Recent studies (e.g., Cormier et al., 2020; Irving et al., 2022) demonstrate that incorporating finite‑frequency sensitivity kernels reduces smearing artifacts inherent in ray‑theoretical inversions, sharpening ULVZ boundaries by ~30 %. These high‑resolution CMB images are essential for testing geodynamic models of mantle‑core heat flux, core‑mantle coupling, and the generation of the geomagnetic field.

Geophysical Constraints on Secular Variation of the Geomagnetic Field from Satellite Observations

Satellite missions — Ørsted (1999–2014), CHAMP (2000–2010), Swarm (2013–present) — have provided continuous, global vector measurements of the geomagnetic field at altitudes of 300–500 km. The Swarm constellation, with three satellites in slightly different orbits, enables the separation of internal (core, lithospheric) and external (ionospheric, magnetospheric) sources through along‑track and cross‑track gradient analysis. Spherical‑harmonic models up to degree 13 (core field) and degree 90 (lithospheric field) are updated every six months, yielding a time series of Gauss coefficients gₙᵐ(t), hₙᵐ(t). Secular variation (SV) is obtained by differentiating these coefficients, revealing the westward drift of non‑dipolar flux patches at 0.2–0.3° yr⁻¹ and the acceleration of the dipole decay (~‑5 nT yr⁻¹).

Core‑flow inversions assume the frozen‑flux approximation (∂B/∂t = −∇ₕ·(vₕBᵣ)) and tangential geostrophy to retrieve large‑scale flow at the top of the outer core. Swarm‑derived SV models (e.g., CHAOS‑7, COV‑OBS.x2) resolve a prominent eccentric gyre beneath the Atlantic hemisphere and a strong azimuthal flow beneath the Pacific, consistent with numerical dynamo simulations that incorporate a heterogeneous CMB heat flux. The South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA) appears as a region of weakened radial field (≈ 22 µT at Earth’s surface) and enhanced SV, linked to a reverse‑flux patch at the CMB that has grown since the 1990s. Satellite data also capture geomagnetic jerks — abrupt changes in SV acceleration — in 2003, 2007, 2011, and 2014, which are interpreted as rapid core‑surface flow reorganizations possibly triggered by buoyancy release from the inner‑core boundary.

Combining satellite SV with ground‑observatory records (INTERMAGNET) extends the temporal baseline to > 150 yr, enabling the detection of a ~60‑yr oscillation in the dipole moment and a possible link to length‑of‑day variations through core‑mantle angular momentum exchange. The high‑precision Swarm gradients (≈ 0.1 nT km⁻¹) have also allowed the first direct mapping of the lithospheric field at wavelengths down to 250 km, constraining crustal magnetization and tectonic history. Ongoing mission extensions and the planned NanoMagSat constellation aim to improve temporal resolution to monthly intervals, opening the possibility of monitoring rapid core dynamics on sub‑annual timescales.

Worked Example: Estimating Core‑Flow Velocity from Swarm Secular Variation
Given a radial magnetic field Bᵣ = 30 µT at the CMB beneath the Atlantic and an observed secular variation ∂Bᵣ/∂t = 0.15 µT yr⁻¹ over a horizontal length scale L = 1500 km, estimate the characteristic horizontal flow speed vₕ using the frozen‑flux approximation.
The frozen‑flux equation in order‑of‑magnitude form is ∂Bᵣ/∂t ≈ vₕ Bᵣ / L. Rearranging gives vₕ ≈ (∂Bᵣ/∂t) · L / Bᵣ. Substituting the numbers: vₕ ≈ (0.15 µT yr⁻¹) × (1.5 × 10⁶ m) / (30 µT) = 7.5 × 10³ m yr⁻¹. Converting to mm yr⁻¹: 7.5 × 10³ m yr⁻¹ = 7.5 × 10⁶ mm yr⁻¹. However, typical core‑flow speeds are 10–20 km yr⁻¹ (10⁴–2 × 10⁴ m yr⁻¹). The discrepancy arises because the simple scaling neglects the divergence operator and the fact that Bᵣ varies spatially. A more realistic inversion using spherical‑harmonic SV up to degree 13 yields vₕ ≈ 15 km yr⁻¹ for the Atlantic gyre, consistent with published core‑flow models.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a quantitative estimate from secular variation, first identify the relevant approximation (frozen‑flux, tangential geostrophy, or magnetostrophic balance). Write the governing equation in symbolic form, list the given quantities with units, and perform a dimensional check before inserting numbers. Examiners often award partial credit for correct setup even if the arithmetic is off.

Mantle Convection Modulated by Phase Transitions at 410 and 660 km Depth

The olivine‑wadsleyite (α‑β) transition at ~410 km and the ringwoodite‑bridgmanite + ferropericlase (γ‑ε) transition at ~660 km impose sharp changes in density and seismic velocity that dynamically influence mantle convection. The Clapeyron slope of the 410 km transition is positive (~+2 MPa K⁻¹), so cold downwellings experience a buoyancy boost as the transition is depressed, accelerating slab penetration into the transition zone. Conversely, the 660 km transition has a negative Clapeyron slope (~‑2.5 MPa K⁻¹); cold slabs depress the transition to greater depth, increasing the density contrast and resisting further descent, which can cause slab stagnation or deflection. Hot upwellings elevate the 410 km discontinuity and depress the 660 km discontinuity, thinning the transition zone — a signature observed beneath hotspots such as Hawaii and Yellowstone.

Numerical models (e.g., Bercovici 1993; Tackley 1996) that incorporate these phase changes reproduce the observed bimodal slab behaviour: some slabs penetrate into the lower mantle (e.g., Tonga, Mariana) while others flatten in the transition zone (e.g., Izu‑Bonin, Japan). The transition‑zone thickness, measured from receiver‑function stacks (e.g., SS precursors, P‑to‑S conversions), varies globally from ~220 km (cold regions) to ~260 km (hot regions). This variation correlates with seismic‑tomography‑derived temperature anomalies of ±300 K. The phase transitions also affect the viscosity structure: the 660 km transition coincides with a viscosity increase of 1–2 orders of magnitude, creating a “viscosity jump” that can decouple upper‑ and lower‑mantle convection.

Recent joint inversions of seismic discontinuity topography, geoid anomalies, and plate motions constrain the effective Clapeyron slopes and the magnitude of the viscosity jump. For instance, a global model by Hager & Richards (1989) updated with modern tomography (S40RTS) suggests a 660 km Clapeyron slope of ‑2.8 MPa K⁻¹ and a viscosity contrast of ~30 across the transition. These parameters control the wavelength of dynamic topography: a stronger negative slope yields longer‑wavelength geoid highs over subduction zones. The phase transitions also modulate the heat flux across the CMB by regulating the frequency of slab avalanches into the lower mantle, thereby influencing the geodynamo’s long‑term power budget.

Seismic Anisotropy in the Upper Mantle and Its Correlation with Plate Motion

Azimuthal anisotropy in the upper mantle, manifested as shear‑wave splitting (SKS, SKKS) and surface‑wave phase‑velocity variations, provides a direct probe of lattice‑preferred orientation (LPO) of olivine induced by mantle flow. Global compilations of SKS splitting parameters (fast direction φ, delay time δt) from > 2000 stations show a strong correlation between φ and the absolute plate motion (APM) direction in oceanic regions (e.g., Pacific, Nazca, Cocos plates), with mean misfit < 15°. In continental interiors, however, φ often aligns with fossilized lithospheric fabric (e.g., Proterozoic orogen trends) rather than present‑day APM, indicating a layered anisotropy: a shallow lithospheric layer (50–150 km) preserving ancient deformation, and a deeper asthenospheric layer (150–300 km) aligned with current flow.

Surface‑wave azimuthal anisotropy (Love‑Rayleigh phase‑velocity differences) resolves depth dependence through period‑dependent sensitivity kernels. At periods of 50–100 s (sampling 100–200 km), fast directions match APM in oceanic basins; at 150–250 s (sampling 200–400 km), the signal weakens, suggesting a transition to more isotropic flow or a change in deformation regime. Finite‑frequency tomography (e.g., SEMUCB‑WM1, S40RTS) combined with splitting data reveals that the asthenospheric anisotropy amplitude (δt ≈ 0.8–1.2 s) corresponds to ~2–3 % shear‑wave anisotropy, consistent with laboratory measurements of olivine LPO at mantle strain rates (10⁻¹⁵–10⁻¹⁴ s⁻¹).

In subduction zones, trench‑parallel fast directions in the forearc mantle wedge are attributed to corner‑flow induced by slab rollback, while trench‑perpendicular directions beneath the back‑arc reflect return flow. The Ryukyu and Mariana arcs exemplify this pattern. In collisional belts (e.g., Himalaya‑Tibet), SKS fast directions rotate from N‑S in the Indian plate to E‑W across the suture, mapping the transition from plate‑driven to crustal‑scale deformation. Joint inversion of SKS splitting, surface‑wave azimuthal anisotropy, and geodetic strain rates (from GNSS) yields a self‑consistent 3‑D mantle‑flow model that satisfies both seismic and geodetic observations, providing a powerful test of plate‑driving force hypotheses.

Geophysical Constraints on Earth's Thermal Budget from Global Heat‑Flow Measurements

The global heat‑flow database (e.g., the International Heat Flow Commission catalogue, > 35 000 measurements) yields a mean continental heat flow of 65 mW m⁻² and a mean oceanic heat flow of 101 mW m⁻², giving a total surface heat loss of 47 ± 2 TW. After correcting for hydrothermal circulation in young oceanic crust (< 60 Myr), the conductive oceanic heat flow rises to ~ 110 mW m⁻², implying a mantle heat flux of ~ 30 TW. Radiogenic heat production in the crust and mantle, estimated from geochemical models (e.g., Bulk Silicate Earth with 20 ppb U, 80 ppb Th, 260 ppm K), contributes ~ 20 TW. The remaining ~ 10 TW must be supplied by secular cooling of the core and mantle, consistent with a Urey ratio (radiogenic/total heat production) of 0.3–0.4.

Heat‑flow measurements also constrain the thermal structure of the lithosphere. The half‑space cooling model predicts heat flow q ∝ t⁻¹ᐟ² for oceanic lithosphere; observed deviations at ages > 80 Myr (flattening to ~ 50 mW m⁻²) indicate a plate‑model thickness of ~ 125 km and a basal temperature of ~ 1350 °C. Continental heat‑flow provinces (e.g., Basin and Range, East African Rift) show elevated values (> 90 mW m⁻²) linked to lithospheric thinning and mantle upwelling. In cratons (e.g., Kaapvaal, Slave), low heat flow (~ 35 mW m⁻²) and thick lithospheric keels (> 200 km) imply a depleted, low‑radiogenic mantle root.

Joint inversion of heat‑flow, seismic tomography, and geoid data refines the mantle temperature field. For example, a 2021 study combined global heat‑flow with S40RTS shear‑velocity anomalies using a mineral‑physics‑based velocity‑temperature conversion (dlnVs/dT ≈ ‑0.5 % / 100 K). The resulting temperature model predicts a CMB heat flux of 12 ± 3 TW, with lateral variations of ± 30 % that correlate with LLSVP edges. This CMB flux is a key boundary condition for geodynamo simulations; values below ~ 8 TW struggle to sustain the observed magnetic field, while values above ~ 15 TW produce excessive secular variation. The heat‑flow budget thus links surface observations to deep‑Earth dynamics, closing the loop between plate tectonics, mantle convection, and the geomagnetic field.

  • Assuming that PKP differential times alone uniquely determine CMB topography — mantle heterogeneity and outer‑core structure also contribute; joint inversion with normal modes is required.
  • Equating satellite‑derived secular variation directly to core flow without accounting for the frozen‑flux approximation’s limitations (e.g., diffusion, magnetic diffusion time ~ 10⁴ yr).
  • Treating the 410 km and 660 km phase transitions as purely thermal boundaries — compositional layering (e.g., basaltic enrichment) can shift discontinuity depths independently of temperature.
  • Interpreting SKS fast directions as a single‑layer anisotropy — most regions exhibit depth‑dependent anisotropy requiring multi‑layer modeling.
  • Using a single global heat‑flow value to infer mantle radiogenic content — lateral variations in crustal heat production and hydrothermal circulation introduce > 30 % uncertainty.
  • Neglecting the effect of mantle viscosity jumps at phase transitions on dynamic topography and geoid predictions — the 660 km viscosity contrast can change geoid amplitudes by a factor of two.
  • Assuming that the South Atlantic Anomaly is a static feature — satellite data show it is expanding westward at ~ 0.3° yr⁻¹ and deepening, reflecting ongoing core‑surface flux evolution.

These advanced topics illustrate how multi‑observable geophysical datasets — seismic, magnetic, thermal, and geodetic — are integrated to resolve Earth’s deep structure and dynamics.

Radial Anisotropy in the Upper Mantle and Its Implications for Mantle Flow Geometry

Radial anisotropy — the difference between vertically and horizontally polarized shear‑wave speeds (VSV versus VSH) — provides a direct window on the geometry of mantle deformation. In the upper mantle, a positive radial anisotropy (VSH > VSV) is typically interpreted as the signature of horizontal shear associated with plate‑driven flow, whereas a negative anomaly (VSV > VSH) points to vertical upwelling or downwelling. Global surface‑wave tomography models such as S40RTS and SEMUCB‑WM1 resolve radial anisotropy down to ~250 km depth with lateral resolution of ~500 km. Beneath oceanic plates, the lithospheric lid shows VSH > VSV by 2–4 % consistent with fossilized spreading‑fabric, while the underlying asthenosphere often displays reduced or even negative radial anisotropy, reflecting a transition to more isotropic or vertically oriented strain. Continental regions exhibit more complex patterns: cratonic keels retain strong positive radial anisotropy to >200 km, whereas rift zones (e.g., East African Rift) show negative values linked to active upwelling. Joint inversion of surface‑wave phase velocities, body‑wave travel times, and SKS splitting allows the separation of azimuthal and radial anisotropy, revealing that the fast shear‑wave polarization direction (azimuthal) aligns with absolute plate motion in the lithosphere but rotates with depth in the asthenosphere, whereas radial anisotropy magnitude peaks at the lithosphere‑asthenosphere boundary (LAB). This depth‑dependent behaviour constrains the viscosity contrast across the LAB: a sharp increase in radial anisotropy at ~80–100 km depth implies a viscosity jump of at least an order of magnitude, supporting a rheologically distinct asthenospheric channel. Moreover, radial anisotropy in the transition zone (410–660 km) is generally weak (<1 %), but localized positive anomalies beneath subducting slabs suggest slab‑induced mantle flow that is predominantly horizontal. These observations, combined with mineral‑physics predictions for olivine and wadsleyite lattice‑preferred orientation, enable quantitative estimates of mantle strain‑rate fields and provide a critical test for geodynamic models of plate‑mantle coupling.

Seismic Imaging of the Mantle Transition Zone Using SS Precursors and Receiver Functions

The mantle transition zone (MTZ), bounded by the 410 km and 660 km discontinuities, is a key region for tracking slab penetration, plume ascent, and water storage. Two complementary seismic techniques — SS precursors and teleseismic receiver functions — have become standard tools for mapping MTZ topography at global and regional scales. SS precursors are underside reflections (S410S, S660S) that arrive before the main SS phase; their differential travel times (ΔtS660S‑S410S) are directly proportional to MTZ thickness. Because the SS ray path samples a broad Fresnel zone (~1000 km), precursor stacking over many events yields a laterally smoothed but robust estimate of average MTZ thickness beneath large‑scale features such as the Pacific LLSVP or the African superswell. Receiver functions, by contrast, exploit P‑to‑S conversions at the 410 and 660 km interfaces recorded at a single station; the move‑out of the Ps phases with epicentral distance provides high‑resolution (~50 km) depth estimates but is limited to the station footprint. Joint inversion of SS precursor stacks and dense receiver‑function arrays (e.g., USArray, European AlpArray) reconciles the two scales, revealing that the global mean MTZ thickness is 250 ± 5 km, with regional deviations of up to ±30 km. Thickened MTZ (>260 km) beneath the western Pacific correlates with stagnant slabs imaged by tomography, while thinned MTZ (<240 km) beneath the central Pacific and the Afar plume indicates elevated temperatures. The Clapeyron slopes of the olivine‑wadsleyite (410 km) and ringwoodite‑bridgmanite + ferropericlase (660 km) transitions have opposite signs; therefore a purely thermal anomaly moves the two boundaries in opposite directions, changing MTZ thickness. Observed correlated topography (both boundaries depressed or elevated together) implies a significant compositional component, such as basaltic enrichment or water‑induced phase‑boundary shifts. Recent studies using finite‑frequency sensitivity kernels for SS precursors demonstrate that the 410 km discontinuity can be depressed by up to 20 km beneath the Tonga slab, while the 660 km is simultaneously uplifted by ~15 km, yielding a net thinning of ~35 km — a signature of cold, hydrated slab material. These combined seismic constraints feed directly into mantle convection models that predict slab stagnation depths and the flux of water into the lower mantle.

Worked Example: Estimating MTZ Thickness from SS Precursor Differential Times
A stacked SS precursor record beneath a Pacific station shows S410S arriving 12.3 s after the direct S arrival and S660S arriving 24.8 s after the direct S. Assuming a 1‑D reference model (PREM) with VS = 4.5 km s⁻¹ at 410 km and 5.0 km s⁻¹ at 660 km, compute the MTZ thickness anomaly relative to the PREM value of 250 km.
In PREM the differential time Δtref = t(S660S) − t(S410S) ≈ (2 × 660 / 5.0) − (2 × 410 / 4.5) ≈ 264 − 182 = 82 s. The observed Δtobs = 24.8 − 12.3 = 12.5 s. The difference Δtobs − Δtref = −69.5 s indicates a much thinner MTZ. Using the approximate sensitivity ∂Δt/∂h ≈ 2 / VS,avg ≈ 2 / 4.75 ≈ 0.42 s km⁻¹, the thickness anomaly Δh ≈ −69.5 / 0.42 ≈ −165 km. Adding to the reference 250 km gives an estimated MTZ thickness of ~85 km, which is unrealistically thin; the discrepancy signals that the simple 1‑D velocity assumption is inadequate and that finite‑frequency kernels or a full waveform inversion are required for quantitative work.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for MTZ thickness from SS precursors, remember the two‑step logic: (1) compute the differential travel time from the given arrivals, (2) convert to thickness using the average shear‑wave speed in the transition zone (≈ 4.7 km s⁻¹). If only receiver‑function Ps‑moveout data are given, use the H‑κ stacking formula h = (tPs − tP) · VP / (√(1 − (VP/VS)² sin²i)). Practice both conversions; examiners often mix the two methods in a single problem.

Lateral Viscosity Variations Inferred from Glacial Isostatic Adjustment and Sea‑Level Fingerprints

Glacial isostatic adjustment (GIA) — the delayed viscoelastic response of the solid Earth to past ice‑sheet loading — provides the most direct observational constraint on mantle viscosity structure at wavelengths of 1000–5000 km. Modern GIA analyses combine three independent data sets: (1) relative sea‑level (RSL) histories from raised beaches and coral terraces, (2) present‑day vertical land motion from GNSS and tide‑gauge records, and (3) time‑variable gravity from the GRACE and GRACE‑FO missions. Forward modelling solves the sea‑level equation for a spherically symmetric, layered Earth model; the resulting predictions are compared to observations in a Bayesian framework that yields posterior probability distributions for the viscosity profile. Classic 1‑D inversions (e.g., ICE‑6G, VM5a) converge on an upper‑mantle viscosity of 0.3–0.5 × 10²¹ Pa s, a transition‑zone viscosity of 1–3 × 10²¹ Pa s, and a lower‑mantle viscosity of 10–50 × 10²¹ Pa s. However, lateral viscosity variations are required to explain regional misfits: the rapid uplift in Hudson Bay (≈ 10 mm yr⁻¹) versus the slower rebound in Fennoscandia (≈ 5 mm yr⁻¹) cannot be reproduced with a purely radial model. 3‑D GIA codes (e.g., SELEN, CitcomS‑GIA) incorporate laterally varying lithospheric thickness and mantle viscosity derived from seismic tomography (Vs anomalies converted to temperature and then to viscosity via an Arrhenius law). These models show that a low‑viscosity asthenospheric channel beneath the former Laurentide ice sheet (η ≈ 10²⁰ Pa s) accelerates local uplift, while a high‑viscosity cratonic root beneath the Canadian Shield (η > 10²² Pa s) damps the signal. Sea‑level fingerprints — the distinct spatial pattern of sea‑level change caused by melting of a specific ice sheet — further constrain viscosity because the fingerprint amplitude depends on the Earth’s deformational response. For example, the observed far‑field sea‑level fall in the equatorial Pacific during the Last Glacial Maximum requires a lower‑mantle viscosity ≥ 30 × 10²¹ Pa s; lower values would produce excessive ocean‑basin deformation inconsistent with coral‑derived RSL curves. Joint inversion of GIA and mantle convection simulations (e.g., using the adjoint method) now yields self‑consistent 3‑D viscosity fields that honor both the glacial rebound record and the present‑day geoid, providing a crucial link between surface processes and deep‑mantle rheology.

Electromagnetic Induction Constraints on Outer‑Core Stratification and Compositional Convection

Time‑varying geomagnetic fields induce electric currents in the electrically conducting outer core; the resulting secondary magnetic field is measured at the Earth’s surface and by low‑Earth‑orbit satellites (Swarm, CHAMP, Ørsted). Electromagnetic (EM) induction studies exploit the frequency dependence of the skin depth δ = √(2 / μ₀σω) to probe conductivity σ at different depths. Diurnal and semi‑diurnal variations (periods ~12–24 h) sample the uppermost outer core (skin depth ~200 km), while secular variation (periods > 10 yr) penetrates the entire outer core. Global EM inversions using spherical harmonic representations of the inducing and induced fields have resolved a radially varying conductivity profile that increases from ~0.5 × 10⁶ S m⁻¹ at the core‑mantle boundary (CMB) to ~1.2 × 10⁶ S m⁻¹ near the inner‑core boundary (ICB). A key finding is a modest but statistically significant conductivity increase of ~5 % in the lowermost 200 km of the outer core (the so‑called F‑layer). This high‑conductivity layer is consistent with a stratified region enriched in light elements (O, S, Si) that have been rejected by the solidifying inner core and accumulate at the top of the outer core. Such compositional stratification would suppress radial convection locally, altering the flow geometry that sustains the geodynamo. Numerical dynamo models that impose a stably stratified layer of thickness 100–300 km reproduce several observed features: reduced secular variation power at high spherical harmonic degrees, a preference for columnar convection aligned with the rotation axis in the bulk of the outer core, and the observed westward drift of magnetic flux patches. However, the same models predict a weaker dipole moment unless the buoyancy flux from inner‑core growth is increased. Independent constraints from seismic PKIKP travel‑time anomalies (which suggest a low‑velocity, possibly compositionally distinct layer at the top of the outer core) and from mineral‑physics calculations of Fe‑light‑element alloy conductivity at core pressures and temperatures support the EM inference. The combined geophysical picture therefore points to a dynamically active, but partially stratified, outer core where compositional convection driven by inner‑core solidification coexists with a thin, stably stratified layer that modulates the magnetic field morphology.

Seismic Attenuation Tomography of the Lower Mantle and Its Thermal Interpretation

Seismic attenuation, quantified by the quality factor Q (where amplitude decays as exp(−πft/Q)), is sensitive to temperature, partial melt, and grain‑size‑dependent anelastic mechanisms. While upper‑mantle Q models are relatively mature, lower‑mantle attenuation tomography has only recently become feasible thanks to the accumulation of broadband body‑wave spectra (S, ScS, Sdiff) and the development of spectral‑ratio and waveform‑fitting techniques that correct for source and receiver effects. Global QS models (e.g., QM1, QL6) reveal a pronounced low‑Q belt (QS ≈ 200–300) encircling the Pacific LLSVP at depths 1500–2500 km, contrasting with higher Q (≈ 600–800) beneath the Atlantic and Indian Oceans. The low‑Q belt correlates with negative shear‑velocity anomalies (δVS ≈ −1 % to −2 %) and elevated temperatures inferred from mineral‑physics velocity‑temperature derivatives (∂lnVS/∂T ≈ −0.5 % / 100 K). Using the laboratory‑derived frequency‑dependent anelastic model of Jackson & Faul (2010), the observed QS values translate to temperature excesses of 200–300 K relative to an adiabatic reference, assuming a grain size of 1–5 mm and a water content < 100 ppm. However, the trade‑off between temperature and water (or melt) is severe: a modest water increase to 200 ppm can lower Q by a factor of two without any temperature change. Joint inversion of QS, VS, and electrical conductivity (from magnetotelluric and satellite induction studies) helps break this trade‑off because conductivity is far more sensitive to water than Q. Recent joint inversions suggest that the Pacific low‑Q belt is primarily thermal (ΔT ≈ 250 K) with only minor water enhancement, whereas the African LLSVP shows moderately low Q (≈ 400) but high conductivity, implying a chemically distinct, possibly iron‑enriched composition rather than pure temperature. At the very base of the mantle (D″), QS drops to < 150 in localized patches beneath the Pacific and Africa, coincident with ultra‑low velocity zones (ULVZs). These extreme attenuation values are best explained by partial melt fractions of 1–3 % or by post‑perovskite phase‑transition induced anelasticity. The emerging 3‑D QS structure thus provides an independent thermal and compositional constraint that complements seismic velocity tomography and is essential for calibrating mantle convection models that predict the heat flux across the CMB and the longevity of the geodynamo.

  • Confusing radial anisotropy (VSH vs VSV) with azimuthal anisotropy (fast‑direction variations) — they diagnose different deformation geometries.
  • Assuming SS precursor differential times alone give MTZ thickness without correcting for 3‑D velocity structure and finite‑frequency effects.
  • Treating GIA‑derived viscosity as a single radial profile when lateral variations of an order of magnitude are required to fit regional uplift rates.
  • Equating a high‑conductivity layer at the top of the outer core solely with temperature; compositional stratification from light‑element accumulation is the leading explanation.
  • Interpreting low Q in the lower mantle as exclusively thermal; water, melt, and grain‑size effects produce comparable attenuation signatures.
  • Neglecting the frequency dependence of Q when comparing body‑wave (1 Hz) and normal‑mode (mHz) attenuation measurements.
  • Using a single global heat‑flow value to infer mantle radiogenic heat production; crustal heterogeneity and hydrothermal circulation introduce > 30 % uncertainty.
The integration of seismic anisotropy, transition‑zone imaging, glacial isostatic adjustment, electromagnetic induction, and attenuation tomography creates a multi‑observable framework that resolves Earth’s deep structure and dynamics with unprecedented fidelity.

Seismic Imaging of the Inner Core Using PKIKP and PKiKP Differential Travel Times

The solid inner core, radius 1221 km, is sampled by two complementary body‑wave phases: PKIKP, which traverses the inner core as a compressional wave, and PKiKP, which reflects off the inner‑core boundary (ICB). Differential travel‑time measurements (PKIKP − PKiKP) isolate the inner‑core segment because the mantle and outer‑core portions cancel to first order. Early global studies (Dziewonski & Gilbert 1976; Creager 1992) established a mean inner‑core P‑wave velocity of 11.26 km s⁻¹ and a modest anisotropy of 2–3 % with the fast axis aligned within 10° of the rotation axis. Later array‑stacking of broadband records from the Global Seismographic Network (GSN) and the International Monitoring System (IMS) refined the anisotropy to 2.5 % ± 0.3 % and revealed a hemispherical asymmetry: the eastern hemisphere (0–180°E) exhibits velocities ~0.5 % higher than the western hemisphere (Song & Helmberger 1998; Wen & Niu 2002). Differential rotation of the inner core relative to the mantle has been inferred from systematic temporal changes in PKIKP travel times; estimates range from 0.1° yr⁻¹ to 0.3° yr⁻¹ eastward (Song & Richards 1996; Zhang et al. 2005), though more recent analyses using cross‑correlation of waveform doublets suggest a slower rate near 0.05° yr⁻¹ (Tkalčić et al. 2013). Key challenges include uneven ray coverage (most paths sample the Pacific sector), mantle heterogeneity that leaks into the differential times, and the trade‑off between anisotropy and ICB topography. Joint inversions that incorporate normal‑mode splitting functions (e.g., ⁰S₂, ⁰T₂) and mineral‑physics constraints on hcp‑Fe elasticity at 330–360 GPa now provide a self‑consistent picture: a predominantly axially symmetric anisotropic inner core with a thin (~100 km) isotropic “inner‑inner core” and a laterally varying hemispherical velocity pattern that may reflect asymmetric solidification driven by outer‑core convection.

Seismic Anisotropy in the Lower Mantle and Its Implications for Mantle Flow

Lower‑mantle anisotropy is diagnosed primarily from shear‑wave splitting of ScS, Sdiff, and the splitting of free‑oscillation overtones (e.g., ⁰S₂, ⁰S₃). Global shear‑wave splitting surveys (Kendall & Silver 1996; Montagner & Kennett 1996) report fast‑direction patterns that correlate with the large low‑shear‑velocity provinces (LLSVPs) beneath the Pacific and Africa. Tomographic models such as SEMUCB‑WM1 and S40RTS resolve a radially anisotropic signature (VSH > VSV) of 1–2 % in the lower 800 km, with azimuthal anisotropy confined to the vicinity of subducted slabs and plume conduits. Mineral‑physics experiments on MgSiO₃ bridgmanite and post‑perovskite at 120–135 GPa demonstrate that dislocation creep on the (010)[100] slip system produces VSH > VSV, while post‑perovskite alignment in the D″ layer can generate strong azimuthal anisotropy with fast axes parallel to the flow direction. The observed correlation of fast axes with the peripheral margins of the Pacific LLSVP suggests a toroidal flow component around the upwelling, whereas the African LLSVP shows a more complex pattern consistent with a combination of upwelling and lateral spreading. Radial anisotropy in the lower mantle also influences the interpretation of geoid anomalies: a 1 % VSH > VSV layer of 500 km thickness can produce a geoid signal of ~2 m, comparable to that from density heterogeneity. Remaining ambiguities include the trade‑off between isotropic velocity reductions (thermal or compositional) and anisotropic signatures, and the limited resolution of azimuthal anisotropy below 1500 km depth where ray coverage is sparse. Future improvements will come from dense ocean‑bottom seismometer arrays and the incorporation of higher‑mode normal‑mode splitting into joint inversions.

Interpreting Shear‑Wave Splitting Measurements
A broadband station records a split S wave with a fast polarization direction of N30°E and a delay time of 1.2 s for a ray path that traverses the lower mantle beneath the Pacific LLSVP. Assuming a uniform anisotropy of 1.5 % and a path length of 2000 km through the anisotropic layer, estimate the expected delay time and comment on the consistency with the observation.
The shear‑wave splitting delay Δt for a uniform anisotropic layer is Δt = (δV/V) × (L / Vₛ), where δV/V = 0.015, L = 2000 km, and a representative lower‑mantle shear velocity Vₛ ≈ 5.0 km s⁻¹. Substituting gives Δt = 0.015 × (2000 / 5.0) = 0.015 × 400 = 6.0 s. The observed delay of 1.2 s is substantially smaller, indicating that either the anisotropic layer is thinner than 2000 km, the anisotropy magnitude is lower than 1.5 %, or the ray samples a mixture of anisotropic and isotropic material. In practice, the effective anisotropic thickness beneath the Pacific LLSVP is estimated at 300–500 km, which yields delays of 0.9–1.5 s, consistent with the measurement.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to estimate shear‑wave splitting delay, first identify the anisotropic layer thickness and the relevant shear‑wave speed; then apply Δt = (δV/V) × (L / Vₛ). Remember that observed delays are often reduced by averaging over multiple paths and by the presence of isotropic overburden.

Geophysical Constraints on the Earth's Inner Core Growth Rate from Seismic and Geochemical Data

Inner‑core growth is driven by the solidification of the liquid outer core at the ICB, releasing latent heat and light elements (O, S, Si, C) that power compositional convection. Seismologically, the growth rate is inferred from temporal changes in PKIKP travel times and from the observed increase in inner‑core radius. Early analyses of repeated earthquake doublets (Song & Richards 1996) reported a radius increase of ~0.5 mm yr⁻¹, corresponding to a solidification rate of ~0.3 mm yr⁻¹ after correcting for inner‑core rotation. More recent waveform‑cross‑correlation studies using the Global Seismographic Network (Tkalčić et al. 2013; Cao et al. 2020) find a slower apparent growth of 0.1–0.2 mm yr⁻¹, highlighting the sensitivity to mantle heterogeneity and inner‑core differential rotation. Geochemical constraints come from the paleomagnetic record: the virtual axial dipole moment (VADM) has been relatively stable over the past 3 Ga, implying a sustained geodynamo power of ~1–2 TW. Thermal evolution models that couple core cooling, radiogenic heating (⁴⁰K, ²³⁸U, ²³²Th), and the partitioning of light elements at the ICB (Labrosse 2015; Nimmo 2020) predict an inner‑core age of 0.5–1.5 Ga and a present‑day growth rate of 0.3–0.5 mm yr⁻¹. The discrepancy between seismic and thermal estimates may be reconciled if a significant fraction of the light‑element budget is sequestered in a stably stratified layer at the top of the outer core (see the section on outer‑core stratification), reducing the buoyancy flux available for inner‑core growth. Joint inversions that simultaneously fit PKIKP travel‑time trends, normal‑mode frequencies, and the paleointensity curve are now being used to narrow the permissible growth‑rate window to 0.25 ± 0.05 mm yr⁻¹.

Seismic Imaging of the Mantle Transition Zone Using ScS Reverberations

The mantle transition zone (MTZ), bounded by the 410 km and 660 km discontinuities, is efficiently sampled by ScS reverberations (ScS, ScS₂, ScS₃) that bounce between the core‑mantle boundary and the free surface. Each reverberation converts at the discontinuities, producing precursory arrivals whose differential travel times (e.g., ScS₂ − ScS) are sensitive to the depth and sharpness of the 410 and 660 km boundaries. Early work by Shearer (1991) and Flanagan & Shearer (1998) used global ScS stacks to map MTZ thickness variations of ±10 km, revealing a thinned transition zone beneath the western Pacific (≈ 230 km) and a thickened zone beneath the central Pacific (≈ 260 km). Modern array processing of USArray and European broadband data (e.g., Tauzin et al. 2018) achieves lateral resolution of ~200 km and confirms that the 410 km discontinuity topography correlates with subduction‑zone thermal anomalies (cold slabs depress the 410 by up to 30 km), while the 660 km discontinuity is elevated by up to 20 km in the same regions, consistent with a negative Clapeyron slope for the ringwoodite‑to‑bridgmanite + ferropericlase transition. The amplitude of the ScS reverberations also constrains the impedance contrast, which in turn limits the water content of the transition zone: a 1 % reduction in shear impedance at the 410 corresponds to ~0.2 wt % H₂O in wadsleyite. Joint inversions of ScS reverberation travel times, SS precursors, and receiver‑function move‑out now produce 3‑D models of MTZ thickness and discontinuity sharpness that are essential for quantifying the deep water cycle and for testing mantle convection models that predict slab stagnation at 660 km.

Geophysical Constraints on the Earth's Magnetic Field Secular Variation from Archaeomagnetic and Satellite Data

Secular variation (SV) of the geomagnetic field on decadal to millennial timescales is documented by two complementary archives: archaeomagnetic measurements from fired ceramics, lava flows, and lake sediments (covering the last ~10 kyr) and continuous satellite vector measurements from Ørsted (1999–2006), CHAMP (2000–2010), and the Swarm trio (2013–present). Archaeomagnetic data provide declination, inclination, and absolute intensity at specific sites, but suffer from dating uncertainties (±30–100 yr) and non‑uniqueness in the inversion for the axial dipole moment. Satellite data deliver global spherical‑harmonic models up to degree 13–15 with monthly resolution, enabling the detection of geomagnetic jerks (abrupt changes in secular acceleration) in 1969, 1978, 1991, 2003, and 2016. Combined field models such as CALS10k.2 (Korte et al. 2011) and gufm1 (Jackson et al. 2000) integrate both archives to produce a continuous SV record from 10 kyr BP to the present. The long‑term SV shows a westward drift of non‑dipolar flux patches at ~0.2° yr⁻¹ and a dipole moment decay of ~5 % per century since 1840. Core‑flow inversions based on the frozen‑flux approximation (Holme 2015) reveal a dominant large‑scale gyre beneath the Atlantic and a time‑varying eccentric jet under the Pacific that correlates with the occurrence of jerks. Outstanding issues include the non‑uniqueness of archaeomagnetic inclination in constraining the axial dipole, the limited spatial coverage of pre‑instrumental data, and the difficulty of separating external (magnetospheric) fields from internal SV in satellite records during geomagnetic storms. Future progress hinges on expanding the archaeomagnetic database in the Southern Hemisphere and on the next‑generation satellite mission (e.g., NanoMagSat) that will improve spatial resolution to degree 20.

  • Confusing PKiKP (reflection) with PKIKP (transmission) when interpreting inner‑core differential times.
  • Assuming lower‑mantle anisotropy is purely azimuthal; radial anisotropy (VSH vs VSV) dominates in many tomographic models.
  • Equating a single inner‑core growth rate from seismic travel‑time trends with the geochemical age; thermal history models allow a factor of two range.
  • Treating ScS reverberation move‑out as a direct measure of discontinuity depth without correcting for 3‑D mantle velocity structure.
  • Ignoring the non‑uniqueness of archaeomagnetic inclination data when reconstructing the axial dipole moment.
  • Over‑interpreting a single geomagnetic jerk as a change in core‑surface flow without corroborating satellite‑derived secular acceleration.
  • Neglecting the frequency dependence of shear‑wave splitting delay when comparing body‑wave (1 Hz) and normal‑mode (mHz) measurements.

Seismic Imaging of the Lower Mantle Using ScS and SS Precursors

ScS and SS precursors provide complementary windows on the lower mantle because they sample different ray paths and conversion points. ScS reflects off the core‑mantle boundary (CMB) and its precursory arrivals (ScS‑to‑ScS conversions at the 660 km discontinuity and at the 410 km discontinuity) are sensitive to the topography and sharpness of these phase boundaries. SS, a surface‑reflected shear wave, generates precursors (SS‑to‑SS conversions) that bounce off the 660 km and 410 km discontinuities from above, offering a near‑vertical incidence geometry that reduces trade‑offs between velocity structure and discontinuity depth. Early global stacks by Shearer (1991) and Flanagan & Shearer (1998) used ScS reverberations to map transition‑zone thickness variations of ±10 km. Later, Ritsema et al. (1999) combined ScS and SS precursor travel‑time residuals in a joint inversion, revealing a laterally heterogeneous 660 km topography with amplitudes up to 30 km beneath subduction zones and a relatively flat 410 km beneath stable continents. The differential travel time between the SS precursor and the main SS phase (Δt = t(SS‑precursor) − t(SS)) is directly proportional to the depth of the conversion point, allowing depth estimates with an uncertainty of ±5 km when 3‑D mantle corrections are applied using a reference model such as PREM or S40RTS.

Amplitude ratios of the precursors to the primary phases constrain the impedance contrast across the discontinuities. A 1 % reduction in shear impedance at the 660 km boundary corresponds to a temperature anomaly of roughly 200 K or a water content of ~0.1 wt % in ringwoodite, based on mineral‑physics calibrations (Karato 1993). Joint inversions of ScS and SS precursor travel times, amplitudes, and waveform shapes have been used to produce 3‑D models of lower‑mantle discontinuity topography (e.g., Tauzin et al. 2018; Cottaar & Lekic 2016). These models show that the 660 km discontinuity is depressed beneath the western Pacific by up to 30 km, consistent with cold slab penetration, while it is elevated beneath the African superswell by ~15 km, indicating a thermal upwelling. The 410 km discontinuity exhibits a weaker but correlated topography, supporting a negative Clapeyron slope for the olivine‑wadsleyite transition. The combined ScS‑SS dataset also resolves fine‑scale scattering near the CMB, revealing ultra‑low velocity zones (ULVZs) with shear‑velocity reductions of 10‑30 % and thicknesses of 5‑40 km (Garnero et al. 2007).

Methodologically, array processing of broadband networks (USArray, European stations) enhances signal‑to‑noise ratios by stacking hundreds of events. Frequency‑domain deconvolution isolates the precursor wavelets, and Bayesian inference quantifies uncertainties in discontinuity depth and sharpness. The resulting models are essential for testing mantle convection simulations that predict slab stagnation at 660 km and for quantifying the deep water cycle through the transition zone.

Geophysical Constraints on the Earth's Inner Core Anisotropy from Normal‑Mode Seismology

Normal‑mode seismology exploits the free oscillations of the whole Earth (spheroidal and toroidal modes) to infer inner‑core anisotropy with a global averaging kernel that is insensitive to source‑receiver geometry. The splitting of degenerate multiplets (e.g., the 0S₂, 0S₃, and 0T₂ modes) provides direct measurements of the elastic anisotropy parameters ε and δ in the inner core. Early work by Dziewonski & Gilbert (1973) identified a 0.3 % difference between the polar and equatorial shear velocities (VSH > VSV), implying a hexagonal symmetry axis aligned with the rotation axis. Subsequent analyses by Masters & Gubbins (2003) and Deuss et al. (2010) refined the anisotropy to 0.5‑0.8 % for VSH‑VSV and detected a weak azimuthal anisotropy (VSH varying with longitude) of ~0.1 % that correlates with the hemispherical seismic velocity asymmetry (eastern hemisphere faster). The normal‑mode splitting functions are inverted using a Bayesian framework that incorporates prior constraints from mineral physics (e.g., hcp‑Fe elasticity at core conditions from ab‑initio calculations by Vocadlo et al. 2009). The posterior distribution yields a preferred inner‑core shear‑wave anisotropy of 0.7 ± 0.2 % with a fast axis within 10° of the rotation axis.

Normal‑mode data also constrain the inner‑core attenuation (Qμ) and its radial variation. The observed quality factors for the 0S₂ and 0S₃ modes imply Qμ ≈ 600‑800 in the upper 200 km of the inner core, increasing to >1500 at the centre, consistent with a temperature gradient of ~100 K per 100 km (Cao et al. 2005). The combination of anisotropy and attenuation profiles supports a model of inner‑core growth by directional solidification, where crystal alignment is driven by the Earth's rotation and the resulting texture produces the observed hexagonal symmetry. Importantly, normal‑mode measurements are independent of the PKIKP/PKiKP differential travel‑time data, providing a cross‑validation that reduces the non‑uniqueness inherent in body‑wave inversions. Recent joint inversions of normal‑mode splitting, PKIKP travel times, and inner‑core sensitive body‑wave phases (e.g., PKiKP‑PKIKP) have produced a self‑consistent 3‑D model of inner‑core anisotropy, attenuation, and hemispherical velocity structure (Irving et al. 2018).

Worked Example: Estimating Inner‑Core Anisotropy from 0S₂ Splitting
A set of 0S₂ multiplet frequencies shows a splitting pattern with a maximum frequency separation of 0.12 µHz between the m = ±2 components. Assuming a reference Earth model with a spherically symmetric inner core, calculate the fractional shear‑wave anisotropy (ΔVSH/VSH) that would produce this splitting. Use the first‑order perturbation formula Δω/ω = (1/2) · (ΔVSH/VSH) · K, where K ≈ 0.8 for the 0S₂ mode.
The observed fractional frequency shift is Δω/ω = 0.12 µHz / (0S₂ frequency ≈ 309 µHz) ≈ 3.88 × 10⁻⁴. Rearranging the perturbation formula gives ΔVSH/VSH = 2 · (Δω/ω) / K = 2 · 3.88 × 10⁻⁴ / 0.8 ≈ 9.7 × 10⁻⁴, i.e. 0.097 %. This value is smaller than the canonical 0.5‑0.8 % because the example uses only the m = ±2 splitting; the full multiplet fit including all m components yields the larger anisotropy. The calculation demonstrates how normal‑mode splitting amplitudes translate directly into elastic anisotropy estimates.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the method that provides a global average of inner‑core anisotropy without source‑receiver bias, answer normal‑mode splitting. Remember the key numbers: 0S₂ and 0S₃ modes, splitting of order 10⁻⁴ in frequency, and the resulting VSH‑VSV anisotropy of ~0.5‑0.8 %. Pair this with the fact that normal‑mode Qμ values (600‑1500) constrain inner‑core temperature gradients.

Seismic Wave Propagation in Viscoelastic Media: Frequency‑Dependent Attenuation and Dispersion

Viscoelasticity introduces a complex, frequency‑dependent modulus M(ω) = M′(ω) + iM″(ω) that governs both wave speed and attenuation. In the standard linear solid (SLS) model, the quality factor Q(ω) varies as Q⁻¹(ω) = (ΔM/M) · (ωτ)/(1 + ω²τ²), where ΔM/M is the relaxation strength and τ the relaxation time. For mantle minerals, laboratory measurements (Karato 1993; Jackson et al. 2002) show that Qμ⁻¹ peaks near the seismic band (0.01‑1 Hz) for temperatures within 200 K of the solidus, producing a strong frequency dependence of shear‑wave velocity (dV/dlog ω ≈ 0.5‑1 % per decade). This dispersion must be accounted for when comparing body‑wave (1 Hz) and normal‑mode (mHz) observations; otherwise, apparent velocity anomalies can be misinterpreted as temperature or composition variations. The Kolsky‑Futterman constant‑Q model approximates Q as frequency‑independent over a limited band, but it violates causality at very low or high frequencies. A more rigorous approach uses the Kramers‑Kronig relations to enforce causality, yielding a phase velocity v(ω) = v₀ [1 + (1/πQ) ln(ω/ω₀)] for a reference frequency ω₀.

In global tomography, the frequency dependence of attenuation is exploited to separate thermal from compositional effects. For example, the shear‑wave velocity reduction in the low‑velocity zone (LVZ) at 100‑200 km depth is ~4 % at 1 Hz but only ~2 % at 0.01 Hz, implying a Qμ of ~80 in the LVZ (Romanowicz & Mitchell 2007). Joint inversion of surface‑wave phase velocities (periods 20‑250 s) and body‑wave travel times (1‑2 Hz) with a viscoelastic forward model (e.g., the spectral‑element method with anelasticity) yields a self‑consistent 3‑D model of Vs, Qμ, and their frequency derivatives. Such models reveal that the asthenosphere has a broad relaxation spectrum (τ ≈ 10⁻²‑10² s) consistent with grain‑boundary sliding and dislocation creep, while the lower mantle exhibits a narrower spectrum (τ ≈ 10⁻¹‑10¹ s) indicative of diffusion creep. The frequency‑dependent attenuation also influences the amplitude of seismic phases used for CMB studies; PKP and PKIKP amplitudes corrected for anelastic attenuation using a 3‑D Q model improve the resolution of inner‑core anisotropy by ~15 % (Cao et al. 2005).

Practical implementation in inversion codes often adopts a set of discrete relaxation mechanisms (e.g., 5‑10 SLS elements) to approximate the continuous spectrum. The parameters (ΔM/M, τ) are either fixed from laboratory data or inverted jointly with Vs. Regularization must penalize unrealistic oscillations in Q(ω) while allowing the data‑driven frequency dependence. Recent adjoint‑based full‑waveform inversion (FWI) frameworks incorporate viscoelastic kernels, enabling the simultaneous update of elastic and anelastic parameters from broadband waveforms (Fichtner et al. 2009). This advancement reduces the trade‑off between velocity and attenuation that plagued earlier travel‑time tomography.

Geophysical Constraints on the Earth's Magnetic Field from Paleomagnetic Secular Variation and Geodynamo Simulations

Paleomagnetic records of secular variation (SV) extend the observational window from the satellite era (≈40 yr) back to the Holocene (≈10 kyr) and, with lower resolution, to the Brunhes chron (≈0.78 Myr). Archaeomagnetic data from fired ceramics, lava flows, and lake sediments provide declination, inclination, and absolute intensity at specific sites, but suffer from dating uncertainties of ±30‑100 yr and non‑uniqueness in the inversion for the axial dipole moment (ADM). Global field models such as CALS10k.2 (Korte et al. 2011) and the updated SHA.DIF.14k (Pavón‑Carrasco et al. 2014) combine archaeomagnetic and volcanic data with a regularized spherical‑harmonic expansion up to degree 10, producing a continuous SV record that shows a westward drift of non‑dipolar flux patches at ~0.2° yr⁻¹ and a dipole moment decay of ~5 % per century since 1840. The long‑term SV also exhibits episodes of rapid intensity change (geomagnetic spikes) around 1000 BC and 500 AD, with virtual axial dipole moment (VADM) peaks exceeding 120 ZAm², which are not reproduced by standard geodynamo simulations.

Numerical geodynamo models solve the magnetohydrodynamic equations in a rotating spherical shell with realistic parameters (Ekman number E ≈ 10⁻¹⁵, magnetic Prandtl number Pm ≈ 10⁻⁶). The landmark simulation by Glatzmaier & Roberts (1995) produced spontaneous reversals and a dipole‑dominated field with a westward drift of ~0.2° yr⁻¹, matching the paleomagnetic drift rate. Modern simulations (e.g., Aubert et al. 2013; Schaeffer et al. 2017) incorporate heterogeneous core‑mantle boundary (CMB) heat flux patterns derived from seismic tomography, which impose longitudinal variations in the radial heat flow of ±30 %. These models generate flux patches that drift westward and produce SV spectra consistent with the observed power‑law decay (f⁻⁴) at decadal periods. However, the simulated dipole moment decay is typically slower (~1 % per century) than the observed 5 % per century, suggesting that the present‑day geodynamo may be in an unusually rapid decay phase or that the paleomagnetic ADM reconstruction underestimates the true dipole moment due to non‑dipole contamination.

Data‑assimilation techniques now combine paleomagnetic SV with geodynamo forecasts in an ensemble Kalman filter framework (e.g., Fournier et al. 2010). The assimilation of archaeomagnetic declination and inclination reduces the uncertainty in the predicted secular acceleration by ~30 % and improves the forecast of geomagnetic jerks (abrupt changes in secular acceleration) such as those in 1969, 1978, 1991, 2003, and 2016. Outstanding challenges include the sparse Southern Hemisphere archaeomagnetic coverage, the difficulty of separating external magnetospheric fields from internal SV in satellite records during storms, and the need for higher‑resolution CMB heat‑flux maps from next‑generation seismic tomography. Future progress hinges on expanding the global archaeomagnetic database (especially from Africa and South America) and on the planned NanoMagSat mission, which will provide vector measurements up to spherical‑harmonic degree 20 with monthly cadence.

Seismic Imaging of the Upper Mantle Using Ambient Noise Tomography and Full‑Waveform Inversion

Ambient noise tomography (ANT) exploits the cross‑correlation of continuous seismic recordings between station pairs to retrieve empirical Green's functions dominated by surface waves. The method, pioneered by Shapiro et al. (2005) using USArray data, yields Rayleigh‑wave phase‑velocity maps at periods 5‑50 s with lateral resolution of ~50 km, sufficient to image lithospheric thickness variations, sedimentary basins, and the lithosphere‑asthenosphere boundary (LAB). The phase‑velocity dispersion curves are inverted for 1‑D Vs profiles using a neighbourhood algorithm, producing a 3‑D Vs model of the upper 200 km. Typical uncertainties are 0.02‑0.03 km s⁻¹ for Vs, translating to ~10 km uncertainty in LAB depth. ANT is particularly powerful in regions lacking earthquakes, such as stable cratons and oceanic interiors, and it provides a dense, uniform ray coverage that complements earthquake‑based tomography.

Full‑waveform inversion (FWI) takes the next step by fitting the complete waveforms (both amplitude and phase) of surface waves and body waves recorded from ambient noise correlations and from teleseismic events. The adjoint‑state method computes the gradient of the misfit function with respect to the model parameters (Vs, Vp, density, anisotropy) by propagating the adjoint wavefield backward in time. Early applications by Fichtner et al. (2009) and Tape et al. (2010) demonstrated that FWI can resolve Vs anomalies of ±2 % at 100 km depth with a horizontal wavelength of ~100 km, a factor of two improvement over ray‑based tomography. The inclusion of radial anisotropy (VSH vs VSV) in the parameterization allows the recovery of azimuthal anisotropy patterns that correlate with plate motion directions, confirming that the asthenospheric fabric is aligned with present‑day shear. Joint inversion of ANT dispersion data and FWI gradients reduces the non‑linearity of the problem: the ANT model provides a smooth starting model that places the FWI in the basin of attraction of the global minimum.

Recent advances incorporate attenuation (Q) and density into the FWI parameterization, using the viscoelastic spectral‑element method to simulate anelastic wave propagation. The simultaneous inversion for Vs, Qμ, and radial anisotropy from ambient noise cross‑correlations (periods 10‑100 s) and earthquake surface waves (periods 20‑250 s) yields a self‑consistent upper‑mantle model that matches both phase velocities and amplitude decay. Such models reveal a low‑Q, low‑Vs asthenospheric channel beneath the Pacific with Qμ ≈ 80 and Vs ≈ 4.2 km s⁻¹, contrasting with a higher‑Q, higher‑Vs cratonic lithosphere (Qμ > 300, Vs > 4.6 km s⁻¹). The combined ANT‑FWI workflow is now standard in continental‑scale studies (e.g., the European AlpArray and the Chinese ChinArray projects) and provides the high‑resolution structural constraints needed for geodynamic modeling of lithospheric deformation and mantle flow.

Common Traps

  • Confusing ScS precursor depth sensitivity with SS precursor depth sensitivity; ScS samples the CMB reflection point while SS samples near‑vertical incidence.
  • Assuming normal‑mode splitting directly gives the inner‑core fast‑axis orientation without accounting for the trade‑off between anisotropy and ellipticity.
  • Treating a constant‑Q model as adequate for broadband waveform fitting; frequency‑dependent attenuation must be modeled to avoid bias in Vs and anisotropy.
  • Equating paleomagnetic VADM spikes with geodynamo reversal precursors; spikes are intensity excursions, not polarity changes.
  • Using ambient noise cross‑correlations without correcting for source‑distribution anisotropy; uneven noise sources can mimic azimuthal anisotropy.
  • Neglecting the regularization difference between ANT (smoothness) and FWI (model‑space preconditioning) when merging the two datasets.
  • Over‑interpreting a single geomagnetic jerk as a permanent change in core flow; jerks are transient secular‑acceleration events that may not alter the time‑averaged flow.

Probabilistic Seismic Hazard Analysis and Ground‑Motion Prediction Equations

Probabilistic Seismic Hazard Analysis (PSHA) provides a framework for estimating the annual frequency of exceeding a specified ground‑motion level at a site. The method, formalised by Cornell (1968), integrates three components: (1) a seismic‑source model that characterises the geometry, maximum magnitude, and recurrence of earthquakes on faults and in areal zones; (2) a magnitude‑frequency distribution, usually the Gutenberg‑Richter relation log₁₀N = a – bM with b ≈ 1.0 for tectonic regions; and (3) a ground‑motion prediction equation (GMPE) that gives the median and logarithmic standard deviation of a ground‑motion parameter (peak ground acceleration, spectral acceleration at a given period) as a function of magnitude, distance, site condition, and faulting mechanism. Modern GMPEs such as the NGA‑West2 models (e.g., Boore‑Stewart‑Seyhan‑Atkinson 2014) are derived from thousands of recorded motions and include terms for hanging‑wall effects, basin depth, and nonlinear site response. The hazard integral is evaluated by discretising magnitude and distance, computing the exceedance probability for each bin, and summing over all sources. Logic‑tree branches capture epistemic uncertainty in source geometry, maximum magnitude, GMPE selection, and site‑amplification factors; the final hazard curve is the weighted mean of the branch curves. Results are expressed as uniform hazard spectra for return periods of 475 yr (10 % in 50 yr) and 2475 yr (2 % in 50 yr), which feed directly into building‑code design spectra. Sensitivity studies show that the choice of GMPE dominates hazard at short periods, whereas the maximum‑magnitude assumption controls long‑period hazard for stable‑continental regions.

Post‑Seismic Viscoelastic Relaxation and Mantle Viscosity Constraints

After a great megathrust earthquake (M ≥ 8.5) the crust and upper mantle undergo time‑dependent deformation that can be recorded by continuous GPS, InSAR, and tide‑gauge data for years to decades. The observed surface displacement field is modelled as the sum of afterslip on the fault plane, poroelastic rebound in the crust, and viscoelastic flow in the asthenosphere and deeper mantle. A standard approach uses a layered spherical Earth model with Maxwell rheology; the viscosity structure is parameterised as a low‑viscosity asthenospheric channel (10¹⁸–10¹⁹ Pa·s, thickness 100–200 km) overlying a higher‑viscosity upper mantle (10²⁰–10²¹ Pa·s) and a lower mantle of 10²² Pa·s. Forward modelling of the 2004 Sumatra‑Andaman (M = 9.1) and 2011 Tohoku‑Oki (M = 9.0) events demonstrates that the far‑field horizontal velocities (≥ 1000 km from the rupture) are dominated by mantle flow, while near‑field uplift/subsidence patterns are sensitive to the asthenospheric viscosity. Inversions that jointly fit GPS time series and GRACE gravity changes yield a best‑fit asthenospheric viscosity of 4 × 10¹⁸ Pa·s beneath the western Pacific and 1 × 10¹⁹ Pa·s beneath the Indian Ocean, with a trade‑off between asthenospheric thickness and viscosity. The inferred viscosity profile is consistent with laboratory‑derived dislocation‑creep flow laws for olivine at 1300–1400 °C, supporting a thermally controlled rheology. Residuals after removing the viscoelastic model often reveal ongoing afterslip, which can be isolated by high‑rate GPS and used to update seismic‑hazard forecasts for the subduction zone.

Worked Example: Estimating Asthenospheric Viscosity from Post‑Seismic GPS
A Mw = 8.8 subduction earthquake produces a far‑field horizontal velocity of 5 mm yr⁻¹ at a GPS station 1500 km from the trench, measured 2 yr after the event. Assuming a Maxwell viscoelastic half‑space with shear modulus μ = 70 GPa and a single relaxation time τ = η/μ, estimate the effective viscosity η that would generate the observed velocity if the coseismic stress step is Δσ = 0.5 MPa.
For a Maxwell body the strain rate after a stress step is ε̇(t) = (Δσ/η) exp(–t/τ). At t ≪ τ the exponential ≈ 1, so ε̇ ≈ Δσ/η. The surface velocity v ≈ ε̇ × L where L is a characteristic wavelength; for a half‑space L ≈ distance = 1.5 × 10⁶ m. Rearranging, η ≈ Δσ L / v. Substituting Δσ = 0.5 × 10⁶ Pa, L = 1.5 × 10⁶ m, v = 5 mm yr⁻¹ = 5 × 10⁻³ m / (3.15 × 10⁷ s) ≈ 1.59 × 10⁻¹⁰ m s⁻¹ gives η ≈ (0.5 × 10⁶ × 1.5 × 10⁶) / 1.59 × 10⁻¹⁰ ≈ 4.7 × 10¹⁸ Pa·s. This value lies within the accepted asthenospheric range, confirming that the observed far‑field velocity can be explained by viscoelastic relaxation with η ≈ 5 × 10¹⁸ Pa·s.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a viscosity estimate from post‑seismic data, identify the rheological model (Maxwell vs. Burgers), the stress step magnitude, and the observation time relative to the relaxation time. Use the short‑time approximation ε̇ ≈ Δσ/η only if t ≪ τ; otherwise include the exponential decay. Keep units consistent (Pa, m, s) and convert mm yr⁻¹ to m s⁻¹ before calculation.

Seismic Interferometry for Time‑Lapse Monitoring of Fluid Migration and Volcanic Unrest

Seismic interferometry exploits the cross‑correlation of ambient noise or coda waves between station pairs to retrieve the Green’s function of the medium. Repeating this process at regular intervals (daily to weekly) yields time‑lapse measurements of relative velocity changes (dv/v) with a precision of 0.01–0.1 % for periods of 0.1–10 s. In volcanic settings, a decrease in dv/v of 0.2–0.5 % often precedes eruptions by weeks to months, reflecting pressurisation of a magma reservoir and associated crack opening. At geothermal fields, injection‑induced pore‑pressure increases produce a measurable dv/v drop that correlates with the injected volume; the recovery curve constrains the hydraulic diffusivity of the fracture network. The technique also resolves spatial variations by stacking correlations from dense arrays (e.g., the 200‑station Hi‑net network in Japan) and applying moving‑window cross‑spectral analysis. A key practical requirement is a stable noise source distribution; seasonal changes in oceanic microseisms can mimic velocity changes, so a reference station pair in a non‑deforming area is used for differential correction. Recent advances combine interferometric dv/v with full‑waveform inversion of the same noise correlations to separate velocity and attenuation (Q) changes, providing a more complete picture of fluid saturation and temperature evolution.

Effective Medium Theory for Seismic Wave Propagation in Fractured and Porous Media

Effective medium theories (EMTs) upscale the elastic response of a rock containing pores, cracks, or aligned fractures to an equivalent homogeneous anisotropic medium. The classic Hudson (1981) model treats a low concentration of penny‑shaped cracks with aspect ratio α ≪ 1 embedded in an isotropic host; the resulting stiffness tensor exhibits weak transverse isotropy with Thomsen parameters ε ≈ (16/45)(1–ν₀²)/(1–2ν₀) · ε_crack and δ ≈ (8/15)(1–ν₀)/(1–2ν₀) · ε_crack, where ε_crack is the crack density and ν₀ the host Poisson’s ratio. For higher crack densities the non‑interaction approximation fails; self‑consistent and differential effective medium schemes (e.g., Berryman 1995) incorporate crack‑crack interaction and predict a stronger azimuthal anisotropy that matches shear‑wave splitting observations in fractured reservoirs. In fluid‑saturated porous rocks, Biot’s poroelastic theory couples solid and fluid phases, introducing a frequency‑dependent complex modulus. At seismic frequencies (10–100 Hz) the squirt‑flow mechanism between stiff pores and compliant cracks dominates attenuation, producing a characteristic Q⁻¹ peak near the squirt‑flow frequency f_s ≈ η/(ρ_f α² κ), where η is fluid viscosity, ρ_f fluid density, α crack aspect ratio, and κ permeability. Joint inversion of velocity, anisotropy, and attenuation data using EMT‑based rock‑physics templates enables quantitative estimation of fracture density, orientation, and fluid saturation — critical for reservoir characterisation and CO₂ storage monitoring.

Normal‑Mode Constraints on Core‑Mantle Boundary Topography and Inner Core Structure

Earth’s free oscillations (normal modes) provide integral constraints on laterally varying structure because each mode samples the entire planet with a known sensitivity kernel. The splitting of degenerate spheroidal and toroidal modes (e.g., ⁰S₂, ⁰T₂) is expressed as a sum over spherical‑harmonic coefficients of perturbations in density, elastic parameters, and boundary topography. For the core‑mantle boundary (CMB), the degree‑2 topography coefficient h₂′ is constrained to ± 0.5 km by the observed splitting of the ⁰S₂ and ⁰T₂ multiplets, implying a dynamic topography amplitude of a few hundred metres consistent with mantle convection models. Higher‑degree CMB topography (ℓ = 3–10) is resolved by overtone splitting (e.g., ¹S₃, ¹T₃) and yields a power spectrum that decays as ℓ⁻², supporting a thermally driven boundary layer. For the inner core, normal‑mode splitting of the ⁰S₀ (breathing) and ⁰S₂ modes isolates the inner‑core shear velocity V_S and its anisotropy. The observed 0.3 % faster velocity along the rotation axis versus the equatorial plane translates to a hexagonal‑close‑packed iron texture with a fast axis aligned within 10° of the rotation axis. Differential splitting of the ⁰S₀ and ⁰S₂ modes also constrains the inner‑core radius to 1221.5 ± 0.3 km and the density jump at the inner‑core boundary to 0.6 g cm⁻³, values that agree with mineral‑physics equations of state for Fe‑Ni alloys at 330 GPa. Combining normal‑mode data with body‑wave travel‑time residuals in a joint inversion reduces trade‑offs between inner‑core anisotropy and CMB topography, delivering a self‑consistent model of the deep Earth.

  • Confusing the Gutenberg‑Richter b‑value with the GMPE sigma; b controls magnitude frequency, sigma controls ground‑motion variability.
  • Assuming a single Maxwell viscosity fits all post‑seismic time scales; a Burgers or multi‑layer model is often required.
  • Interpreting dv/v changes from ambient noise as purely velocity changes without correcting for seasonal noise‑source anisotropy.
  • Applying Hudson’s low‑crack‑density formulas to highly fractured reservoirs where interaction effects dominate.
  • Neglecting the trade‑off between inner‑core anisotropy and CMB topography when using only a few normal modes.
  • Using a constant‑Q model in full‑waveform inversion of broadband data, which biases both velocity and anisotropy estimates.
  • Treating the logic‑tree weights in PSHA as probabilities of occurrence rather than epistemic weights for alternative models.

Distributed Acoustic Sensing and Fiber‑Optic Seismology: Principles and Applications

Distributed Acoustic Sensing (DAS) transforms a standard telecommunications fiber‑optic cable into a dense array of strain‑rate sensors by interrogating Rayleigh backscatter from laser pulses. The phase of the backscattered light at each gauge length (typically 1–10 m) is proportional to the axial strain rate induced by passing seismic waves, yielding a spatial sampling density orders of magnitude higher than conventional geophone arrays. A single 50 km fiber can provide >10,000 virtual channels, enabling wavefield imaging with unprecedented spatial resolution. The measurement principle relies on coherent optical time‑domain reflectometry (C‑OTDR) or phase‑OTDR; the phase difference between successive pulses at the same spatial location gives the strain rate ε̇ = (λ/4πnL) Δφ/Δt, where λ is the laser wavelength, n the fiber refractive index, L the gauge length, and Δφ the phase change over time interval Δt. DAS responds primarily to strain parallel to the cable axis, making it sensitive to P‑waves and SV‑waves with particle motion in the vertical plane containing the fiber, but largely insensitive to SH‑waves polarized perpendicular to the cable. This directional sensitivity must be accounted for in array processing and inversion.

Applications span scales from near‑surface characterization to teleseismic tomography. In urban environments, dark fibers beneath cities have been used for ambient‑noise surface‑wave tomography at metre‑scale resolution, imaging sedimentary basins, fault zones, and anthropogenic noise sources. At regional scales, submarine cables crossing ocean basins (e.g., the Curie cable off Chile, the Google cable in the Pacific) have recorded teleseismic P‑ and S‑waves, microseisms, and T‑phases, demonstrating the potential for global seismic monitoring without dedicated ocean‑bottom seismometers. In active‑source settings, DAS vertical seismic profiling (VSP) and walk‑away VSP provide high‑resolution images of reservoir compartments, fracture corridors, and CO₂ plume migration. A key challenge is the unknown coupling between the fiber and the host medium: cemented cables in boreholes approximate strain coupling, while loose cables in conduits or buried directly in soil exhibit complex transfer functions that depend on burial depth, soil stiffness, and cable‑soil friction. Calibration using co‑located geophones or controlled sources is essential for quantitative amplitude analysis. Recent advances include polarization‑diverse DAS that measures strain in two orthogonal axes, enabling full vector wavefield reconstruction, and the use of DAS for train‑based seismic interferometry where passing trains act as moving sources for continuous subsurface monitoring.

Seismic Imaging of the Mid‑Lithospheric Discontinuity and Its Geodynamic Significance

The mid‑lithospheric discontinuity (MLD) is a seismic velocity gradient or sharp interface observed within the continental lithospheric mantle at depths of 60–120 km, distinct from the lithosphere‑asthenosphere boundary (LAB) which typically lies at 100–250 km beneath cratons. Receiver‑function studies, SS precursors, and surface‑wave tomography consistently image a negative velocity contrast (2–5 % reduction in Vs) at the MLD beneath stable cratons including the Kaapvaal, Slave, North China, and Siberian cratons. The MLD is often sharper and more laterally continuous than the LAB, with a typical thickness of 10–20 km. Its origin remains debated: proposed mechanisms include (1) a metasomatic front where volatile‑rich melts or fluids (carbonatitic, kimberlitic, or hydrous silicate) have refertilized the depleted lithospheric mantle, introducing clinopyroxene, garnet, and phlogopite that reduce seismic velocities; (2) a fossilized melt‑extraction boundary marking the base of the highly depleted, buoyant cratonic root formed during Archean komatiite extraction; (3) an anisotropic layer where lattice‑preferred orientation of olivine changes due to a shift in deformation fabric from horizontal (fossil) to vertical (active) flow; and (4) a thermal boundary layer within the lithosphere where temperature gradients steepen due to radiogenic heating or small‑scale convection.

Joint inversion of receiver functions, surface‑wave dispersion, and body‑wave travel times shows that the MLD velocity contrast correlates with high electrical conductivity in magnetotelluric models, supporting the metasomatic interpretation. Xenolith data from kimberlite pipes sampling the MLD depth range reveal enriched garnet peridotites with elevated clinopyroxene modes and water contents of 50–200 ppm H₂O, consistent with a refertilized layer. The MLD appears to be a persistent feature of cratonic lithosphere, surviving multiple Wilson cycles, suggesting it is chemically rather than thermally controlled. Its presence influences lithospheric strength: the weakened, hydrated layer may act as a decoupling horizon, allowing the cratonic root to translate relative to the overlying crust during plate motions. In some regions (e.g., the North China Craton), the MLD has been destroyed or modified by Mesozoic lithospheric thinning, providing a seismic marker for lithospheric destruction. Finite‑frequency tomography using teleseismic P‑ and S‑waves resolves the MLD as a dipping structure in some cratons, possibly reflecting paleo‑subduction or plume‑lithosphere interaction. The global distribution and sharpness of the MLD make it a key target for testing models of craton formation, stabilization, and destruction.

Worked Problem: DAS Gauge Length and Spatial Aliasing
A DAS system uses a gauge length of 10 m and a spatial sampling interval of 1 m along a straight fiber. A Rayleigh wave with phase velocity 2.5 km/s and frequency 10 Hz propagates at 30° to the fiber axis. Determine whether spatial aliasing occurs and calculate the apparent wavelength measured along the fiber.
The true wavelength is λ = v/f = 2500 m/s ÷ 10 Hz = 250 m. The component of wavevector along the fiber is k_∥ = (2π/λ) cos 30° = (2π/250) × 0.866 = 0.0218 rad/m. The apparent wavelength along the fiber is λ_app = 2π/k_∥ = 288 m. The Nyquist wavelength for spatial sampling Δx = 1 m is λ_Nyquist = 2Δx = 2 m. Since λ_app ≫ λ_Nyquist, no spatial aliasing occurs from channel spacing. However, the gauge length L = 10 m acts as a spatial low‑pass filter with first null at λ_gauge = 2L = 20 m. Because λ_app = 288 m ≫ 20 m, the gauge‑length filtering is negligible. The measurement is valid. If the frequency were 100 Hz (λ = 25 m, λ_app = 28.8 m), gauge‑length filtering would attenuate the signal by ~30 % but aliasing would still be absent.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question mentions DAS, identify the gauge length and channel spacing separately — they control different artifacts (gauge length = spatial smoothing, channel spacing = Nyquist limit). For MLD questions, distinguish it from the LAB by depth (MLD 60–120 km, LAB >100 km), polarity (MLD usually negative Vs contrast, LAB can be positive or negative), and tectonic setting (MLD primarily cratonic). Remember that DAS measures strain rate, not displacement; convert to displacement by integration in frequency domain (divide by iω) for comparison with geophone data.

Geophysical Constraints on Mantle Noble Gas Isotopes, Primordial Helium‑3, and Degassing History

Noble gas isotopes provide unique tracers of mantle reservoir formation, degassing, and mixing because they are chemically inert and have distinct radiogenic and primordial components. The ³He/⁴He ratio (expressed as R/Rₐ, where Rₐ = 1.39 × 10⁻⁶ is the atmospheric ratio) is the premier indicator of primordial versus recycled material. Mid‑ocean ridge basalts (MORBs) display a narrow range of ³He/⁴He ≈ 8 ± 1 Rₐ, defining the depleted upper mantle (DMM) reservoir that has lost >99 % of its primordial helium through melt extraction and degassing over Earth history. Ocean‑island basalts (OIBs) from hotspots such as Hawaii, Iceland, and Samoa show elevated ³He/⁴He up to 50 Rₐ, signaling a less‑degassed, primordial reservoir often attributed to the deep mantle or core‑mantle boundary. The ⁴He is produced by α‑decay of ²³⁸U, ²³⁵U, and ²³²Th; the ³He is purely primordial (trapped during accretion). The present‑day mantle ³He flux to the surface is ~1000 mol/yr, integrated over 4.5 Ga implies a total degassed ³He inventory of ~4.5 × 10¹⁵ mol, requiring a bulk silicate Earth ³He concentration of ~1.5 × 10⁻¹³ cm³ STP/g, consistent with carbonaceous chondrite models.

Neon isotopes (²⁰Ne/²²Ne, ²¹Ne/²²Ne) further discriminate reservoirs: solar‑wind‑implanted neon has ²⁰Ne/²²Ne ≈ 13.8, while nucleogenic ²¹Ne from ¹⁸O(α,n)²¹Ne raises ²¹Ne/²²Ne in degassed mantle. MORB neon plots on a mixing line between solar and nucleogenic end‑members, whereas some OIBs (e.g., Iceland) approach the solar ²⁰Ne/²²Ne, supporting a solar‑like primordial component in the deep mantle. Argon isotopes (⁴⁰Ar/³⁶Ar) track potassium decay (⁴⁰K → ⁴⁰Ar); the atmospheric ⁴⁰Ar/³⁶Ar = 295.5 reflects near‑complete degassing of ⁴⁰Ar, while MORB values of 10,000–40,000 indicate the upper mantle retains significant radiogenic argon. The ¹²⁹Xe/¹³⁰Xe and ¹³¹–¹³⁶Xe/¹³⁰Xe ratios record extinct radionuclides ¹²⁹I (t₁/₂ = 15.7 Ma) and ²⁴⁴Pu (t₁/₂ = 80 Ma), constraining early degassing to within the first 100 Ma. The observation that ³He/²²Ne in MORB is ~10× higher than in air, while ³He/²²Ne in OIB can be >100× air, implies that helium is less compatible than neon during melting, or that the deep mantle reservoir has a distinct volatile budget. Coupled noble‑gas and lithophile isotope systematics (e.g., ³He/⁴He vs. ¹⁴³Nd/¹⁴⁴Nd, ⁸⁷Sr/⁸⁶Sr) reveal that high‑³He/⁴He OIBs do not always correlate with extreme radiogenic isotope signatures, suggesting the primordial helium reservoir is not simply the most primitive in terms of lithophile elements. This decoupling challenges simple two‑reservoir models and supports a heterogeneous deep mantle with multiple primordial components.

Seismic Wave Propagation in Tilted Transversely Isotropic Media and Dipping Anisotropy

Transverse isotropy with a tilted symmetry axis (TTI) generalizes the vertically transverse isotropic (VTI) and horizontally transverse isotropic (HTI) models to arbitrary symmetry‑axis orientation, essential for imaging dipping shale layers, folded strata, and mantle fabric in subduction zones. The elastic stiffness tensor for TTI media is obtained by rotating the VTI stiffness matrix Cᵛᵀᴵ by polar angle θ (tilt from vertical) and azimuth φ (rotation about vertical). In the rotated frame, the five independent VTI parameters (Vₚ₀, Vₛ₀, ε, δ, γ) plus two angles (θ, φ) fully describe the medium. The phase velocity for qP‑waves in TTI media is given by the exact solution of the Christoffel equation or by the Alkhalifah (2000) approximation: Vₚ(θ,φ) ≈ Vₚ₀ √[1 + 2δ sin²(θ−θ₀) + 2ε sin⁴(θ−θ₀)] for propagation in the symmetry‑axis plane, where θ₀ is the symmetry‑axis tilt. For arbitrary propagation directions, the full 3D Christoffel equation must be solved numerically. The qSV‑wave velocity exhibits a characteristic shear‑wave singularity (kissing point) where qSV and qSH velocities coincide, leading to strong polarization anomalies and triplications in wavefronts.
