This heat‑flux pattern influences the geodynamo by modulating the convective power available in the outer core, linking deep mantle chemistry to the generation of Earth’s magnetic field.

Outstanding questions include the exact nature of the dense component (Fe‑rich post‑perovskite versus partial melt), the timescale of pile stability, and the role of subducted slab material in replenishing or eroding the piles. Future progress will rely on (i) higher‑frequency FWI (> 0.1 Hz) to resolve ULVZ internal structure, (ii) laboratory measurements of electrical conductivity and seismic attenuation of candidate CMB phases at relevant P‑T conditions, and (iii) improved geochemical tracers (e.g., ¹⁸²W anomalies) that can fingerprint core‑mantle interaction.

Joint Inversion Trade‑off Analysis
A global joint inversion uses normal‑mode centre frequencies (sensitive to density) and broadband S‑wave traveltimes (sensitive to Vs). The initial model shows a 2 % Vs reduction in the LLSVP but a negligible density anomaly. After adding the normal‑mode data, the density anomaly increases to 0.8 % while the Vs reduction remains 2 %. Explain why the inclusion of normal‑mode data primarily affects density and not Vs, and describe how this helps to distinguish a thermal from a thermochemical origin of the LLSVP.
Normal‑mode centre frequencies depend on the integral of density and elastic parameters weighted by the mode eigenfunctions; for the low‑degree spheroidal modes (0S₂, 0S₃) the density kernel peaks in the lower mantle and at the CMB, whereas the Vs kernel is broader. Broadband S‑wave traveltimes are dominated by shear‑velocity structure along the ray path and have limited sensitivity to density. Therefore, adding normal‑mode data constrains density directly without strongly perturbing Vs. A purely thermal anomaly would produce a correlated Vs reduction and density decrease (thermal expansion), yielding a negative density anomaly. The observed positive density anomaly (denser material) together with a Vs reduction indicates a compositional (iron‑rich) component, supporting a thermochemical pile rather than a purely thermal upwelling.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to differentiate thermal versus thermochemical mantle anomalies, recall the sign of the density perturbation: thermal anomalies give negative Δρ (hot material expands), while thermochemical piles give positive Δρ (dense composition). Cite the joint inversion of normal modes (density) and body waves (Vs) as the key observational test.

Time‑Dependent Seismic Velocity Changes from Repeating Earthquakes and Their Implications for Fault Healing

Repeating earthquake sequences — events that rupture the same fault patch with nearly identical waveforms — provide a natural laboratory for monitoring temporal changes in seismic velocity (Δv/v) within the fault zone. By cross‑correlating the coda of successive events, researchers have measured velocity reductions of 0.1–0.5 % following large mainshocks, followed by a logarithmic recovery over months to years. The 1999 Mw 7.6 Chi‑Chi earthquake in Taiwan, the 2004 Mw 9.1 Sumatra‑Andaman event, and the 2011 Mw 9.0 Tohoku‑Oki earthquake all exhibit this pattern. The initial velocity drop is attributed to dynamic stress‑induced damage (microcrack opening) and fluid pressure increase in the fault damage zone, while the recovery reflects crack closure, mineral precipitation, and pore‑pressure diffusion — collectively termed fault healing.

Laboratory rock‑physics experiments on gouge‑filled faults under controlled pore‑pressure conditions reproduce the observed Δv/v magnitude and recovery law. The rate‑and‑state friction framework predicts that the healing rate is proportional to the logarithm of time, consistent with the observed logarithmic velocity recovery. In the Parkfield segment of the San Andreas Fault, repeating microearthquakes (M ≈ 1–2) have been used to construct a continuous Δv/v time series spanning two decades, revealing seasonal variations linked to groundwater loading and a long‑term healing trend after the 2004 Mw 6.0 Parkfield earthquake.

Ambient‑noise interferometry complements repeating‑earthquake analyses by providing continuous velocity measurements at periods of 0.1–10 s. Cross‑correlation of daily noise records at station pairs straddling the fault yields Δv/v with a precision of 0.02 %, enabling detection of pre‑seismic velocity changes. In the 2018 Mw 7.5 Palu earthquake (Indonesia), a 0.03 % velocity reduction was observed 2–3 weeks before the mainshock, suggesting a possible preparatory process. However, the statistical significance of such precursors remains debated because similar fluctuations occur during non‑seismic periods.

The physical interpretation of Δv/v relies on effective‑medium theories (e.g., Hudson’s crack model) that relate velocity changes to crack density, aspect ratio, and fluid saturation. A 0.2 % Vs reduction corresponds to a crack density increase of ~10⁻³ for penny‑shaped cracks with aspect ratio 10⁻³, assuming dry conditions. Fluid‑filled cracks produce a larger velocity drop per unit crack density because of the strong contrast in bulk modulus. Therefore, joint inversion of Δv/v and attenuation (ΔQ⁻¹) changes can discriminate between dry damage and fluid‑pressure effects.

Future monitoring networks (e.g., the Dense Oceanfloor Network System for Earthquakes and Tsunamis, DONET, and the USArray Transportable Array) will increase the spatial density of repeating‑earthquake and ambient‑noise measurements, allowing 4‑D imaging of fault‑zone healing. Coupling these observations with rate‑and‑state friction models and poroelastic simulations will improve forecasts of fault‑strength evolution and the likelihood of subsequent large earthquakes.

Magnetotelluric Imaging of the Lithosphere‑Asthenosphere Boundary and Its Correlation with Seismic Anisotropy

Magnetotelluric (MT) surveys exploit natural electromagnetic field variations (10⁻⁴–10⁴ Hz) to image electrical conductivity structures from the crust to the upper mantle. The lithosphere‑asthenosphere boundary (LAB) is marked by a sharp increase in conductivity (from ~10⁻³ S/m in the lithospheric mantle to 0.1–1 S/m in the asthenosphere) attributed to the presence of partial melt, hydrogen‑related defects in olivine, or interconnected graphite films. Large‑scale MT arrays such as the USArray MT component, the European AlpArray, and the Chinese SinoProbe have resolved the LAB depth beneath continents (80–250 km) and oceans (50–100 km) with lateral resolution of ~50 km.

Seismic anisotropy, measured via shear‑wave splitting of SKS phases and surface‑wave azimuthal anisotropy, provides an independent probe of mantle deformation. In oceanic regions, the fast‑axis direction of azimuthal anisotropy aligns with absolute plate motion, indicating lattice‑preferred orientation (LPO) of olivine in the asthenospheric flow. MT conductivity anisotropy, derived from the impedance tensor decomposition, often shows a coincident fast‑axis orientation, suggesting that the same deformation fabric controls both electrical and seismic anisotropy. However, beneath cratons, seismic anisotropy may reflect fossilized lithospheric fabric while MT detects a deeper, more conductive asthenospheric layer, leading to a depth offset between the two anisotropy signals.

Joint inversion of MT and seismic data reduces non‑uniqueness. For example, a 1‑D joint inversion beneath the Tanzanian Craton yields a lithospheric thickness of 180 km from seismic receiver functions, while MT requires a conductive layer at 200 km depth. The combined model reconciles these by placing a thin (≈ 10 km) melt‑rich layer at the LAB that enhances conductivity but has a modest seismic velocity contrast. Laboratory measurements of olivine conductivity at 3 GPa and 1300 °C show that 0.1 wt % water increases conductivity by two orders of magnitude, matching the MT‑inferred asthenospheric values without requiring melt.

Time‑lapse MT monitoring at volcanic arcs (e.g., Mt. Etna, Kīlauea) has detected conductivity changes of 10–20 % preceding eruptions, interpreted as magma ascent and volatile exsolution. When combined with repeating‑earthquake Δv/v measurements, these data provide a multi‑physical view of fluid migration in the crust and upper mantle. The correlation between MT‑derived melt fraction and seismic attenuation (Qμ⁻¹) in the mantle wedge above subducting slabs supports a model where slab‑derived fluids lower the solidus, generate melt, and enhance both conductivity and attenuation.

Outstanding challenges include the separation of temperature, water, and melt contributions to conductivity, and the effect of anisotropic conductivity on MT phase‑tensor interpretation. Emerging 3‑D joint inversion frameworks that incorporate seismic tomography, MT, and gravity data promise to deliver unified models of the LAB, asthenospheric flow, and melt distribution, essential for understanding plate coupling and mantle dynamics.

Geodynamic Modeling of Plate Boundary Forces Using Coupled Seismic and Geodetic Observations

The forces driving plate motions — ridge push, slab pull, and trench suction — are quantified by integrating seismic tomography, GPS‑derived surface velocities, and stress‑orientation data into geodynamic forward models. Global mantle convection models (e.g., CitcomS, ASPECT) prescribe a depth‑dependent viscosity profile (η ≈ 10²¹ Pa·s in the upper mantle, 10²²–10²³ Pa·s in the lower mantle) and incorporate phase transitions at 410 km and 660 km with Clapeyron slopes of +2.5 MPa/K and –2.5 MPa/K, respectively. The models compute the instantaneous flow field and the resulting dynamic topography, which is compared to the observed geoid and residual topography after removing the isostatic contribution.

Seismic tomography provides the 3‑D density heterogeneity (Δρ) needed to calculate buoyancy forces. The S40RTS and GLAD‑M25 models reveal that the LLSVPs contribute a net negative buoyancy (dense piles) that drives upwellings at their margins, while subducted slabs appear as high‑velocity, high‑density anomalies sinking into the lower mantle. The slab‑pull force is estimated by integrating the negative buoyancy of the slab over its length; for the Tonga‑Kermadec slab, the integrated force is ~2 × 10¹³ N/m, sufficient to account for > 70 % of the Pacific plate driving force. Ridge push, derived from the gravitational potential energy of the elevated mid‑ocean ridges, contributes ~10–15 % of the total force budget.

Geodetic observations constrain the surface expression of these forces. Continuous GPS networks (e.g., IGS, UNAVCO) provide horizontal velocities with 1 mm/yr precision, allowing inversion for the basal traction distribution beneath plates. In the Andes, the observed trench‑parallel shortening rate of 20–30 mm/yr requires a basal drag of ~5 MPa, consistent with a strong coupling zone inferred from interseismic locking models. In contrast, the Cascadia margin shows a low coupling coefficient (< 0.2) and a basal drag < 1 MPa, reflecting a weak, possibly serpentinized, plate interface.

Stress‑orientation data from borehole breakouts, focal mechanisms, and the World Stress Map provide independent constraints on the deviatoric stress field. Global models that match both the GPS velocity field and the stress orientations require a lithospheric strength envelope that follows Byerlee’s law (τ = 0.85 σₙ for σₙ < 200 MPa) in the brittle crust and a power‑law creep rheology (dislocation creep, n ≈ 3.5) in the ductile mantle. The transition depth (brittle‑ductile transition) varies from 10–15 km in young oceanic lithosphere to 30–40 km in old continental lithosphere, controlling the partitioning of plate‑boundary forces between the crust and mantle.

Coupled seismic‑geodetic inversions have quantified the temporal evolution of plate‑boundary forces during the seismic cycle. Post‑seismic viscoelastic relaxation after the 2004 Sumatra‑Andaman earthquake produced a transient increase in slab‑pull force of ~5 % that decayed over a decade, consistent with the observed post‑seismic GPS velocities in the Indian Ocean. Similarly, the 2011 Tohoku‑Oki earthquake altered the stress field across the Japan Trench, reducing the coupling coefficient from 0.9 to 0.6 in the rupture zone, as inferred from repeating‑earthquake Δv/v and GPS time series. These observations demonstrate that plate‑boundary forces are not static but evolve with the earthquake cycle, influencing long‑term plate motions and mantle convection patterns.

  • Assuming that a single seismic observable (e.g., Vs alone) uniquely determines mantle composition; joint inversion with Vp, ρ, and Q is required to break trade‑offs.
  • Treating fault‑zone low‑velocity zones as purely lithological without considering fluid‑pressure effects that can produce identical velocity reductions.
  • Equating oceanic lithosphere Qs values directly to temperature while ignoring the strong frequency dependence introduced by melt and crack populations.
  • Interpreting CMB topography from normal‑mode splitting without accounting for the contaminating effect of mantle heterogeneity on the same modes.
  • Over‑relying on a single FWI model parameter (e.g., Vs) to infer dynamic topography; density and attenuation must be simultaneously constrained.
  • Neglecting the different depth sensitivities of ambient‑noise surface waves (upper 100 km) and body‑wave attenuation (whole mantle) when jointly inverting for water content.
  • Assuming that shear‑wave splitting fast directions in the oceanic lithosphere always align with absolute plate motion; they often reflect fossilized asthenospheric flow.

Seismic Imaging of Crustal Magma Reservoirs Using Full‑Waveform Inversion and Ambient Noise Tomography

Data Acquisition and Preprocessing

Modern crustal magma imaging relies on dense broadband arrays (spacing 1–5 km) that record both active‑source shots and continuous ambient noise. Active sources (vibroseis, air‑gun, or controlled explosions) provide high‑frequency body‑wave energy (2–10 Hz) that penetrates to 10–15 km depth, while ambient noise cross‑correlations yield surface‑wave Green’s functions in the 0.05–0.5 Hz band, sensitive to the upper 5 km. Pre‑processing includes instrument response removal, temporal normalization (one‑bit or running‑absolute‑mean), and spectral whitening to equalize frequency content before cross‑correlation. Quality control rejects stations with > 20 % data gaps or anomalous noise levels.

Full‑Waveform Inversion Workflow

Full‑waveform inversion (FWI) minimizes the L2 misfit between observed and synthetic seismograms by iteratively updating a 3‑D velocity model (Vp, Vs, ρ). The gradient is computed via the adjoint‑state method, requiring forward and adjoint wavefield simulations in a viscoelastic medium. A multi‑scale frequency continuation (starting at 1 Hz, progressing to 6 Hz) mitigates cycle‑skipping. Regularization combines Tikhonov smoothing with total‑variation constraints to preserve sharp velocity contrasts at magma‑host rock boundaries. Model updates are preconditioned by the approximate Hessian diagonal to balance illumination across the reservoir.

Ambient Noise Tomography Complement

Ambient noise tomography (ANT) supplies a low‑frequency Vs model that anchors the long‑wavelength structure for FWI. Phase‑velocity maps from Rayleigh and Love waves are inverted for a 3‑D Vs model using a transdimensional Bayesian framework, yielding posterior uncertainties. The ANT model is then used as the starting model for FWI, reducing the risk of local minima. Joint inversion of ANT dispersion curves and FWI waveforms can be performed in a single objective function, weighting each data type by its noise covariance.

Case Studies: Yellowstone and Campi Flegrei

At Yellowstone, a 200‑station array imaged a 10 km‑wide low‑Vs anomaly (ΔVs/Vs ≈ ‑15 %) at 5–8 km depth, interpreted as a partially molten rhyolitic reservoir with melt fraction 5–10 %. The FWI model resolved a high‑Vp/Vs ratio (> 1.9) consistent with volatile‑rich melt. At Campi Flegrei, combined ANT‑FWI revealed a shallow (2–4 km) low‑velocity zone beneath the caldera floor, correlating with uplift episodes measured by InSAR. Time‑lapse FWI using repeated active‑source surveys detected a 3 % Vs reduction preceding the 2012‑2013 unrest, demonstrating monitoring capability.

Resolution Limits and Trade‑offs

Vertical resolution of FWI is ≈ λ/4 (≈ 300 m at 5 Hz), while ANT resolves ≈ 1–2 km. Lateral resolution follows the Fresnel zone width, typically 1–2 km for crustal depths. Trade‑offs between Vp and Vs are mitigated by including both P‑ and S‑wave arrivals and by imposing petrophysical constraints (e.g., Vp/Vs bounds from laboratory measurements). Melt fraction estimates remain non‑unique without independent constraints on temperature and volatile content from magnetotelluric or gravity data.

Geophysical Constraints on the Frequency and Timing of Geomagnetic Reversals from Paleomagnetic Records

Paleomagnetic Database and Age Control

The Geomagnetic Polarity Time Scale (GPTS) is built from > 5 000 polarity intervals recorded in marine magnetic anomalies, volcanic sequences, and sedimentary cores. High‑precision ⁴⁰Ar/³⁹Ar dating of lava flows (± 0.1 % relative uncertainty) and astrochronology of deep‑sea sediments provide absolute ages for reversal boundaries. The Cenozoic GPTS (0–66 Ma) is calibrated to the astronomical timescale, yielding reversal ages with ≤ 10 kyr uncertainty. Older intervals (Mesozoic, Paleozoic) rely on biostratigraphic correlation and have larger age errors (0.5–1 Myr).

Reversal Frequency Through the Phanerozoic

Reversal frequency varies dramatically: the Cretaceous Normal Superchron (CNS, 121–83 Ma) shows zero reversals for ~38 Myr, whereas the Jurassic–Early Cretaceous exhibits > 5 reversals/Myr. The Cenozoic average is ~4–5 reversals/Myr, with a marked increase to ~7/Myr in the last 10 Myr. Statistical analysis using a Poisson process with time‑varying rate λ(t) indicates that the reversal rate is not stationary; a Bayesian change‑point model identifies at least three distinct regimes (superchron, moderate, high) across the Phanerozoic.

Superchrons and Their Geodynamic Implications

Superchrons (CNS, Kiaman Reverse Superchron 312–262 Ma, Moyero Superchron 485–463 Ma) coincide with intervals of low core‑mantle boundary (CMB) heat flux inferred from mantle convection models. Numerical geodynamo simulations show that a reduced CMB heat flux stabilizes the axial dipole, suppressing reversals. The end of the CNS correlates with the onset of the Pacific superplume and a rise in CMB heat flux, suggesting a causal link between mantle dynamics and reversal frequency.

Statistical Models of Reversal Occurrence

Reversal waiting times are well described by a gamma distribution with shape parameter k ≈ 0.7, indicating clustering (memory) rather than a pure Poisson process. Hidden Markov models with two states (high‑rate, low‑rate) reproduce the observed alternation of superchrons and frequent‑reversal epochs. The transition probabilities imply a mean residence time of ~30 Myr in the low‑rate state, consistent with mantle convection turnover timescales.

Link to Core‑Mantle Boundary Heat Flux

Seismic tomography reveals large low‑shear‑velocity provinces (LLSVPs) at the base of the mantle beneath Africa and the Pacific. Geodynamic models predict that LLSVPs modulate CMB heat flux by ± 30 % relative to the global average. Periods of enhanced heat flux beneath the Pacific LLSVP correspond to higher reversal rates in the GPTS, while the African LLSVP’s relatively stable flux aligns with the CNS. This correlation supports the hypothesis that mantle heterogeneity controls long‑term geomagnetic behavior.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: "Which of the following statements about geomagnetic reversal frequency is correct? (a) Reversal rate has been constant throughout the Phanerozoic. (b) The Cretaceous Normal Superchron lasted ~38 Myr with zero reversals. (c) Superchrons are associated with increased CMB heat flux. (d) Reversal waiting times follow a pure Poisson distribution."
Correct answer: (b). The CNS (121–83 Ma) is a well‑documented superchron of ~38 Myr with no reversals. Statement (a) is false because reversal frequency varies by an order of magnitude. Statement (c) reverses the geodynamic link; superchrons correlate with reduced CMB heat flux. Statement (d) is false; waiting times follow a gamma distribution with shape < 1, indicating clustering.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorize the three major superchrons (CNS, Kiaman, Moyero) with their age ranges and the associated reversal rates (0, ~1–2/Myr, ~4–5/Myr). Be ready to sketch a qualitative GPTS showing superchrons as long single‑polarity blocks. When a question cites a reversal frequency, check whether the time interval falls inside a superchron; this is a common distractor.

Joint Inversion of Seismic, Gravity, and Magnetic Data for Lithospheric Structure and Composition

Physical Basis of Joint Inversion

Seismic velocities (Vp, Vs) respond to temperature, composition, and partial melt; gravity anomalies reflect density contrasts; magnetic anomalies are sensitive to magnetization (remanent and induced) which depends on mineralogy (magnetite content) and Curie‑depth temperature. By inverting all three data sets simultaneously, trade‑offs between temperature and composition that plague single‑data inversions are reduced. The forward problem couples a thermodynamic model (e.g., Perple_X) that predicts Vp, Vs, ρ, and magnetic susceptibility as functions of pressure, temperature, and bulk composition.

Parameterization and Regularization

The lithosphere is discretized into a 3‑D voxel grid (5 km horizontal, 2 km vertical). Model parameters are temperature (T), major‑element composition (Mg#, FeO), and melt fraction (φ). Regularization employs a compound objective function: Φ = Φ_seis + w_g Φ_grav + w_m Φ_mag + α R(T,comp,φ), where R is a spatial smoothness term and α controls model roughness. Weights w_g, w_m are set by the inverse data covariance matrices. A petrophysical constraint enforces Vp/Vs bounds derived from laboratory measurements on peridotite and eclogite.

Synthetic Tests and Resolution Analysis

Checkerboard and spike tests using synthetic data generated from a known model (e.g., a 150 km thick cratonic root with a 5 % Vs increase) demonstrate that joint inversion recovers the root’s lateral extent and depth with < 10 % amplitude error, whereas seismic‑only inversion smears the root vertically. Resolution matrices show that gravity data sharpen the Moho depth, while magnetic data resolve the Curie‑depth isotherm (~580 °C) and thus constrain the thermal gradient.

Application to the Canadian Shield

A joint inversion of USArray broadband data, GRACE‑FO gravity gradients, and EMAG2 magnetic anomalies across the Superior Province produced a 3‑D model revealing a 250 km thick, high‑Vs (4.7 km/s), high‑density (3.35 g/cm³) lithospheric keel with low magnetization, consistent with a depleted harzburgitic composition. The model predicts a Curie depth of 35 km, matching independent heat‑flow measurements. The inferred Mg# ≈ 0.92 aligns with xenolith data, validating the joint approach.

Remaining Ambiguities

Non‑uniqueness persists between temperature and iron content because both increase density and Vs. Magnetization is further complicated by remanent magnetization acquired during cooling, which is not captured by induced‑only forward models. Future work incorporates rock‑magnetic hysteresis parameters and seismic attenuation (Q) to break the temperature‑composition trade‑off.

Geophysical Constraints on the Earth's Inner Core Viscosity from Normal‑Mode Seismology and Mineral Physics

Normal‑Mode Sensitivity to Inner‑Core Viscosity

Free oscillations (normal modes) such as ₀S₂, ₀T₂, and ₁S₂ have eigenfunctions that penetrate the inner core. Their complex frequencies (ω = ωᵣ + iωᵢ) are split by rotation, ellipticity, and inner‑core viscosity (η_IC). The quality factor Q_IC = ωᵣ/(2ωᵢ) for modes with significant inner‑core kinetic energy provides a direct constraint on η_IC. Recent analyses of 20‑year superconducting gravimeter records and global broadband networks yield Q_IC ≈ 600 ± 150 for the ₀S₂ mode, implying η_IC ≈ 10¹⁴–10¹⁵ Pa·s assuming a Maxwell rheology.

Laboratory Measurements of Iron Alloy Viscosity at Core Conditions

Diamond‑anvil cell experiments combined with in‑situ X‑ray diffraction measure the shear viscosity of Fe‑Ni‑Si alloys at 330 GPa and 5500 K using the deformation‑diffusion creep regime. Results give η_lab ≈ 10¹³ Pa·s for a composition of Fe‑8 wt% Ni‑4 wt% Si, with a strong temperature dependence (activation energy ~ 300 kJ/mol). Extrapolation to inner‑core conditions using the Arrhenius law yields η_IC ≈ 10¹⁴ Pa·s, consistent with normal‑mode estimates.

Inversion Results and Viscosity Estimates

Bayesian inversion of a suite of 30 normal modes (including toroidal and spheroidal) with a prior on η_IC from mineral physics produces a posterior distribution peaked at η_IC = 3 × 10¹⁴ Pa·s (95 % credible interval 1–8 × 10¹⁴ Pa·s). The inversion simultaneously solves for inner‑core anisotropy (ξ ≈ 0.03) and differential rotation (ΔΩ ≈ 0.1°/yr), showing that viscosity and anisotropy are partially correlated but distinguishable because they affect different mode families.

Implications for Inner‑Core Dynamics and Geodynamo Coupling

A viscosity of 10¹⁴–10¹⁵ Pa·s implies a viscous relaxation time τ = η/μ ≈ 10⁴–10⁵ yr (μ ≈ 1.5 × 10¹¹ Pa). This is short compared to the age of the inner core (~1 Ga), allowing the inner core to deform viscously in response to gravitational coupling with the mantle and electromagnetic torque from the geodynamo. The resulting differential rotation and translational motion (the “Slichter mode”) can modulate the length‑of‑day at the 0.1 ms level, a signal sought in modern VLBI and GNSS data.

Future Observational Prospects

Next‑generation gravimeters (e.g., atom‑interferometric) and the deployment of ocean‑bottom broadband seismometers will improve normal‑mode Q measurements by a factor of 3–5. Simultaneous inversion of normal modes, body‑wave attenuation (Q_ScS), and seismic anisotropy will tighten η_IC constraints to < 30 % uncertainty, enabling a decisive test of whether the inner core behaves as a Newtonian fluid or exhibits non‑Newtonian rheology.

  • Assuming that a single data type (e.g., seismic velocities alone) uniquely determines lithospheric composition; joint inversion with gravity and magnetics is essential to separate temperature and composition effects.
  • Treating the Cretaceous Normal Superchron as a period of high reversal frequency; it is a zero‑reversal interval lasting ~38 Myr.
  • Equating inner‑core viscosity derived from normal‑mode Q values directly to laboratory measurements without accounting for the different deformation mechanisms (dislocation vs. diffusion creep) and grain‑size effects.
  • Ignoring the frequency dependence of seismic attenuation when using Q to infer melt fraction; melt‑induced attenuation peaks at seismic frequencies, biasing temperature estimates.
  • Over‑relying on a single normal mode (e.g., ₀S₂) to constrain inner‑core viscosity; a suite of modes with varied inner‑core sensitivity is required to break trade‑offs with anisotropy and rotation.
  • Assuming that magnetic anomalies solely reflect induced magnetization; remanent magnetization acquired during cooling can dominate the signal, especially in cratonic regions.
  • Neglecting the different depth sensitivities of ambient‑noise surface waves (upper 100 km) and body‑wave normal modes (whole Earth) when jointly inverting for inner‑core properties.

Seismic Imaging of the D'' Layer Using ScS and Sdiff Phases

The D'' region, the lowermost 200–300 km of the mantle, is a primary target for high‑frequency body‑wave studies because it hosts the post‑perovskite phase transition, strong lateral velocity gradients, and pronounced seismic anisotropy. ScS (a shear wave reflected from the core‑mantle boundary) and Sdiff (a diffracted shear wave that grazes the CMB) provide complementary constraints: ScS samples the D'' layer at near‑vertical incidence, while Sdiff samples it at grazing angles, making the two phases sensitive to different components of the elastic tensor. Travel‑time residuals of ScS relative to a 1‑D reference model (e.g., PREM) are typically 1–3 s, with systematic positive residuals beneath the Pacific Large Low Shear Velocity Province (LLSVP) and negative residuals beneath the African LLSVP. These residuals map directly onto shear‑velocity perturbations of ±2–3 % in the lowermost 150 km. Sdiff amplitudes and waveforms, measured on broadband arrays such as the Global Seismographic Network, reveal a sharp velocity increase of ~1.5 % at the top of the post‑perovskite stability field, consistent with mineral‑physics predictions for a MgSiO₃ transition at ~125 GPa and 2500 K. Joint inversion of ScS and Sdiff travel times with normal‑mode splitting functions yields a D'' thickness that varies laterally from ~150 km under the Pacific to ~250 km under Africa, and a radial anisotropy parameter ξ = (Vsh/Vsv)² that reaches 1.08 beneath the Pacific LLSVP, indicating horizontal shear‑wave speed exceeding vertical speed. The anisotropy pattern correlates with the orientation of subducted slab remnants imaged by P‑wave tomography, suggesting that lattice‑preferred orientation of post‑perovskite crystals records the integrated mantle flow history. Remaining challenges include trade‑offs between temperature, composition, and anisotropy, and the limited azimuthal coverage of Sdiff paths, which future ocean‑bottom seismometer deployments aim to improve.

Geophysical Constraints on Outer‑Core Stratification from Seismic and Magnetic Observations

The outer core is not a perfectly homogeneous convecting fluid; seismic and magnetic data together indicate a stably stratified layer at the top of the outer core, often termed the “F‑layer,” with a thickness of 100–300 km. Body‑wave phases that traverse the outer core — PKIKP, PKPbc, PKPdf, and the SmKS family — exhibit travel‑time anomalies and waveform complexities that cannot be explained by a purely adiabatic velocity profile. In particular, the differential travel time between PKPbc and PKPdf (the “PKP differential”) shows a systematic increase of ~0.2 s for rays that sample the uppermost 200 km of the outer core beneath the Pacific, implying a reduction of the P‑wave velocity gradient by ~0.02 s⁻¹ relative to PREM. Normal‑mode centre frequencies of the ₀S₂ and ₀T₂ modes, which have significant kinetic energy in the outer core, are shifted by up to 0.3 µHz, a shift reproduced only when a low‑velocity, high‑viscosity layer is included in the model. Magnetic secular variation and geomagnetic jerks provide an independent constraint: rapid changes in the secular acceleration (e.g., the 2003 and 2007 jerks) require a reduction of the magnetic diffusion time across the top of the core, which is achieved if the electrical conductivity is lowered by ~10 % in a stratified layer enriched in light elements (O, S, Si). Electromagnetic induction studies using satellite (Swarm) and ground observatory data infer a conductivity profile that drops from ~1.0 × 10⁶ S m⁻¹ in the bulk outer core to ~0.8 × 10⁶ S m⁻¹ in the upper 150 km, consistent with a compositional gradient produced by the preferential partitioning of light elements into the solidifying inner core. Joint Bayesian inversion of the seismic travel‑time dataset (≈12 000 PKP differential measurements) and the magnetic induction dataset (monthly spherical‑harmonic coefficients up to degree 13) yields a posterior distribution for the F‑layer thickness peaked at 180 km (95 % credible interval 120–250 km) and a light‑element concentration increase of 0.5–0.8 wt % relative to the bulk outer core. This stratified layer damps small‑scale convection, modifies the torque balance between the mantle and core, and must be accounted for in geodynamo simulations that aim to reproduce the observed westward drift of the magnetic field.

Worked Example: Estimating D'' Thickness from ScS Residuals
A set of ScS travel‑time residuals measured at stations in the western Pacific averages +2.1 s relative to PREM. Assuming a simple two‑layer model where the lowermost mantle has a uniform shear‑velocity reduction ΔVs/Vs = –2.5 % over a thickness H, and the ray path through D'' is approximately vertical, estimate H. Use the reference shear velocity Vs₀ = 7.3 km s⁻¹ at the base of the mantle.
The travel‑time perturbation for a vertical ray crossing a layer of thickness H with fractional velocity change δ = ΔVs/Vs is Δt ≈ –δ · H / Vs₀. Rearranging gives H ≈ –Δt · Vs₀ / δ. Substituting Δt = +2.1 s (positive residual means slower wave, so δ is negative), Vs₀ = 7.3 km s⁻¹, and δ = –0.025 yields H ≈ 2.1 × 7.3 / 0.025 ≈ 613 km. This simple estimate exceeds the expected D'' thickness, indicating that the assumption of a uniform –2.5 % reduction over the whole layer is too large; a more realistic model distributes the velocity anomaly over a thinner region (≈150–200 km) with a larger local reduction (≈–5 %). The exercise illustrates the trade‑off between anomaly amplitude and layer thickness that must be resolved by joint inversion with Sdiff and normal‑mode data.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks you to infer a layer thickness from travel‑time residuals, first identify the ray geometry (vertical vs. grazing) and the reference velocity. Write the linearised relation Δt = –(ΔV/V)·(path length)/V₀, then solve for the unknown. Remember that a positive residual corresponds to a slower velocity (negative ΔV/V). Always check whether the result is physically plausible; if not, discuss the trade‑off between anomaly magnitude and thickness, and mention the need for multi‑phase or normal‑mode constraints.

Seismic Imaging of the Subduction Zone Interface Using Seismic Reflection and Converted Phases

The megathrust interface that accommodates the largest earthquakes on Earth is imaged by a combination of wide‑angle seismic reflection, converted‑phase (Ps, Sp) receiver‑function analysis, and high‑resolution tomography. In the Nankai Trough, Japan, a dense ocean‑bottom seismometer (OBS) array recorded reflected P‑to‑P (PmP) and P‑to‑S (PmS) arrivals from the plate boundary at depths of 10–30 km. The PmP reflection coefficient, after correcting for geometrical spreading and attenuation, yields an impedance contrast of 0.12–0.18, consistent with a fluid‑rich, low‑velocity zone (Vp ≈ 5.5 km s⁻¹, Vs ≈ 3.0 km s⁻¹) overlying the subducting oceanic crust (Vp ≈ 6.8 km s⁻¹). Converted Ps phases from the same interface arrive 0.8–1.2 s after the direct P, and their move‑out with offset constrains the dip of the interface to 8–12° landward. In the Cascadia margin, onshore broadband stations combined with offshore OBS data provide receiver‑function stacks that image a double‑layered interface: a shallow, high‑Vp/Vs (≈1.85) zone interpreted as a sediment‑rich décollement, and a deeper, lower‑Vp/Vs (≈1.70) zone marking the top of the oceanic crust. The thickness of the high‑Vp/Vs layer correlates with the interseismic coupling coefficient derived from GPS: strongly coupled segments (coupling >0.8) exhibit a thin (<2 km) décollement, whereas weakly coupled segments show a thick (>5 km) sedimentary layer. Seismic attenuation (Qp⁻¹) measured from the spectral decay of reflected phases is elevated (Qp ≈ 80) in the décollement, supporting the presence of overpressured fluids that reduce effective normal stress. Joint inversion of reflection amplitudes, Ps move‑out, and Qp estimates yields a pore‑fluid pressure ratio λ* = (Pf – Phydro)/(Plith – Phydro) of 0.6–0.85 beneath the locked zone, a key parameter for physics‑based earthquake cycle simulations. Future dense OBS deployments and full‑waveform inversion of the reflected wavefield promise to resolve along‑strike variations in interface roughness and fluid distribution at kilometre scales.

Geophysical Constraints on the Earth's Deep Water Cycle from Seismic Velocity and Electrical Conductivity

Water stored in nominally anhydrous minerals (NAMs) and as free fluid in the mantle transition zone (MTZ) and lower mantle influences seismic velocities, electrical conductivity, and rheology. Global shear‑wave tomography shows a broad low‑Vs anomaly (–1.5 % to –2.5 %) centred at 520–660 km depth beneath the western Pacific, spatially coincident with a high‑conductivity anomaly (σ ≈ 0.1–0.3 S m⁻¹) inferred from electromagnetic induction using satellite (Swarm) and ground magnetometer data. Laboratory measurements on wadsleyite and ringwoodite at 15–20 GPa and 1500–1800 K demonstrate that 0.5–1.0 wt % H₂O reduces Vs by 1.5–2.5 % and increases conductivity by two orders of magnitude relative to dry samples. Joint inversion of the seismic Vs model (parameterised by spherical harmonics up to degree 30) and the conductivity model (parameterised by a 1‑D radial profile with lateral perturbations) using a petrological forward model that links water content to Vs and σ via the Arrhenius‑type conductivity law yields a water concentration of 0.3–0.6 wt % in the MTZ beneath the Pacific, corresponding to a total water mass of 0.5–1.0 × 10²¹ kg — comparable to the mass of the world’s oceans. Dehydration reactions at the 660‑km discontinuity (ringwoodite → bridgmanite + ferropericlase) release water that may flux into the lower mantle, where high‑pressure experiments on bridgmanite indicate a solubility of <0.1 wt % at 25 GPa, implying that most water is either trapped in defect structures or transported upward by mantle upwellings. Electrical conductivity profiles from magnetotelluric (MT) arrays across the Altiplano‑Puna volcanic complex reveal a high‑conductivity layer (σ ≈ 1 S m⁻¹) at 80–120 km depth, interpreted as a melt‑water mixture sourced from the MTZ. The combined seismic‑electromagnetic approach thus quantifies the deep water budget, links it to surface volcanism, and constrains the rheological weakening that facilitates plate‑boundary localisation.

Seismic Wave Propagation in Transversely Isotropic Media with a Vertical Symmetry Axis (VTI) and Crustal Applications

Many crustal and upper‑mantle rocks exhibit transverse isotropy with a vertical symmetry axis (VTI) due to aligned cracks, layering, or lattice‑preferred orientation of minerals such as mica and amphibole. The elastic behaviour of a VTI medium is described by five independent stiffness coefficients (C₁₁, C₃₃, C₄₄, C₆₆, C₁₃) or, equivalently, by the Thomsen parameters ε, δ, γ, and the vertical P‑ and S‑wave velocities Vp₀, Vs₀. For weak anisotropy (|ε|,|δ|,|γ| ≪ 1), the phase velocities of quasi‑P (qP) and quasi‑SV (qSV) waves as a function of propagation angle θ from the vertical are: VqP(θ) ≈ Vp₀[1 + ε sin²θ cos²θ + δ sin⁴θ] and VqSV(θ) ≈ Vs₀[1 + (ε – δ) sin²θ cos²θ]. The quasi‑SH wave velocity is VqSH(θ) ≈ Vs₀[1 + γ sin²θ]. In the crust, typical values are ε = 0.10–0.20, δ = –0.05–0.05, γ = 0.05–0.15, reflecting aligned microcracks (positive ε, negative δ) or sedimentary layering (positive ε, positive δ). Shear‑wave splitting measurements from local earthquakes in the Basin and Range province show fast‑polarisation directions parallel to the maximum horizontal compressive stress (SHmax) and delay times of 0.05–0.15 s for paths of 20–30 km, consistent with a crack‑density parameter ε ≈ 0.12. In the upper mantle beneath stable cratons, SKS splitting yields fast directions parallel to absolute plate motion and delay times of 1.0–1.5 s, which are reproduced by a VTI layer of 150 km thickness with γ ≈ 0.03, implying lattice‑preferred orientation of olivine with the [100] axis aligned horizontally. Joint inversion of surface‑wave phase velocities (Love and Rayleigh) and body‑wave travel times (Pn, Sn) using a VTI parameterisation resolves the depth dependence of ε and δ, revealing a strong positive ε in the upper 30 km (crack‑dominated) that decays to near zero at 50 km, while δ remains slightly negative throughout the crust, indicating a persistent influence of compliant pores. These VTI models are essential for accurate depth migration of reflection seismic data, for correcting anisotropy‑induced bias in earthquake location, and for interpreting the mechanical anisotropy that controls fault nucleation and propagation.

  • Assuming that a single seismic phase (e.g., ScS) uniquely determines D'' thickness without accounting for trade‑offs with anisotropy and temperature.
  • Treating the outer‑core F‑layer as a purely thermal boundary layer; compositional stratification from light‑element partitioning is required to match both seismic and magnetic data.
  • Equating high Vp/Vs in a subduction décollement solely to fluid overpressure; sediment composition and porosity also raise Vp/Vs.
  • Neglecting the frequency dependence of electrical conductivity when inferring water content from magnetotelluric data; laboratory conductivity is measured at 0.01–1 Hz, while MT periods span 0.001–10⁴ s.
  • Using isotropic velocity models for crustal imaging in regions with known VTI anisotropy; this introduces systematic depth errors of 5–10 % in reflection migration.
  • Over‑interpreting a single shear‑wave splitting measurement as the full anisotropy tensor; a suite of back‑azimuths and incidence angles is needed to separate VTI from HTI (horizontal transverse isotropy) contributions.
  • Assuming that the deep water cycle is closed at the 660‑km discontinuity; experimental evidence shows limited water solubility in lower‑mantle minerals, implying a net flux to the surface over geological time.

These advanced topics illustrate how multi‑observable geophysical inversions, laboratory mineral physics, and wave‑propagation theory combine to resolve Earth’s deep structure and dynamics.

Seismic Imaging of Subduction Megathrusts Using Ocean‑Bottom Seismometer Arrays and High‑Resolution Reflection Profiling

Subduction megathrusts generate the largest earthquakes on Earth, yet their shallow locked zone and deeper transition to stable sliding remain poorly resolved by land‑based networks alone. Deployments of dense ocean‑bottom seismometer (OBS) arrays — typically 30–100 instruments spaced 5–15 km apart — combined with multi‑channel seismic (MCS) reflection profiling provide complementary constraints: OBS record local and teleseismic body‑wave arrivals with high signal‑to‑noise ratios, while MCS images the plate‑interface reflector and fault‑zone architecture at metre‑scale vertical resolution. Joint travel‑time tomography of OBS P‑ and S‑wave arrivals yields a three‑dimensional Vp/Vs model that delineates the locked patch (high Vp/Vs, low Vp) from the creeping segment (lower Vp/Vs). Simultaneously, pre‑stack depth migration of MCS data maps the décollement geometry, subducted sediment thickness, and the presence of splay faults. In the Nankai Trough, such integrated studies have identified a 30‑km‑wide locked zone beneath the Kumano Basin, a shallow low‑velocity zone interpreted as overpressured sediments, and a deeper high‑velocity body marking the subducted Philippine Sea Plate crust. The combined model predicts a slip‑deficit accumulation rate of 3–4 cm yr⁻¹, consistent with geodetic coupling estimates. In the Cascadia margin, OBS‑MCS integration revealed a segmented megathrust with alternating locked and creeping patches that correlate with variations in subducted sediment thickness and the presence of a subducted seamount chain. These results demonstrate that the spatial resolution of megathrust locking is fundamentally limited by the offshore observational aperture; expanding OBS coverage and acquiring wider‑azimuth MCS lines remain the most effective strategy for reducing seismic‑hazard uncertainty.

Joint Inversion of Seismic, Gravity, and Magnetic Data for Lithospheric Architecture

Individual geophysical datasets suffer from non‑uniqueness: seismic velocities are sensitive to temperature, composition, and partial melt; gravity anomalies respond to density contrasts that can be produced by thermal or compositional variations; magnetic anomalies reflect the distribution of ferromagnetic minerals, which is controlled by crustal composition and thermal history. Joint inversion mitigates these trade‑offs by enforcing a shared structural model — typically a set of layer interfaces and physical‑property fields (Vp, Vs, density, magnetic susceptibility) — that simultaneously fits travel‑time residuals, Bouguer gravity, and total‑field magnetic data. The forward problem couples a seismic ray‑tracing or finite‑difference wave‑propagation engine with a 3‑D gravity‑magnetic potential‑field solver. Regularisation is applied through a block‑diagonal model covariance that encodes expected correlations (e.g., higher density correlates with higher Vp in mafic rocks). In practice, the inversion proceeds iteratively: a starting model from seismic tomography is perturbed, gravity and magnetic responses are computed, and a Gauss‑Newton update minimizes the joint misfit functional Φ = Φ_seis + λ_g Φ_grav + λ_m Φ_mag. Applications to the western United States have resolved the thickness of the Basin and Range crust (30–35 km), the depth to the Moho beneath the Colorado Plateau (45 km), and the lateral extent of the mid‑crustal low‑velocity zone associated with partial melt. In the Tibetan Plateau, joint inversion of receiver‑function constraints, satellite gravity (GRACE‑FO), and aeromagnetic data delineated a high‑density, high‑susceptibility lower crustal body beneath the Lhasa terrane, interpreted as eclogitized lower crust. The method also quantifies model uncertainty via the posterior covariance, revealing that density is best resolved in the upper crust (σ_ρ ≈ 30 kg m⁻³) while magnetic susceptibility remains poorly constrained below 20 km depth. These multi‑observable inversions are now standard for constructing lithospheric‑scale models that honor all available geophysical evidence.

Worked Example: Designing a Joint Inversion Weighting Scheme
A researcher has three data sets for a 200 km × 200 km region: 1 200 P‑wave travel‑time residuals (σ_t = 0.05 s), 500 Bouguer gravity observations (σ_g = 2 mGal), and 400 total‑field magnetic measurements (σ_m = 5 nT). The goal is to choose λ_g and λ_m so that each data type contributes roughly equally to the total misfit at the start of the inversion. Assuming the initial model predicts RMS residuals equal to the data uncertainties, compute λ_g and λ_m.
The contribution of each data set to the misfit is Φ_i = Σ (r_i/σ_i)². With N_i observations and RMS residual = σ_i, each term contributes N_i. Thus Φ_seis = 1 200, Φ_grav = 500, Φ_mag = 400. To equalise contributions we need λ_g Φ_grav = Φ_seis and λ_m Φ_mag = Φ_seis. Hence λ_g = Φ_seis / Φ_grav = 1 200 / 500 = 2.4. λ_m = Φ_seis / Φ_mag = 1 200 / 400 = 3.0. Using these weights the initial joint misfit is Φ_total = Φ_seis + λ_g Φ_grav + λ_m Φ_mag = 1 200 + 2.4×500 + 3.0×400 = 1 200 + 1 200 + 1 200 = 3 600, with each component contributing 1 200.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the rationale behind joint inversion, emphasise the complementary sensitivities of each data type and the reduction of non‑uniqueness. Be ready to write the generic misfit functional and explain how weighting parameters are chosen from data uncertainties. A quick sketch of the block‑diagonal covariance matrix earns partial credit even if the full derivation is not required.

Seismic Anisotropy in the D'' Layer and Its Implications for Core‑Mantle Interaction

The D'' region, the lowermost 200–300 km of the mantle, exhibits strong seismic anisotropy that differs markedly from the overlying lower mantle. Shear‑wave splitting of ScS and S‑diff phases recorded at epicentral distances of 70°–90° shows fast‑polarisation directions that rotate with azimuth, indicating a transversely isotropic symmetry axis that is not purely vertical. In the Pacific Large Low Shear Velocity Province (LLSVP), the fast direction aligns roughly with the plate‑motion vector of the overlying Pacific Plate, suggesting that mantle flow at the base of the mantle is coupled to plate‑driven circulation. In contrast, beneath the African LLSVP the fast direction is more variable, reflecting a complex flow pattern possibly influenced by the proximity of the core‑mantle boundary (CMB) heat‑flux anomalies. Mineral‑physics experiments on post‑perovskite (MgSiO₃) at CMB pressures (125–135 GPa) and temperatures (2500–3000 K) demonstrate that lattice‑preferred orientation (LPO) of post‑perovskite develops a strong [100]‑axis alignment in the plane of shear, producing a shear‑wave anisotropy of 2–4 % with the fast polarisation parallel to the flow direction. Numerical models of mantle convection that incorporate a viscously weak post‑perovskite layer reproduce the observed splitting magnitudes when the CMB heat flux is laterally heterogeneous by ±10 mW m⁻². The anisotropy also affects the propagation of PKP and PKIKP phases that sample the outer core; differential travel‑time residuals between polar and equatorial paths can be partially explained by D'' anisotropy rather than inner‑core rotation alone. Consequently, any inference of inner‑core differential rotation must first correct for the D'' contribution using a joint inversion of ScS splitting and PKP differential times. The emerging consensus is that D'' anisotropy records the integrated effect of basal mantle flow, compositional layering (e.g., iron‑enriched post‑perovskite), and thermal boundary‑layer instabilities, providing a unique window on core‑mantle coupling.

Geophysical Constraints on the Earth's Mantle Viscosity from Post‑Seismic Viscoelastic Relaxation and Afterslip Modeling

Large megathrust earthquakes (Mw ≥ 8.5) generate measurable surface deformation for years to decades after the mainshock. Continuous GPS and InSAR time series capture the combined signal of afterslip on the fault plane and viscoelastic relaxation in the asthenosphere and upper mantle. Disentangling these contributions requires a rheologically layered Earth model: an elastic lithosphere (thickness 80–120 km), a Maxwell viscoelastic asthenosphere (viscosity η_asth), and a deeper mantle with a higher viscosity (η_lm). Forward models compute the surface displacement Green’s functions for a given slip distribution and viscosity structure using the correspondence principle in the Laplace domain. Inversion of the observed post‑seismic time series (e.g., the 2011 Tohoku‑Oki Mw 9.0 event) yields η_asth ≈ 1–3 × 10¹⁸ Pa s and η_lm ≈ 1–5 × 10²¹ Pa s, with the asthenospheric viscosity showing a strong lateral variation beneath the Japanese arc (lower beneath the back‑arc). The afterslip component is parameterised as a rate‑and‑state friction law on the downdip extension of the coseismic rupture; its moment release typically accounts for 10–30 % of the mainshock moment in the first year. Joint inversion of GPS horizontal and vertical velocities, InSAR line‑of‑sight displacements, and repeating‑earthquake slip rates constrains the depth extent of afterslip (30–50 km) and the effective viscosity of the asthenosphere. A key trade‑off exists between a low‑viscosity asthenosphere with limited afterslip and a higher‑viscosity asthenosphere with extensive afterslip; this is resolved by incorporating far‑field GPS sites (>1000 km) that are sensitive primarily to mantle relaxation. The resulting viscosity profile is consistent with laboratory‑derived dislocation‑creep flow laws for olivine at 1300–1500 K, supporting a thermally activated rheology. These post‑seismic studies provide the most direct, time‑dependent constraints on mantle viscosity at spatial scales of 100–1000 km, complementing the long‑wavelength constraints from glacial isostatic adjustment.

Seismic Wave Propagation in Transversely Isotropic Media with a Horizontal Symmetry Axis (HTI) and Fracture Characterization

While vertical transverse isotropy (VTI) dominates in layered sedimentary basins, many crustal and reservoir settings exhibit a horizontal symmetry axis (HTI) caused by aligned vertical fractures or stress‑induced microcracks. The HTI stiffness tensor has five independent parameters (C₁₁, C₃₃, C₄₄, C₆₆, C₁₃) but the symmetry axis lies in the horizontal plane, so the Thomsen‑style parameters become ε^(H), δ^(H), γ^(H) defined with respect to the fracture strike. For weak anisotropy, the quasi‑P phase velocity varies with azimuth φ as VqP(φ) ≈ Vp₀[1 + ε^(H) cos²φ sin²θ + δ^(H) sin⁴θ cos²φ], where θ is the incidence angle from vertical. The quasi‑SV and quasi‑SH velocities split as a function of azimuth, producing shear‑wave splitting with a fast polarisation parallel to the fracture strike. In practice, multi‑azimuth walkaway VSP or surface‑seismic surveys acquire data over a full 360° azimuthal range. Azimuthal variation of P‑wave NMO velocity yields ε^(H) and δ^(H) via the generalized NMO equation: Vnmo²(φ) = Vp₀²[1 + 2ε^(H) cos²φ + 2δ^(H) sin²φ]. Shear‑wave splitting analysis of converted‑wave (PS) data provides γ^(H) and the fracture orientation. Laboratory measurements on cracked rock samples show that ε^(H) scales with fracture density parameter e = (π/4) n a³ (n = number density, a = half‑length) while δ^(H) is sensitive to fracture aspect ratio and fluid saturation. In the Barnett Shale, HTI analysis of 3‑D seismic data revealed ε^(H) = 0.12, δ^(H) = –0.04, γ^(H) = 0.08, and a fracture strike of N30°E, consistent with borehole image logs. The negative δ^(H) indicates compliant, fluid‑filled fractures that reduce the normal stiffness more than the shear stiffness. These parameters feed directly into discrete‑fracture‑network models for hydraulic‑fracture design and reservoir‑stimulation planning. Moreover, HTI anisotropy must be accounted for in depth migration of seismic data; ignoring it introduces lateral positioning errors of up to 200 m at 3 km depth for typical fracture densities.

  • Assuming that a single OBS deployment can resolve the full downdip extent of megathrust locking without complementary MCS reflection data.
  • Treating gravity and magnetic data as independent constraints in a joint inversion without modelling their petrophysical coupling (density–susceptibility relationships).
  • Equating observed D'' shear‑wave splitting solely to post‑perovskite LPO; ignoring the possible contribution of shape‑preferred orientation of iron‑rich melt pockets.
  • Using a uniform Maxwell viscosity for the entire mantle when interpreting post‑seismic deformation; lateral viscosity variations are required to fit far‑field GPS.
  • Neglecting azimuthal coverage in seismic acquisition and then applying an isotropic migration algorithm in a known HTI reservoir; this produces systematic lateral mispositioning of reflectors.
  • Over‑interpreting a single shear‑wave splitting measurement as the complete anisotropy tensor; a suite of back‑azimuths and incidence angles is needed to separate VTI from HTI contributions.
  • Assuming that the deep water cycle is closed at the 660‑km discontinuity; experimental evidence shows limited water solubility in lower‑mantle minerals, implying a net flux to the surface over geological time.

Seismic Wave Propagation in Tilted Transversely Isotropic Media and Subsurface Imaging

When the symmetry axis of a transversely isotropic (TI) medium is neither vertical (VTI) nor horizontal (HTI) but tilted by an angle θ from the vertical, the medium is described as tilted transversely isotropic (TTI). The stiffness tensor retains five independent elastic constants, but the rotation introduces coupling between the quasi‑P and quasi‑SV wavefields that is absent in VTI or HTI. In the weak‑anisotropy approximation the phase velocity of the quasi‑P wave for a propagation direction defined by polar angle ψ (from the symmetry axis) and azimuth φ (around the symmetry axis) can be written as

VqP(ψ,φ) ≈ VP0[1 + ε sin²ψ + δ sin²ψ cos²ψ],

where ε and δ are the Thomsen parameters defined in the symmetry‑axis coordinate system. The tilt angle θ enters through the transformation of the propagation vector from the acquisition (vertical) frame to the symmetry‑axis frame. This transformation makes the move‑out of reflected P‑waves on a common‑midpoint gather depend on both offset and azimuth, producing non‑hyperbolic move‑out that cannot be fitted with a simple VTI NMO equation.

In practice, TTI models are essential for imaging beneath steeply dipping salt bodies, overthrust belts, and basaltic sequences where the symmetry axis aligns with the bedding or foliation that is itself tilted. Depth migration algorithms that incorporate TTI kinematics (e.g., TTI reverse‑time migration) use the full anisotropic dispersion relation to extrapolate wavefields. The additional parameters — tilt angle θ and azimuth of the tilt plane φtilt — are typically estimated from well logs, check‑shot surveys, or by anisotropic tomography of wide‑azimuth surface seismic data. Errors in the tilt estimate of 5° can shift the imaged reflector position by several hundred metres at 3 km depth, a magnitude comparable to the salt‑flank imaging error budget.

Shear‑wave splitting in TTI media provides an independent constraint on the tilt. The fast shear‑wave polarisation aligns with the projection of the symmetry axis onto the plane perpendicular to the propagation direction. Multi‑azimuth VSP or ocean‑bottom‑node (OBN) surveys that record both PP and PS arrivals allow joint inversion for ε, δ, γ, θ, and φtilt. Laboratory measurements on shale cores confirm that ε correlates with the degree of clay‑particle alignment, while δ is sensitive to the aspect ratio of micro‑cracks. In the Gulf of Mexico subsalt plays, TTI migration has reduced the residual depth error from >300 m (VTI) to <80 m, directly impacting drilling‑target placement.

Core‑Mantle Boundary Topography from Normal‑Mode Splitting and Its Geodynamic Implications

Free oscillations of the Earth (normal modes) are standing waves that sample the entire planet. Each mode is characterized by its angular order l, radial order n, and type (spheroidal S or toroidal T). In a laterally homogeneous Earth, modes of the same n and l are degenerate — they share a single eigenfrequency. Lateral heterogeneity, including topography on the core‑mantle boundary (CMB), lifts this degeneracy and splits each multiplet into 2l+1 singlets. The splitting pattern is described by the splitting function s(θ,φ) which can be expanded in spherical harmonics. The coefficients of degree‑2 and degree‑3 harmonics are most sensitive to CMB topography because the eigenfunctions of low‑order spheroidal modes (e.g., 0S₂, 0S₃) have large amplitudes at the CMB.

Forward modelling shows that a 1 km peak‑to‑peak CMB undulation produces a frequency shift of order 10 nHz for the 0S₂ mode, well within the resolution of modern global seismic networks (e.g., GSN, GEOSCOPE). Inversion of observed splitting functions for the 0S₂–0S₆ multiplets yields a CMB topography model with a dominant degree‑2 component of ~400 m amplitude, consistent with the “African” and “Pacific” large low‑shear‑velocity provinces (LLSVPs) acting as thermal piles that depress the boundary. The degree‑3 component (~150 m) correlates with the locations of known mantle plumes (Hawaii, Iceland, Afar), suggesting that plume‑root buoyancy locally elevates the CMB.

Geodynamically, the inferred topography provides a boundary condition for mantle convection models. A depressed CMB beneath LLSVPs implies a negative buoyancy flux that stabilises the piles, while elevated regions beneath plumes indicate positive buoyancy flux that drives upwelling. Coupled core‑mantle evolution models that assimilate the normal‑mode topography predict a heat‑flux heterogeneity of ±15 mW m⁻² across the CMB, sufficient to modulate the geodynamo and produce the observed secular variation pattern (e.g., the South Atlantic Anomaly). The normal‑mode approach is complementary to body‑wave tomography (PKP, PKIKP) because it samples the CMB globally with uniform sensitivity, avoiding the uneven ray coverage that plagues body‑wave studies.

Technique: Normal‑Mode Splitting Inversion for CMB Topography
A student is given the observed splitting coefficients for the 0S₂ multiplet: the degree‑2 coefficient c₂₀ = 12 nHz and the degree‑3 coefficient c₃₀ = 4 nHz. Assuming the sensitivity kernel for 0S₂ gives a linear relation Δf = K₂ h₂ + K₃ h₃, where h₂ and h₃ are the spherical‑harmonic amplitudes of CMB topography (in km), and K₂ = 8 nHz km⁻¹, K₃ = 5 nHz km⁻¹. Estimate h₂ and h₃.
The splitting coefficients are directly the frequency shifts: Δf₂ = c₂₀ = 12 nHz, Δf₃ = c₃₀ = 4 nHz. Solve the linear system: 12 = 8 h₂ → h₂ = 1.5 km, 4 = 5 h₃ → h₃ = 0.8 km. Thus the degree‑2 CMB topography amplitude is 1.5 km (peak‑to‑peak ≈ 3 km) and the degree‑3 amplitude is 0.8 km (peak‑to‑peak ≈ 1.6 km). These values are larger than typical published models, indicating that the assumed kernels may be oversimplified or that the observed splitting includes contributions from mantle heterogeneity as well.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a quantitative estimate from normal‑mode splitting, first identify the mode, the relevant spherical‑harmonic degrees, and the published sensitivity kernels. Write the linear relation explicitly, substitute the given numbers, and solve. Always comment on the non‑uniqueness: mantle heterogeneity and inner‑core anisotropy also contribute to splitting, so a pure CMB topography interpretation requires joint inversion or a priori constraints.

Distributed Acoustic Sensing with Fiber‑Optic Arrays for Crustal Seismic Imaging

Distributed acoustic sensing (DAS) converts a standard telecom fiber‑optic cable into a dense array of strain‑rate sensors by interrogating Rayleigh backscatter with a coherent laser. The gauge length (typically 10–50 m) defines the spatial averaging interval, and the channel spacing (often 1–2 m) yields thousands of virtual sensors over tens of kilometres. Unlike conventional geophones, DAS measures axial strain (or strain‑rate) along the cable axis, making it sensitive to both P‑ and S‑wave particle motion projected onto the fiber direction. The response is broadband (0.1 Hz–10 kHz) and the dynamic range exceeds 120 dB, allowing detection of microseismicity (ML < 0) and strong ground motion in the same recording.

In crustal imaging, DAS deployments on existing dark‑fiber telecom cables have produced high‑resolution ambient‑noise tomography of the upper crust. Cross‑correlation of continuous strain records between channel pairs yields empirical Green’s functions that contain surface‑wave dispersion curves. Because the channel spacing is an order of magnitude finer than typical seismic arrays, the resulting dispersion maps resolve shear‑velocity structure at 100‑m lateral scales, revealing fault‑zone damage zones, sedimentary basin edges, and shallow magma conduits. In the Imperial Valley, California, a 30‑km DAS array imaged a 2‑km‑wide low‑velocity zone associated with the Brawley Seismic Zone that was invisible in conventional 1‑km‑spaced station data.

Active‑source DAS surveys use a vibroseis or weight‑drop source and record the strain response along the fiber. The dense spatial sampling enables full‑waveform inversion (FWI) with a spatial Nyquist wavelength far below the dominant source wavelength, mitigating cycle‑skipping. A 2019 pilot in the North Sea used a 12‑km fiber laid on the seafloor to image a shallow gas‑chimney complex; the FWI model recovered velocity anomalies of ±15 % at 200‑m resolution, matching well‑log data. DAS also excels at monitoring induced seismicity: the continuous, high‑density recordings allow precise relative relocation of micro‑earthquakes via double‑difference algorithms, illuminating fault activation patterns during hydraulic‑fracturing operations.

Key limitations include the directional sensitivity (only strain along the fiber), coupling variability (buried vs. surface‑laid cable), and the large data volumes (terabytes per day) that demand efficient edge‑computing workflows. Recent advances in interrogator technology (phase‑sensitive OTDR, coherent detection) and machine‑learning‑based noise suppression are expanding the usable bandwidth and reducing the gauge‑length trade‑off.

Swarm Satellite Constellation and High‑Resolution Geomagnetic Field Modeling

The European Space Agency’s Swarm mission, launched in November 2013, consists of three identical satellites (Alpha, Bravo, Charlie) in near‑polar orbits at altitudes of 460–530 km. Each satellite carries a vector field magnetometer (VFM) with a noise floor of 50 pT Hz⁻¹ᐟ², an absolute scalar magnetometer (ASM) for calibration, a GPS receiver for precise orbit determination, and an electric field instrument. The constellation geometry — two satellites flying side‑by‑side at ~1.4° longitudinal separation and a third in a higher, drifting orbit — provides simultaneous measurements at different spatial scales, enabling separation of core, lithospheric, ionospheric, and magnetospheric field contributions.

Core field modeling benefits from the low‑altitude, high‑precision vector data. The Swarm Level‑2 product “MCO_SHA2C” provides spherical‑harmonic coefficients up to degree 130 (≈300 km wavelength) for the static core field and its secular variation (SV) up to degree 18. The SV model reveals rapid, localized changes — geomagnetic jerks — with amplitudes of 10–20 nT yr⁻¹ at the Earth’s surface, corresponding to core‑surface flow accelerations of ~10 km yr⁻¹. Joint inversion of Swarm vector data with ground observatory records (e.g., INTERMAGNET) yields a time‑dependent core field model (CHAOS‑7) that resolves SV down to 6‑month intervals, a factor of two improvement over pre‑Swarm models.

Lithospheric field mapping exploits the high‑degree coefficients (degree 15–130) where the core field is negligible. Swarm’s along‑track gradient measurements (difference between Alpha and Bravo) suppress the large‑scale core field, enhancing the crustal anomaly signal. The resulting “Swarm Lithospheric Magnetic Model” (SWARM_LM) resolves magnetic anomalies associated with oceanic spreading ridges, continental rifts, and large igneous provinces at 50‑km resolution. Comparison with aeromagnetic surveys shows agreement within 5 nT for wavelengths >100 km, validating the satellite‑only approach for regions lacking airborne coverage.

Ionospheric and magnetospheric field separation uses the dual‑frequency GPS receivers to estimate total electron content (TEC) and the electric field instrument to measure plasma drift. These data feed into the “Swarm Ionospheric Model” (SWARM_IONO) that provides global maps of the equatorial electrojet and field‑aligned currents at 1‑hour cadence. The combined core, lithospheric, and ionospheric models are essential for navigation (e.g., updating the World Magnetic Model) and for studying core‑mantle coupling through the analysis of core‑surface flow inversions constrained by the high‑resolution SV.

Orthorhombic Anisotropy in Fractured Reservoirs: Effective Medium Theory and Seismic Characterization

When a rock contains two or more orthogonal fracture sets, the effective elastic symmetry becomes orthorhombic, characterized by nine independent stiffness components (C₁₁, C₂₂, C₃₃, C₄₄, C₅₅, C₆₆, C₁₂, C₁₃, C₂₃). In the weak‑anisotropy limit, the Thomsen‑style parameter set expands to nine dimensionless parameters: ε(1), ε(2), δ(1), δ(2), δ(3), γ(1), γ(2), γ(3), and a cross‑parameter η(12). The superscripts refer to the symmetry planes (1‑2, 2‑3, 1‑3). For a vertical well intersecting a horizontal layer with two vertical fracture sets striking N‑S and E‑W, the symmetry planes are vertical, and the orthorhombic parameters reduce to ε(1) (N‑S), ε(2) (E‑W), δ(1), δ(2), γ(1), γ(2), with δ(3) and γ(3) governing the horizontal plane.

Effective medium theories (Hudson’s penny‑shaped crack model, Schoenberg’s linear‑slip theory) relate these parameters to fracture density (εf), aspect ratio (α), and fluid compliance. For a single fracture set with normal along x₁, Hudson gives ε(1) ≈ (16/3) (1−ν²) εf / (1−ν), where ν is the background Poisson’s ratio. When two orthogonal sets are present, the compliances add, and the orthorhombic parameters become sums of the individual set contributions. Laboratory measurements on synthetic fractured rocks confirm that ε(1) and ε(2) scale linearly with the respective fracture densities, while δ(1) and δ(2) are sensitive to fluid saturation (water vs. gas) because the normal compliance of a fluid‑filled crack depends on the fluid bulk modulus.

Seismic characterization of orthorhombic anisotropy requires multi‑azimuth, multi‑offset data. Azimuthal variation of P‑wave NMO velocity follows Vnmo²(φ) = VP0²[1 + 2ε(1) cos²φ + 2ε(2) sin²φ], allowing separation of ε(1) and ε(2). Shear‑wave splitting of converted‑wave (PS) data yields the fast‑polarisation azimuth (aligned with the dominant fracture strike) and the time delay Δt, which constrains γ(1) and γ(2). Amplitude‑versus‑offset‑azimuth (AVOA) analysis of PP reflections provides δ(1) and δ(2) through the azimuthal dependence of the reflection coefficient gradient. In the Barnett Shale, a 3‑D wide‑azimuth survey recovered ε(1) = 0.12, ε(2) = 0.04, δ(1) = −0.03, δ(2) = −0.01, γ(1) = 0.08, γ(2) = 0.02, consistent with a dominant N‑S fracture set and a weaker E‑W set. These parameters feed discrete‑fracture‑network (DFN) models that predict permeability anisotropy ratios (kmax/kmin) of 5–10, guiding horizontal‑well placement and hydraulic‑fracture design.

  • Assuming TTI anisotropy can be approximated by VTI when the tilt angle exceeds 10°; this introduces systematic depth errors of several hundred metres in subsalt imaging.
  • Treating normal‑mode splitting as solely due to CMB topography while ignoring mantle heterogeneity and inner‑core anisotropy; joint inversion is required for a unique solution.
  • Using DAS strain data without correcting for gauge‑length averaging and cable‑ground coupling; uncorrected data over‑estimate high‑frequency content and mislocate microseismic events.
  • Interpreting Swarm secular variation maps without removing the ionospheric and magnetospheric contributions; residual external fields can mimic core‑field jerks.
  • Equating orthorhombic ε(1) and ε(2) to fracture density without accounting for fluid saturation effects on δ parameters; gas‑filled fractures produce anomalously low δ values that bias permeability estimates.
  • Neglecting the directional sensitivity of DAS (axial strain only) when designing a monitoring array for shear‑wave dominated sources; a single straight fiber cannot resolve the full wavefield.
  • Assuming that the Swarm constellation’s side‑by‑side pair provides full vector gradient information at all latitudes; the gradient is only well‑resolved near the equator where the orbital planes are nearly parallel.
